, by the definition of the substitution (the coefficients
c_{i}
are the products, in white vertex groups of the substitution coefficients
c_{e_{i}}
of
e_{i}
and
c_{\bar{e}_{i+1}}^{-1}
of
\bar{e}_{i+1}
; one could work with
2
-sided substitutions too, here). In other words,
\alpha(g^{\prime})=g^{\prime e_{1}\dots e_{r}\eta}
.

Therefore, if
t
is the element of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
that induces
\alpha
by conjugation on
G
,
(g^{\prime})^{t}=g^{\prime e_{1}\dots e_{r}\eta}
, and the element
t_{v}=t\eta^{-1}\bar{e}_{r}\dots\bar{e}_{1}
induces the identity on
{\rm Stab}_{G}(v)
by conjugation. This proves the two first points of the definition of piecewise trivial suspension (and that black vertex stabilizers are equal to their adjacent edges stabilizers).

If (as it is the case with the assumption on acylindricity)
{\rm Stab}_{G}(v)
fixes only one white vertex, then
t_{v}
has to fix it too.

Moreover, if the substitution is full, consider a triple of adjacent edges
e_{r+1},e_{r+2},e_{r+3}
, forming a reduced path from a white vertex
v
to a black vertex
v^{\prime}
. We need to check that no non-trivial power of
t_{v}
fixes
v^{\prime}
. If one fixes
v^{\prime}
, it must normalize its stabilizer: the image (from the Bass group) of

e_{1}\dots e_{r}e_{r+1}e_{r+2}\mathbb{X}_{\mu}\bar{e}_{r+2}\bar{e}_{r+2}\bar{e}_{r}\dots\bar{e}_{1}.

Let
g
be non-trivial in
\mathbb{X}_{\mu}
and

g^{\prime}=e_{1}\dots e_{r}e_{r+1}e_{r+2}e_{r+3}\,\cdot g\cdot\,\bar{e}_{r+3}\bar{e}_{r+2}\bar{e}_{r+1}\bar{e}_{r}\dots\bar{e}_{1}\in{\rm Stab}(v^{\prime}).

After applying the substitution, by our hypothesis that edge groups in
\mathbb{X}
map surjectively onto cylinders groups, the relations in the Bass group allow us to completely migrate (by relations of the Bass group) the substitution coefficients across edges:

e_{r+1}e_{r+2}\mapsto c_{e_{r+1}}^{-1}e_{r+1}e_{r+2}c_{e_{r+2}}=e_{r+1}e_{r+2}c^{\prime}.

Since the substitution is full, we have that
1\neq c^{\prime}\in\tau_{e_{r+2}}(\mathbb{X}_{e_{r+2}}).

Conjugating
g^{\prime}
by
t_{v}=t\eta^{-1}\bar{e}_{r}\dots\bar{e}_{1}
, one obtains

(g^{\prime})^{t_{v}}=g^{(\bar{e}_{r+3}c(c^{\prime})^{-1}\bar{e}_{r+2}\bar{e}_{r+1}\bar{e}_{r}\bar{e}_{r-1}\dots\bar{e}_{1})}

with
c\in\tau_{\bar{e}_{r+3}}(\mathbb{X}_{e_{r+3}})
and
(c^{\prime})^{-1}
is nontrivial as above. It follows that the element
c(c^{\prime})^{-1}
cannot be conjugated into the image
\tau_{\bar{e}_{r+3}}(\mathbb{X}_{e_{r+3}})
, so from the reduced form it is immediate that
(g^{\prime})^{t_{v}}
is not in the stabilizer of
v^{\prime}
.

∎

It is worth noting that if we drop the requirement, in the fiber, that the images of edge groups of
\mathbb{X}
are surjective onto black vertex groups, then the action of
G\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
on the tree
T
will no longer be acylindrical.Theorem
2.7
now immediately implies the following.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
F
be a free group,
\alpha
be a linearly growing unipotent automorphism, and
\mathcal{E}
the class of
\alpha
-non-growing subgroups of
F
. If
T
is the tree of cylinders for abelian splittings of
F
relative to
\mathcal{E}
then
\Gamma=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
is a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension over
T
, as a
\Gamma
-tree.

3.2
Canonical structural trees of piecewise trivial suspensions

In this subsection, we will prove the following, and then discuss consequences.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group, with two
\mathcal{A}
-bipartite
\Gamma
-trees
T,T^{\prime}
each such that
\Gamma
is a piecewise trivial suspension over
T
and over
T^{\prime}
. Then, there is an isomorphism of
\Gamma
-trees
f:T\to T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let us define an equivariant bijection between the white vertices of
T
and the white vertices of
T^{\prime}
.

Consider a white vertex
v
of
T
, and
\Gamma_{v}
its stabilizer in
\Gamma
. We claim that
\Gamma_{v}
is elliptic on
T
. Would it be otherwise, it would have a hyperbolic element
g_{v}
, with an axis in
T
. However, every element of
\Gamma_{v}
has a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in its centralizer (if
g_{v}\notin\langle t_{v}\rangle
, it contains
\langle g_{v},t_{v}\rangle
, and if
g_{v}\in\langle t_{v}\rangle
, any
h\in G_{v}<\Gamma_{v}
commutes with
g_{v}
). Therefore the axis of
g_{v}
in
T^{\prime}
is pointwise fixed by an infinite cyclic group, and this contradicts the acylindricity of the action of
\Gamma
on
T^{\prime}
(Proposition
3.2
). We have established that
\Gamma_{v}
is elliptic. But
\Gamma_{v}
cannot fix any black vertices of
T^{\prime}
, because the local direct factor
G_{v}
is assumed to be non-abelian. Therefore it fixes a unique white vertex. This produces a well defined equivariant map from the set of white vertices of
T
to the set of white vertices of
T^{\prime}
.

By symmetry of the assumptions, we have an equivariant map from the white vertices of
T^{\prime}
to those of
T
. The compositions are the identities on
T
and
T^{\prime}
: if
v
is a white vertex in
T
, the image of
v
is fixed by
\Gamma_{v}
, so
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
contains
\Gamma_{v}
, and then the image of
v^{\prime}
is fixed by
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
, so in particular by
\Gamma_{v}
, and it is white, so it is
v
.

Now that we have an equivariant bijection between white vertices of
T
and
T^{\prime}
, we extend it to the whole tree.

Consider two white vertices in
T
that are separated only by a black vertex. They are sent on different white vertices of
T^{\prime}
(by injectivity) and the intersection of their stabilizers, which is abelian non-cyclic, by the first point of Proposition
3.2
, (as a subgroup fixing a black vertex in
T
) fixes the segment between their images. If this segment contains a white vertex in its interior, by the last point of Proposition
3.2
, the fixator of the segment is cyclic or trivial. This is a contradiction with non-cyclicity of the intersection.

∎

If
\Gamma
is a piecewise trivial suspension, Proposition
3.5
allows us to talk about the unique (up to isomorphism) tree
T
over which it is a piecewise trivial suspension. We call it its
canonical structural tree
. All automorphisms of
\Gamma
must therefore give rise to equivariant automorphisms of
T
:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a piecewise trivial suspension and let
T
be a canonical structural tree. Let
\mathbb{X}
be the graph-of-group dual to
T
. Then the natural map
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
is surjective.

Another consequence of the canonicity of structural trees is the following computational result which is important for calculations later in the paper.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated free group,
\alpha
a linearly growing unipotent automorphism, and let
\Gamma={{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
as in Proposition
3.4
then it is possible algorithmically to construct a splitting
\mathbb{X}
of
\Gamma
that is dual to the structural tree
T
for
\alpha
directly from a finite presentation of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
{\left\langle X|R\right\rangle}
be a presentation for
\Gamma
, and enumerate the presentations for
\Gamma
until there is a presentation of
\Gamma
as a graph of groups where:

1.

The vertex groups are either explicitly isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or explicitly direct products of non-abelian free groups and cyclic groups.

2.

The underlying graph is bipartite with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
black vertices.

3.

The images of edge groups are maximal abelian subgroups of the vertex groups.

4.

In the white (non-abelian) vertex group, distinct edges groups map to non-conjugate subgroups.

5.

The centralizer of a white vertex group is not equal to the centralizer of another white vertex group at distance 2.

Since such a splitting
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
exists, an enumeration of all finite presentations of
\Gamma
will eventually produce such a splitting. Furthermore, all such conditions are easily verifiable in the vertex groups and from the Bass-Serre relations.

The conditions above immediately imply the two first properties of the definition of piecewise trivial suspensions. Let us now check the third property.
Consider, for each white vertex
w
in
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, a generator
t_{w}
of the given cyclic direct factor. We need to check that if
e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}
is a reduced path in the Bass Serre tree of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
starting at the vertex fixed by
\Gamma_{w}
, then
t_{w}
does not fix
e_{3}
. Let
b
be the black vertex adjacent to
e_{1}
, by the third condition we verified on
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, it follows that the stabilizer of
e_{1}
and
b
coincide and similarly that it coincides with the stabilizer of
e_{2}
. But, by the fifth condition
t_{w}
is not central in
\Gamma_{w^{\prime}}
, for
w^{\prime}
the vertex between
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
. By properties of maximal abelian subgroups of direct products of a free group and a cyclic group and the fourth condition,
t_{w}
fixes only one edge adjacent to
w^{\prime}
, and it must be
e_{2}
. Therefore it does not fix
e_{3}
, and we are done.

Proposition
3.5
then implies that
T^{\prime}
the Bass-Serre tree dual to
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the canonical tree
T
.
∎

3.3
Subgroups of globally fibered piecewise trivial suspensions

In this subsection we study finitely generated subgroups of a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension
\Gamma
. We will denote by
T
the canonical structural tree of
\Gamma
, and
G
the global fiber: the local fibres are the intersections of
G
with the vertex stabilizers. We denote by
t
an element such that

\Gamma=G\rtimes{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
. Our main example is when
G
is a free group.

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is as above, and that the local fibres, i.e. the intersection of
G
with the vertex stabilizers, are free. Let
H<\Gamma
be a finitely generated freely indecomposable subgroup of
\Gamma
, with minimal subtree
T_{0}\subset T
, that is infinite.

If for all edges
e
of
T_{0}
, the index of
H\cap\Gamma_{e}
in
\Gamma_{e}
is finite, then
T_{0}
is a
\mathcal{A}
-bipartite
H
-tree,
and
H
is piecewise trivial suspension over
T_{0}
.

Proof.

Consider a white vertex
v
in
T_{0}
. Its stabilizer
\Gamma_{v}
in
\Gamma
is a direct product
G_{v}\oplus{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
. Let
H_{v}
denote
H\cap\Gamma_{v}
. We first show that
H_{v}
is a direct product of a non-abelian free group and a cyclic group.

Consider the quotient map
\Gamma_{v}\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma_{v}/{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
. In restriction to
H_{v}
, it is non-injective, because it is so on the adjacent edge stabilizers. Therefore
H_{v}
intersects

{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
, necessarily in an infinite cyclic group.

The quotient by
H_{v}\cap{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
maps
H_{v}
into
\Gamma_{v}/{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
, which is isomorphic to
G_{v}
. Hence the image of
H_{v}
by this quotient is free, and therefore
H_{v}
splits as a semi-direct product
H_{v}=({\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}\cap H)\rtimes F_{v}
, for
F_{v}
any lift of the free group
H_{v}/(H\cap{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}))
in
H_{v}
.
Since the left factor is central, this is a direct product. Observe however that we cannot guarantee that the other factor is contained in
G_{v}
.

Since
H_{v}
intersects different (at least
2
) edge stabilizers adjacent to
v
as finite index subgroups, it is non-abelian (recall that, by Proposition
3.2
, two distinct incident edge groups intersect in a cyclic subgroup that itself trivially intersects the local fibre of
v
, thus their union generate a non-abelian group).

For each edge of
T_{0}
, its stabilizer in
H
is of finite index in its stabilizer in
\Gamma
, by assumption, so it is free abelian of rank
2
. This holds too for stabilizers of black vertices of
T_{0}
in
H
. It remains to show the third point of Definition
3.1
for
H
on
T_{0}
.

If
v
is a white vertex of
T_{0}
, we know that all non-trivial powers of
t_{v}
fix exactly the ball of radius
2
around
v
, and we know also that the centre of
H_{v}
is generated by a power of
t_{v}
. Therefore we have the third point of the definition.
∎

We will say that a subgroup
K\leq H
is
thick
if contains a subgroup isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. If a subgroup
K
is not thick, we will say it is
thin
.

Recall from
[
RS97
,
FP06
,
DS99
,
GL17
]
that a group
R
is
rigid
relative to a collection
\mathcal{H}
of subgroups if
R
admits no abelian splittings relative to
\mathcal{H}
. A vertex groups
\mathbb{X}_{q}
of a graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
with cyclic edge groups is said to be
quadratically hanging (QH) in
\mathbb{X}
it is the fundamental group of a compact surface
\Sigma
with boundary and the incident edge groups coincide with the
\pi_{1}
-images of the connected components of the boundary
\partial\Sigma
. We say a QH subgroup is
maximal QH
if it is not conjugate into a proper subgroup of another QH group.

The next proposition analyses a freely indecomposable finitely generated subgroup of a piecewise trivial suspension, with a free global fibre. In order to do so we will need to consider a new JSJ theory. Because many properties need to be verified, the proof below is long. We refer to reader to Section
2.2
for the terminology that will be used in the proof.

Proposition 3.9
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension over a tree
T
, with a global fibre
G
that is free.
Let
H<\Gamma
be a finitely generated freely indecomposable subgroup of
\Gamma
, with minimal subtree
T_{H}\subset T
, that is infinite. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the class of cyclic subgroups of
H
, and
\mathcal{H}
be the collection of subgroups of
H
that are piecewise trivial suspension. Then there exists
\mathcal{T}
a collapsed tree of cylinders of an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-JSJ tree for the relation of commensurability.

The action of
H
on
\mathcal{T}
is dual to a bipartite graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
with cyclic edge groups edge groups. Black vertex groups are maximal cyclic and white vertex groups
\mathbb{X}_{w}
either:

(a)

isomorphic to piecewise trivial suspensions,

(b)

free groups that are either do not admit any cyclic splitting relative to incident edge groups or that are maximal QH in
\mathbb{X}
.

Furthermore the following hold:

1.

For any edge of
\mathcal{T}
adjacent to a vertex
w
of with edge group
\mathbb{X}_{w}
of type (
a
), the edge group
\mathbb{X}_{e}
is coincides with the centralizer of a subgroup isomorphic to
F\oplus\mathbb{Z}
that is a vertex group of the canonical splitting of
\mathbb{X}_{w}
as a piecewise trivial suspension.

2.

Any other splitting of
H
with the same

Finally
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
maps surjectively onto
{\mathrm{Out}\left(H\right)}
.

Proof.

Let
T_{H}
be the minimal
H
-subtree for the action of
H
on
T
. Non-abelian thin vertex groups of
T_{H}
are subgroups of white vertex groups of
T
, so these must be subgroups that intersect the centre of the white vertex group trivially and therefore are themselves free groups. Since
H
is one-ended all edge groups of
T_{H}
must be isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

We call a maximal subtree of
T_{H}
consisting of edges with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
edge stabilizers a
thick component
. By Proposition
3.8
the stabilizers of thick components are themselves piecewise trivial suspensions, though it is possible that they do not admit a global fibre.

We colour the vertices of
T_{H}
the same as the vertices of
T
and we say a vertex
v
is
thick,thin
if the vertex group
H_{v}
is thick, thin respectively. Similarly we will say a vertex is
black,white
if the vertex it stabilizes is black, white respectively. We first investigate cyclic edge groups of
T_{H}
.

It is clear that any edge of
T_{H}
with cyclic stabilizer will not be adjacent to a thick black vertex. This next fact is a bit more subtle.

Claim:

If an edge
e
of
T_{H}
with a cyclic stabilizer is adjacent to a thick subgroup
v
then
v
must be white and the subgroup
H_{e}\leq H_{v}
must lie in the centre of
H_{v}
.
Consider the overgroup
\Gamma_{v}\geq H_{v}
. We have
\Gamma_{v}=G_{v}\oplus\langle t_{v}\rangle
and
H_{v}\simeq K_{v}\oplus\langle t_{v}^{n_{v}}\rangle
for some subgroup
K_{v}\leq G_{v}
and where the subgroup
\langle t_{v}^{n_{v}}\rangle
is the centre of
H_{v}
. If the image of
H_{e}
in
H_{v}
was not central, then
\langle H_{e},t_{v}^{n_{v}}\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is a subgroup of
H
that stabilizes the edge
e
of
T
, contradicting
H_{e}\simeq\mathbb{Z}
. This proves the claim.

The argument above also shows that white vertex groups of
T_{H}
are non-abelian, for otherwise such a vertex group would be completely contained in an edge group. Since distinct edges incident to a white vertex group have distinct edge groups, this would force the vertex to have valence 1 which contradicts the minimality of
T_{H}
.

We define a
thin component
of
T_{H}
to be a maximal subtree whose vertices are thin. The acylindricity of the action of
\Gamma
on
T
and the claim above imply that a thin black vertex is adjacent to at most one thick white vertex and therefore that non-empty thin components are non-abelian. We say an edge is
interstitial
if it joins a thick and a thin vertex. Interstitial edges are precisely the edges that are neither in a thick or a thin component and their stabilizers coincide with those of the edges considered in item
1
in the statement of the proposition.

We will now modify
T_{H}
in order to get a canonical
H
-tree. However, doing so requires some care. We want
\mathcal{A}
, our class of edge groups, to be either the class of cyclic or abelian subgroups, but since
T_{H}
has a mix of cyclic and abelian edge groups, and since
H
does not satisfy commutation transitivity, there is no possible equivalence relation on
\mathcal{A}
that is admissible described in Section
2.2
. Our solution is to let
\mathcal{H}
, our peripheral structure, be the set of subgroups of
H
that are isomorphic to piecewise trivial suspensions, to let our edge groups lie in
\mathcal{A}
the set of infinite cyclic groups, and to let
\sim
be the commensurability relation. For
H
, given our knowledge of piecewise trivial suspension, it is easy to verify
\sim
is an admissible equivalence relation on
\mathcal{A}
. Our first task is to show that there indeed exists a canonical
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree.

Claim:
With the equivalence relation
\sim
and the classes of subgroups
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{H}
given above,
H
admits a canonical
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
\mathcal{T}
that is constructed as the tree of cylinders of an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-JSJ tree.
This amounts to showing that the hypotheses of
[
GL17
, Corollary 9.1]
are satisfied. In our case, it will be sufficient to show two things. Firstly, that for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
the stabilizer (under conjugation)
H_{[A]}
of the
\sim
-equivalence class
[A]
is
small in any
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
, which means that
H_{[A]}
will always fix a vertex or a point in the boundary of an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree. Secondly, that if
A\leq A^{\prime},[A^{\prime}:A]=2
and
A\in\mathcal{A}
, then
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Let
S
be an arbitrary
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree and let
A\in\mathcal{A}
. The group
H_{[A]}\leq H
that fixes the equivalence class
[A]
via conjugation is then the normalizer of the maximal cyclic subgroup containing
A
. If
H_{[A]}
is not cyclic then
H_{[A]}
must be contained in a subgroup isomorphic to a piecewise trivial suspension and therefore must be contained in a subgroup in
\mathcal{H}
, so
H_{[A]}
fixes a vertex of
S
. If
H_{[A]}
is cyclic, by the classification of isometries of simplicial trees, either it fixes a vertex or leaves a line of
S
invariant. In all cases the action of
H_{[A]}
is
small
. We now move on the the second thing we need to show.

Next, note that for any cyclic group in
A\leq H
, we have that if
[A^{\prime}:A]\leq 2
then
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
. Indeed suppose this was not the case. We pass to the overgroup
\Gamma\geq H
.
A
is elliptic in the
\Gamma
tree
T
if and only if
A^{\prime}
is elliptic. By the structure of trivial suspensions, if
A
is elliptic in
T
then
A^{\prime}
must also be cyclic,. If
A
is hyperbolic in
T
then
A^{\prime}
must also fix the axis of
A
. We have
A^{\prime}={\left\langle A,g\right\rangle}
for some element
g\in\Gamma
. If
A^{\prime}
is not itself cyclic then, by the classification of discrete isometries of a line,
g
must fix a point and
g^{2}
must fix the entire axis. This contradicts acylindricity since
g^{2}\neq 1.

Finally, since any equivalence class stabilizer
H_{[A]},A\in\mathcal{A}
, is either cyclic or lies inside a trivial suspension, we have that every group in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{nvc}}
, the set of equivalence class stabilizers that are not virtually cyclic, is contained in a group in
\mathcal{H}
. The claim now immediately follows from
[
GL17
, Corollary 9.1]
.

Now that we have proven the existence of a canonical
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
tree in our novel JSJ theory, it remains to describe it. We will do so by first constructing a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-JSJ tree (recall Section
2.2
for the precise definition) from
T_{H}
.

Consider the tree
\dot{T}_{H}
obtained by
H
-equivariantly collapsing every thick and every thin component to a point. In particular there is an
H
-equivariant map
T_{H}\twoheadrightarrow\dot{T}_{H}
and the only edges that are not collapsed to points are the interstitial edges. Now
T_{H}
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree and it has vertices that are either piecewise trivial suspensions that we call
\mathcal{H}
-vertices
, or acylindrical graphs of free groups with cyclic edge groups, that we call
thin type
. Let
w
be a thin-type vertex of
\dot{T}_{H}
and denote by
\mathcal{E}_{w}
the images of the incident edge groups. On the one hand, since
H
is one ended,
H_{w}
is also one ended relative to
\mathcal{E}_{w}
. On the other hand
H_{w}
, being the fundamental group of a graph of free groups without any Baumslag-Solitar subgroups is torsion-free hyperbolic by
[
BF92
]
, so

[
GL17
, Theorem 9.5]
and immediately tells us that that
H_{w}
admits a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}_{w})
-JSJ tree
T_{w}
, that the collapsed tree of cylinders coincides with the tree of cylinders
(T_{w})_{c}
and that
T_{w}
is in the same deformation space as
(T_{w})_{c}
. Let
\widehat{T}_{H}
be the tree obtained by blowing up every thin type vertex
w
of
\dot{T}_{H}
to the tree
T_{w}
as follows:
H
-equivariantly delete
w
from
\dot{T}_{H}
. Now every edge
e
incident to
w
in
\dot{T}_{H}
has a stabilizer
H_{e}
that fixes a vertex
w_{e}
of
T_{w}
, so we
H
-equivariantly attach
e
to
w_{e}
.

The next three claims are verifications of facts that are unsurprising but still need to be shown since we are dealing with a novel JSJ theory.

Claim:

Every edge group in
\widehat{T}_{H}
is
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-universally elliptic.
Indeed, first note that every interstitial edge has stabilizer lying in a subgroup of
\mathcal{H}
. Let
e
be some non-interstitial edge. We need to show that
H_{e}
acts elliptically on an arbitrary
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
S
. By construction
e
must lie in some subtree
T_{w}
where
w\in{{\textbf{V}(\dot{T}_{H})}}
is a thin-type vertex. There is an induced action of
H_{w}
on
S
with abelian edge stabilizers and where the subgroups in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
are elliptic, this
S
, viewed as an
H_{w}
-tree, is a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}_{w})
-tree. Since
H_{e}\leq H_{w}
is an edge group of
T_{w}
and
T_{w}
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}_{w})
-JSJ tree,
H_{e}
must act elliptically on
S
. This proves the claim.

Claim:

\widehat{T}_{H}
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-JSJ tree
. We’ve already shown that
\widehat{T}_{H}
is a universally elliptic
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree. Now we must show it dominates every other universally elliptic
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree. Suppose towards a contradiction that this was not the case, then there would exist some universally elliptic
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree
S
with a single edge orbit that is not dominated by
\widehat{T}_{H}
. This implies that some vertex group
H_{u}
of
\widehat{T}
must acts hyperbolically on
S
. This vertex group cannot by thick-type so by universal ellipticity of
\widehat{T}_{H}
,
H_{u}
must be flexible-type vertex group of some subtree
T_{w}\subset\widehat{T}_{H}
where
w
is a thin-type vertex of
\widehat{T}
. By
[
GL17
, Theorem 9.14]

H_{u}
must be a QH subgroup in
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}
, the incident edge groups of
H_{u}
must be elliptic. Thus, up to conjugacy, any edge group in
S
is commensurable to the
\pi_{1}
-image of some simple closed curve
\gamma
in the underlying surface
\Sigma_{u}
with boundary. In particular, by taking another simple closed curve
\delta
that essentially intersects
\gamma
, it is possible to produce a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-splitting of
H
in which this edge stabilizer of
S
is hyperbolic, which is a contradiction. The claim now follows.

It now follows from
[
GL17
, Corollary 9.1]
that
\cal T
and
\widehat{T}_{H}
have the same vertex stabilizers and by
[
GL17
, Theorem 9.14]
that the vertex stabilizers are as in the statement of the proposition since the stabilizers of thin-type vertices of
\dot{T}_{H}
are torsion-free hyperbolic.
\cal T
can now be obtained from
\widehat{T}_{H}
as a tree of cylinders. There is a natural mapping from
{{\textbf{V}(T_{H})}}
to
{{\textbf{V}(\mathcal{T})}}
, that is injective on the set of vertices with non-abelian stabilizer, but that may identify cyclic edge groups that lie in the same cylinder. We note that this may “merge” subtrees coming from distinct thin components of
T_{H}
.

Claim:

Suppose that an
H
-tree
S
is yet another
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
-tree whose non-abelian vertices are as described in (
a
) and (
b
) in the statement of the proposition, then
S
is in the same deformation space as
\mathcal{T}
.
In this case, consider a connected component
S^{\prime}
of
S\setminus IE
where
IE
is the set of edges adjacent to vertex groups that lie in
\mathcal{H}
. Denote by
H_{S^{\prime}}
the stabilizer of the subtree
S^{\prime}
. As an
H_{S^{\prime}}
-tree,
S^{\prime}
is an
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
-tree, where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is the set of subgroups that lie in the images of edges groups coming form edges in
IE
, or equivalently, are adjacent to vertex groups in
\mathcal{H}
. As for
H_{w}
above,
H_{S^{\prime}}
must be one ended relative to
\mathcal{E}_{S^{\prime}}
and
H_{S^{\prime}}
must be torsion-free hyperbolic. Since the vertex groups of
H^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}}
in this
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
splitting are either rigid or maximal QH, we have that, by
[
DT19
, Theorem 3.22]
,
S^{\prime}
must be a
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
-JSJ tree for
H_{S^{\prime}}
. This claim now follows from the two previous claims, but where
S
is used instead of
\widehat{T}_{H}
. This works because the subtree
S^{\prime}
of
S
and the subtree
T_{w}
of
\widehat{T}_{H}
above share the same essential properties.

Finally
[
GL17
, Corollary 9.1]
states that
\cal T
is invariant under automorphisms of
H
that fix
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{H}
, since all automorphisms of
H
preserve these two classes we have a surjection
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}_{c}\right)}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathrm{Out}\left(H\right)}
, where
\mathbb{X}
is the graph of groups splitting dual to
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

The previous proposition proves that there exists a suitable decomposition for our subgroup
H
of
\Gamma
. The next statement covers the computability of this decomposition and also gives a refinement of this decomposition that we will use in Section
6
. The corollary below has some extra hypotheses (solution to word problem, effective coherence) that will be proven in Section
4
.

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
G
be a finitely generated free group.
Suppose that
\Gamma=G\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
is a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension over
T
with fiber
G
, where
G
has decidable word problem and
\Gamma
is effectively coherent.

Then there is an algorithm that, given
H\leq\Gamma
be a finitely generated one-ended subgroup, constructs the splitting
\mathbb{X}
of
H
dual to the tree
\mathcal{T}
given in Proposition
3.9
. Furthermore, the maximal thick vertex groups can be algorithmically refined to give a graph of groups
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}
whose vertex groups are either trivial suspension, i.e. isomorphic direct products of subgroups of
G
with
\mathbb{Z}
, or to finitely generated subgroups of
G
. The edge groups are either isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
or
\mathbb{Z}
, and if a
\mathbb{Z}
-edge group maps into a trivial suspension, then its image is in the centre of that suspension. Furthermore, we still have a surjection
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\widehat{\mathbb{X}}\right)}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathrm{Out}\left(H\right)}
.

Proof.

The idea is along the same lines as the proof of Proposition
3.7
. Given a generating set
S\subset\Gamma
for
H
, we can use the effective coherence of
\Gamma
to construct a presentation
{\left\langle X|R\right\rangle}
for
H
. Next, we enumerate presentations of
H
. By Proposition
3.9
one of these presentations of
H
is explicitly that of a graph of groups isomorphic to
\mathbb{X}
. For the algorithm constructing
\mathbb{X}
to terminate we need to recognize the following conditions:

1.

The underlying graph should be bipartite, black vertices are maximal cyclic in
H
.
Verifying that the underlying graph is bipartite is obvious, verifying that vertex groups are cyclic follows from effective coherence and a solution to the word problem. Verifying that a vertex group is maximal cyclic in
H
can be done by computing centralizers in vertex groups.

2.

White vertex groups are as given in Proposition
3.9
.
This falls into two subcases:

(a)

Piecewise trivial suspensions.
This can be verified by finding a presentation of the vertex groups of a bipartite graph of groups whose red vertex groups are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and whose green vertices are direct products of subgroups of
G
with
\mathbb{Z}
, which again can be seen from the right kind of presentation.

(b)

Free.
Because we can find presentations of the vertex groups from the presentation of
H
, because the word problem is decidable in these vertex groups, and because maximal abelian subgroups are malnormal in these vertex groups, we can apply
[
Tou18
, Theorem B]
to certify whether a thin vertex group is rigid relative to incident edge groups. We can verify that a subgroup is QH by finding a presentation of that subgroup that is a standard presentation of the fundamental group of a with boundary, that the incident edge groups are conjugate to the boundary subgroups. We can verify that such groups are maximal QH by ensuring that there are no black vertices of valence 2 that are adjacent to two QH groups such that collapsing the edges gives a larger QH group.

3.

If two edges
e,e^{\prime}
terminate in a vertex
w
then the images
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
in
\mathbb{X}_{w}
intersects all conjugates of the image of
\tau_{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X}{e^{\prime}})
trivially.
This again can be verified by computing centralizers in vertex groups and by using hereditary algorithmic tractability. In particular, we need only solve the conjugacy problem either in subgroups of
G
or in subgroups of
G\times\mathbb{Z}
. The latter reduces to the conjugacy problem in subgroups of
G
.

Proposition
3.9
already implies that if
\mathbb{X}
satisfies
2
then
\mathbb{X}
is dual to a JSJ tree. Conditions
1
and
3
imply that the tree dual to
\mathbb{X}
is equal to its tree of cylinders. It follows that there is an algorithm that will terminate and output the splitting
\mathbb{X}
dual to
\mathcal{T}
.

Finally a canonical graph of groups
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}
as in the statement of the Proposition can be constructed as follows. For every vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
whose vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}
is a piecewise trivial suspension, blow up
v
to the canonical splitting
\mathbb{Y}^{v}
that can be computed by Proposition
3.7
. We must now reattach the the edges originally incident to
v
to vertices of
Y^{v}
. There is a subtlety here: such an incident edge group will actually lie in multiple vertex groups of
\mathbb{Y}^{v}
. By Proposition
3.9
item
1
, the edge group associated to every such edge must coincide with the the centre of exactly one of the non-abelian vertex groups of
\mathbb{Y}^{v}
. This thus gives us a canonical choice of which vertex group of
\mathbb{Y}^{v}
we want to send the edge group.

It is clear from Propositions
3.9
and
3.6
that
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}
is still
{\mathrm{Out}\left(H\right)}
-invariant, so the last claim follows.
∎

We will call such a decomposition,
canonical trivially partially suspended
.

3.4
Fibre and orientation preserving automorphisms of suspensions

We are interested in the fibre and orientation-preserving automorphisms of suspensions. We begin by drawing a connection between these automorphism groups and centralizers of outer automorphisms.

3.4.1
Centralizers and
{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}

Let
F
be a group, and
\alpha
an automorphism. The subgroup
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
of
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
is the subgroup of all automorphisms that preserve the fibre and the orientation of the semi-direct product, i.e. those that preserve
F
and send
t
in
tF
. The subscript
\mathrm{fo}
stands for fibre and orientation.

All inner automorphisms are in
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
. The quotient by this normal subgroup of inner automorphisms is the group
{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
.

As a general observation, one can note that the image of
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
in
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
by the restriction map composed by the quotient map
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
, is the centralizer of
[\alpha]
in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
. Also, the image of
{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left(F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\right)}
in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
by the restriction map is
Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}}([\alpha])/{\left\langle[\alpha]\right\rangle}
, for
Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}}([\alpha])
the centralizer in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
of the outer class of
\alpha
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}=F\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle t\rangle
. Then

{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\simeq Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}}([\alpha])/\langle[\alpha]\rangle
.

Proof.

There is a natural homomorphism
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
, by restriction, and hence by composition, one has the homomorphism
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
.

If
\phi
is in
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}
, one has
\phi(t)=f_{0}t
for some
f_{0}\in F
. For
f\in F
, one has

\phi(f^{t})=\phi(\alpha(f))=\phi(f)^{\phi(t)}=\alpha(\phi(f)^{f_{0}})={\alpha(\phi(f))^{\alpha(f_{0})}}.

It follows that the image of
\phi
in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
commutes with
\alpha
, so one can restrict the target of the previous homomorphism, and obtain the homomorphism
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}}([\alpha])
. Conversely, any automorphism of
F
commuting with
\alpha
allows us to define an automorphism of the semidirect product, hence this later homomorphism is surjective.

Also by composition, one has the homomorphism

{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}}([\alpha])/\langle[\alpha]\rangle.

Observe that any inner automorphism of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
is in the kernel of this homomorphism (it is obvious for inner automorphism by conjugation by
t
, and by any
f\in F
, hence by any product
ft^{k}
).

Thus, there is a natural surjective homomorphism

{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}}([\alpha])/\langle[\alpha]\rangle.

Now consider an element
\Phi
in the kernel of this homomorphism. Realize
\Phi
as an automorphism
\phi
. In restriction to
F
, it is in the group generated by
\alpha
and the inner automorphisms of
F
. Therefore, its restriction to
F
is that of an inner automorphism of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
. Up to choosing
\phi
suitably in the class of
\Phi
, we may thus assume that
\phi|_{F}=Id_{F}
. But then again,
\phi(t)=tf_{0}
, and
\alpha(f)=f^{t}=\phi(f^{t})=\phi(f)^{\phi(t)}=\alpha(f)^{f_{0}}
. Thus,
f_{0}=1
, and
\phi
is the identity. Thus, the homomorphism
{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\to Z_{{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}}([\alpha])/\langle[\alpha]\rangle
is injective, hence an isomorphism.

∎

3.4.2
Fiber and orientation preservation in unipotent linearly growing suspensions

We will now specialize to the case of a suspension
\Gamma={{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
where
F
is free and
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
is a unipotent linear automorphism. The data
\Gamma=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
encodes a homomorphism
\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma/F\simeq\mathbb{Z}
as well as a distinguished generator
tF
of the
\mathbb{Z}
-quotient. We call the free normal subgroup
F
a
fibre
and the coset
tF
an
orientation
. Fibres and orientations are not unique to
\Gamma
and we may consider other fibrations
\Gamma=F^{\prime}\rtimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}{\left\langle t^{\prime}\right\rangle}
which correspond to different homomorphisms to
\mathbb{Z}
or different distinguished generators of the quotient.

Since
\Gamma
is a piecewise trivial suspension it admits a canonical graphs of groups decomposition
\mathbb{X}
, by Proposition
3.5
.
X
the graph underlying
\mathbb{X}
is bipartite and we colour the vertices as follows: a vertex
b
is
black
if the corresponding vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{b}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and a vertex
w
is
white
if
\mathbb{X}_{w}\simeq F_{w}\times\mathbb{Z}
, where
F_{w}
is a free group.

We note that the centre of every white vertex group is characteristic (invariant by automorphisms) and isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, if
\Gamma
is given an orientation
tF
, then for each
\mathbb{X}_{w}
we will pick an element
t_{w}\in tF\cap\mathbb{X}_{w}
that generates the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{w}
. We note that, although without fixing a basepoint or spanning tree of
\mathbb{X}
, vertex groups only define subgroups up to conjugacy, the choice of orientation of
\Gamma
still uniquely determines a distinguished central element of
\mathbb{X}_{w}
. If we are also explicit about the fibre
F\triangleleft\Gamma
then we can write
\mathbb{X}_{w}=F_{w}\times{\left\langle t_{w}\right\rangle}
where
F_{w}=\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F
. This is well-defined because
F
is a normal subgroup.

We start by examining how fibres coming from unipotent linear automorphisms intersect the black vertex groups.

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose that
\Gamma=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}=F^{\prime}\rtimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}{\left\langle t^{\prime}\right\rangle}
where both
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are unipotent and linear, and let
\mathbb{X}
be the canonical graph of groups decomposition of
\Gamma
as a piecewise trivial suspension. Then for every black vertex in
\mathbb{X}
one has

\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F=\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F^{\prime}.

In particular, black vertex groups have canonical fibres.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{X}_{b}
be a black vertex group and let
e,f
be two distinct edges incident to
b
. Let
w,u
be the white vertices adjacent to
e,f
respectively. For
v=u,w
we have
\mathbb{X}_{v}\simeq F_{v}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
where
F_{v}=\mathbb{X}_{v}\cap F
and
F_{w}^{\prime}=\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F^{\prime}
, and in particular that
F^{\prime}_{w}\simeq F_{w}
. Now although the fibres may not match up in a white vertex group, the couple
\{t_{w}^{\pm 1}\}
is canonical: it is the symmetric generating couple of the centre of the vertex group. Let
t_{w}
be the oriented central element corresponding to an orientation of
\Gamma
with fibre
F
and let
t^{\prime}_{w}=t_{w}^{\pm 1}
be the oriented central element corresponding to an orientation of
\Gamma
with fibre
F^{\prime}
.

The element
t_{w}
maps to a generator of either quotient
\mathbb{X}_{w}/(\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F)
or
\mathbb{X}_{w}/(\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F^{\prime})
. Moreover
t_{w}
lies in the image
i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
so we can pick the unique pre-image
t_{e}
such that
i_{e}(t_{e})=t_{w}
.
If we take the oriented central element
t_{u}\in\mathbb{X}_{u}
for the same orientation as we took for
\Gamma/F
and define
t_{f}\in\mathbb{X}_{b}
to be the pre-image such that
i_{f}(t_{f})=t_{u}
, then by looking at the quotient
\Gamma\to\Gamma/F
we have

t_{e}t_{f}^{-1}=c_{ef}\in\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F

and restricting the quotient to the abelian subgroup
\mathbb{X}_{b}
we see that
\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F
is the cyclic direct summand of
\mathbb{X}_{b}
containing
c_{ef}
. Furthermore we note that
c_{ef}\neq 1
, for otherwise we would have
t_{e}=t_{f}
which would imply that the element
t_{u}\in\mathbb{X}_{u}
also centralizes
\mathbb{X}_{w}
so that
t_{u}
pointwise fixes an infinite subtree of the Bass-Serre tree dual to
\mathbb{X}
, contradicting acylindricity guaranteed by Proposition
3.2
.

Now if we were to consider the orientation for
\Gamma/F^{\prime}
then we would have corresponding elements
t^{\prime}_{w},t^{\prime}_{e},t^{\prime}_{u},t^{\prime}_{f}
and we have
t^{\prime}_{w}=t_{w}^{\pm 1}

if and only if
t^{\prime}_{e}=t_{e}^{\pm 1},t^{\prime}_{u}=t_{u}^{\pm 1},t^{\prime}_{f}=t_{f}^{\pm 1}
. So that

t_{e}^{\prime}{t_{f}}^{-1}{}^{\prime}={c_{ef}}^{\pm 1}\in\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F^{\prime}.

It therefore follows that
\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F
and
\mathbb{X}_{b}\cap F^{\prime}
have a common non-trivial element and, since these subgroups are codimension 1 direct summands of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, they coincide.
∎

We, therefore, have the peculiar situation that although
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
could admit multiple unipotent linear fibrations, all fibres must intersect the black vertex groups the same way, yet unlike in the white vertex groups, there is no canonical pair of central elements. We now work towards matching up fibres in white vertex groups.

Lemma 3.13

(Fibre matching vertex group automorphism)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a trivial suspension of a free group with centralizer
{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
and let
F,F^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
be two free fibres, i.e.
F\simeq F^{\prime}
are free groups and

F^{\prime}\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\simeq{\left\langle F^{\prime},t\right\rangle}=\Gamma={\left\langle F,t\right\rangle}\simeq F\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}.

Then there is an automorphism
\eta_{F^{\prime},F}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\Gamma\right)}
such that
\eta_{F^{\prime},F}(F^{\prime})=F
and such that for all
g\in F\cap F^{\prime}
,
\eta_{F^{\prime},F}(g)=g
.

Proof.

Let
d:F\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\to\Gamma={\left\langle F,t\right\rangle}
be the isomorphism given by
(f,t^{n})\mapsto ft^{n}
and let
d^{\prime}:F^{\prime}\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\to\Gamma
be defined analogously. Let
\rho_{F}:\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow F
be the projection onto the fibre
F\leq\Gamma
given by
d\circ p_{F}\circ d^{-1}
where
p_{F}:F\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\to F\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
is the projection
(f,t^{n})\mapsto(f,1)
. Let
\kappa:F^{\prime}\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}\to F\oplus{\left\langle t\right\rangle}
be the isomorphism given by

(f^{\prime},t^{n})\mapsto(\rho_{F}|_{F^{\prime}}(f^{\prime}),t^{n}),

where
F^{\prime}
is viewed as
F^{\prime}\leq\Gamma
and
\rho_{F}|_{F^{\prime}}:F^{\prime}\to\Gamma
is the restriction of the projection onto
F
. Then the composition of isomorphisms
\eta_{F^{\prime},F}=d\circ\kappa\circ(d^{\prime})^{-1}
has the desired properties.
∎

Proposition 3.14
.

Suppose that
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}=F^{\prime}\rtimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}{\left\langle t^{\prime}\right\rangle}
where both
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
are unipotent and linear, and let
\mathbb{X}
be the canonical graph of groups decomposition of
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
as a piecewise trivial suspension. Then there is an automorphism of
\Psi\in\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\leq{\mathrm{Aut}\left({{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\right)}
that restricts to an isomorphism
\Psi|_{F}:F^{\prime}\to F
.

Proof.

We first note that
F
and
F^{\prime}
are both normal subgroups of
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
, therefore the notion of an intersection with a vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v},v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
is well-defined irrespective of the choice of basepoint in
\mathbb{X}
. By Lemma
3.12
, for any black vertex
b\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
,
F\cap\mathbb{X}_{b}=F^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{X}_{b}
.

From this it follows that in every white vertex
w
, for every edge
e
joining
w
to some black vertex
b
we have
F\cap i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{b})=F^{\prime}\cap i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{b})={\left\langle g_{e}\right\rangle}\leq\mathbb{X}_{w}
. Since the images of the edge group is generated as
i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{b})={\left\langle g_{e},t_{v}\right\rangle}
, the matching isomorphism
\phi_{w}=\eta_{\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F,\mathbb{X}_{w}\cap F^{\prime}}:\mathbb{X}_{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}_{w}
given Lemma
3.13
fixes a generating of
i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{b})
pointwise and thus restricts to the identity on
i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{b})
. Thus, each matching automorphism extends to a graph of groups automorphism of
\mathbb{X}
. Applying them all in parallel gives a graph of groups automorphism
\Phi\in\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\leq{\mathrm{Aut}\left({{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\right)}
given by

\Phi=(Id,(\phi_{v})_{v},(Id_{e})_{e},(1)_{e}),

where
\phi_{v}
is as given above for white vertex and is the identity on black vertex groups. It follows that
\Phi(F^{\prime})\cap\mathbb{X}_{v}=F\cap\mathbb{X}_{v}
, when
v
is a white vertex group. If
v
is a black vertex group, then the equality follows from Lemma
3.12
.
We still cannot yet conclude that
\Phi(F^{\prime})=F
as we haven’t fully considered what happens ”outside” the vertex groups.

We now fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
and identify
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},x_{0})
. We have our target fibre
F\leq{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
and we denote the image of the fibre
F^{\prime\prime}=\eta(F^{\prime})
. Let
\tau\subset X
be a spanning tree and let
G_{\tau}\leq{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
be the subgroup of
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
that is the iterated amalgamated product along the edges groups with edges lying in
\tau
. Because
F^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathbb{X}_{v}=F\cap\mathbb{X}_{v}
for every
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
, and
G_{\tau}
is an iterated fibered coproduct, in passing to the category of abelian groups we see that
F\cap G_{\tau}
and
F^{\prime\prime}\cap G_{\tau}
have the same image
\bar{F}_{A}
in the abelianization
A=G_{\tau}/[G_{\tau},G_{\tau}]
.

Now
G={\left\langle G_{\tau},e_{1},\ldots,e_{-\chi(X)+1}\right\rangle}
where the edges
e_{i}
play the role of stable letters realizing
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
as multiple HNN extension of
G_{\tau}
as is the case in the construction of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},\tau)
given in Section
2.1
. Treating these edges as group elements also makes sense given that
G
is realized as a quotient of the Bass group
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
given in (
1
). We think of the graph
X/\tau
, obtained by collapsing the subtree
\tau
to a vertex as the graph underlying this multiple HNN extension. We also note (recall the construction in Theorem
2.7
) that
F^{\prime\prime}
gets an induced splitting as an iterated HNN extension
{\left\langle F^{\prime\prime}\cap G_{\tau},r_{1},\ldots,r_{-\chi(X)+1}\right\rangle}
with the same underlying graph
X/\tau
. In particular we have that
r_{i}=e_{i}g_{i}
for some
g_{i},\in G_{\tau}
.

Consider the abelianization
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}={{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}/[{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}},{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}]
. By functoriality, the inclusion
G_{\tau}\leq{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
gives rise to a canonical (possibly non-injective) map
i:A\to{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}
. The subgroup generated by stable letters
{\left\langle r_{1},\ldots,r_{-\chi(X)+1}\right\rangle}\leq F^{\prime\prime}
maps to a direct factor of
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}
with rank
-\chi(X)+1
.

The group
F
is precisely the preimage in
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
of
\ker(f)=\bar{F}
of some mapping
f:{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
. The image
\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}
of
F^{\prime\prime}
in
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}
is generated by
i(\bar{F}_{A})\leq\ker(f)
and by the images of the stable letters
\bar{r_{i}}
. One has
F^{\prime\prime}\neq F
if and only if for some stable letter image, one has
f(\bar{r_{i}})\neq 0
. For every stable letter
e_{i}
, the corresponding edge group
\mathbb{X}_{e_{i}}
contains a central element
t_{i}
such that
f(\bar{t}_{i})=1
, where
\bar{t}_{i}
is the image of
t_{i}
in
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}_{ab}
. Thus setting
n_{i}=f(\bar{r_{i}}),i=1,\ldots,-\chi(X)+1
and applying the generalized Dehn twist specified by the
{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
-substitution

e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}{t_{i}}^{-n_{i}},e_{i}\in{{\textbf{E}(X\setminus\tau)}}

which can be written as
\Delta\in\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
given by

\Delta=(Id,(Id_{v})_{v},(Id_{e})_{e},(\gamma_{e})_{e}),

where
\gamma_{e}=1
if
e\in{{\textbf{E}(\tau)}}
and
\gamma_{e}={t_{i}}^{-n_{i}}
if
e=e_{i}\in{{\textbf{E}(X\setminus\tau)}}
, gives us
\Delta(F^{\prime\prime})
which which vanishes in the map
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\twoheadrightarrow{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}/F\simeq\mathbb{Z}
and we therefore have
\Delta(F^{\prime\prime})=F
. The result now follows by taking
\Psi=\Delta\circ\Phi.

∎

From this proposition we immediately have:

Corollary 3.15

(Unipotent linear monofibration)
.

Let
F,F^{\prime}
be two finitely generated free groups
and
\phi\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
and
\psi\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F^{\prime}\right)}
be unipotent linearly growing automorphisms.
The corresponding suspensions are abstractly isomorphic, i.e.

F\rtimes_{\phi}\mathbb{Z}\simeq F^{\prime}\rtimes_{\psi}\mathbb{Z}

if and only
F
and
F^{\prime}
have same rank, and for an isomorphism
\iota:F\to F^{\prime}
,
\iota^{-1}\psi\iota
and
\phi^{\pm 1}
have conjugate images in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(F\right)}
.

It is worth noting that by a result of Button
[
But07
, Theorem 3.4]
, for all such groups, there are infinitely many other ways they can be given as suspensions of non-unipotent automorphisms with fibre ranks going to infinity.

3.4.3
Detection of isomorphisms preserving the fibre in vertex groups and graphs of groups

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
be two canonical graph of groups decompositions of globally fibered piecewise trivial suspensions
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle},{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\rtimes_{\alpha^{\prime}}{\left\langle t^{\prime}\right\rangle}
both with the same underlying graph
X
. Suppose furthermore that we have fixed an orientation for each. There is an algorithm which decides whether there is an isomorphism
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
that restricts to isomorphisms

\mathbb{X}_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}_{v}^{\prime};v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}\qquad\mathbb{X}_{e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}_{e}^{\prime};e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}},

which preserves the orientation on the white vertex groups.

Only for the proof below, since we are following
[
DT19
, §4]
, we will call the images of edge groups in vertex groups
peripheral subgroups
of the vertex groups. An
unmarked ordered peripheral structure
is a tuple whose entries form a set of representatives of conjugacy classes of images of edge groups in vertex groups and a
marked ordered peripheral structure
is a tuple of tuples of generators of the images of edge groups in vertex groups.

Proof.

Our algorithm consists of three steps. Each step will introduce a level of structure and will end with a test. It will be evident that the desired isomorphism of graphs of groups will exist if and only if all three tests are passed.

Step 1 Isomorphism of vertex groups.
The hypotheses put us in the setting of
[
DT19
, §4]
. The first test is straightforward.

Test 1.
Are the vertex groups
\mathbb{X}_{v}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{v}
isomorphic for each vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
? Otherwise, we can conclude that no suitable isomorphism of graphs of groups exists.

Step 2 Extensions adjustments and matching peripheral subgroups in white vertex groups.
Having passed the first test, we have that
X
is a bipartite graph and we can assume that white vertices have vertex groups that are direct products of non-abelian free groups and cyclic groups and that the black vertices are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. For each
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
we can find a fibre and orientation preserving isomorphism
\varphi_{v}:\mathbb{X}_{v}\to\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{v}
. Let
b,w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
and let
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
join these vertices. Consider the following diagram:

\mathbb{X}_{b}
\mathbb{X}_{b}
’
\mathbb{X}_{e}
\mathbb{X}_{e}
’
\mathbb{X}_{w}
\mathbb{X}_{w}
’
i_{e,w}
i_{e,b}
i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}
i_{e^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
\varphi_{w}
\varphi_{b}

(7)

where the horizontal monomorphisms are the edge group monomorphisms and the vertical maps are fibre and orientation preserving isomorphisms between vertex groups. By
[
DT19
, Proposition 4.4]
, the graphs of groups
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
are isomorphic if and only if for each
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
and each
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
incident to
v
there is an automorphism
\alpha_{v}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{v}\right)}
and elements
g_{e,v}\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{v}
such that for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{X}_{e}
we have

i_{e^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,b}}}\circ\alpha_{b}\circ\varphi_{b}\circ i_{e,b}(\gamma)=i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}\circ\alpha_{w}\circ\varphi_{w}\circ i_{e,2}(\gamma).

(8)

This equation should be interpreted as using the automorphisms
\alpha_{b},\alpha_{w}
and the conjugations
{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,b}}},{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}
to make the diagram (
7
) commutative. Now the horizontal maps in(
7
) are not bijective so they do not have inverses, however, because they are injective, they are bijective onto their ranges. Therefore for (
8
) to make sense we must be able to match up the peripheral subgroups. Since black vertex groups coincide with the images of incident edge groups, we must first only focus on white vertex groups

Test 2.
Decide for each white vertex
w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
whether there exists a fibre preserving automorphism
\alpha_{w}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{v}\right)}
such that for each
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
incident to
w
, with
b
being the other vertex incident to
e
, there is a conjugating element
g_{e,w}
that

{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}\circ\alpha_{w}(\varphi_{w}(i_{e,w}(\mathbb{X}_{e}))=i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X}_{e}^{\prime}),

where the various mappings are those given in (
7
). By our colouring convention
\mathbb{X}_{w}=F_{w}\oplus{\left\langle t_{w}\right\rangle}
. Then
i_{e,w}(\mathbb{X}_{e})={\left\langle c_{e,w},t_{w}\right\rangle}
where
c_{e,w}
lies in the fibre and
\varphi_{w}(i_{e,w}(\mathbb{X}_{e}))={\left\langle\varphi_{w}(c_{e,q}),t_{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle}
, where
t_{w}^{\prime}
is a generator of the centralizer of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}=F_{w}^{\prime}\oplus{\left\langle t_{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle}
. The second test therefore amounts to finding an automorphism
\alpha_{w}
such that each edge
e
incident to
w
the element
\alpha_{w}(\varphi_{w}(c_{e,w})
is conjugate to
c_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}^{\pm 1}
, where
i_{e^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X}_{e}^{\prime})={\left\langle c_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}},t_{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle}
and
c_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}
lies in the fibre. But this is precisely what the classical Whitehead algorithm for tuples of elements in
F_{w}^{\prime}
decides.

So either the Whitehead algorithm gives us an automorphism of the fibre, which gives us a fibre preserving automorphism of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
that matches up images of edge groups, or there is no such automorphism of the fibre. If there is no such automorphism of the fibre, then we can conclude that there is no automorphism at all which matches the peripheral subgroups. This is because a non-fibre preserving automorphism of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
will still descend to an automorphism of the image

\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}=F_{w}^{\prime}\oplus{\left\langle t_{w}^{\prime}\right\rangle}\twoheadrightarrow F_{w}^{\prime},

since the centre is characteristic. Therefore, if
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
are isomorphic, then there is a fibre-preserving automorphism of each vertex group that passes Test 2. Furthermore, composition with the automorphisms of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
that inverts the generator of the centre and fixes the fibre pointwise gives another automorphism that passes Test 2, so we may assume that the automorphism we found is also orientation preserving.

Step 3 Orbits of markings and matching peripheral subgroups in black vertex groups.
Passing Test 2 ensures the right hand side of (
8
), namely

i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}\circ\alpha_{w}\circ\varphi_{w}\circ i_{e,2}(\gamma)

is well-defined. Recall that the marking of a group is a choice of a tuple of generators. Picking
(c_{e},t_{w})
as a marking of
i_{e,w}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\leq\mathbb{X}_{w}
, as shown in diagram (
7
), gives us a pullback marking of
\mathbb{X}_{e}
, which we can then push forward to
\mathbb{X}_{b}
to get the pair of elements

(c_{e}^{\prime},t_{e}^{\prime})=(\varphi_{b}\circ i_{e,b}\circ i_{e,w}^{-1}(c_{e}),\varphi_{b}\circ i_{e,b}\circ i_{e,w}^{-1}(t_{w}))\in\mathbb{X}_{b}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{X}_{b}^{\prime}.

(9)

Similarly for each of the pairs
(c_{e},t_{w})\in\mathbb{X}_{w}\times\mathbb{X}_{w}
given above we can push them forward to

(c_{e}^{\prime\prime},t_{w}^{\prime\prime})=({\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}\circ\alpha_{w}\circ\varphi_{w}(c_{e}),{\mathrm{ad}_{g_{e,w}}}\circ\alpha_{w}\circ\varphi(t_{w}))\in\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}.

We note that the hypothesis that
\varphi_{w}
is fibre and orientation preserving gives us that
t_{w}^{\prime\prime}=t_{w}^{\prime}
, the generator of centre of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
prescribed by the orientation. Let
\mathcal{P}_{uo}
denote the unmarked ordered peripheral structure on
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
, then
A_{w}^{\prime}={\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{uo}\right)}
denotes the set of fibre and orientation preserving automorphisms of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
that map each peripheral group to a conjugate of itself.
It is easy to see that for any
\beta\in A_{w}^{\prime}
, after post composing by inner automorphism so that
{\mathrm{ad}_{p_{\beta,e^{\prime}}}}\circ\beta(c_{e}^{\prime\prime})=c_{e}^{\beta}\in i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X}_{e}^{\prime})
, the element
c_{e}^{\beta}
depends only on
\beta
and, in fact, we must have
c_{e}^{\beta}=c_{e}^{\prime\prime\pm 1}
. It follows that the orbit of the marked peripheral structure, originally marked by the pairs
(c_{e}^{\prime\prime},t_{w}^{\prime})
, has a finite orbit
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\beta_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{m}^{\beta_{k_{w}}}
under
A_{w}^{\prime}
. We call these the
admissible marked peripheral structures of
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{P}}_{m}
be a choice of admissible marked peripheral structures for each
\mathbb{X}_{w}^{\prime}
where
w
ranges over the white vertices of
X
. For each edge
e
incident to a white vertex
w
there is an element
c_{e}^{\beta_{j}}\in\mathbb{X}^{\prime}_{w}
which is the generator of the fibre component the image of the edge group for the marking in
\underline{\mathcal{P}}_{m}
. Denote

(c_{e}^{\underline{\mathcal{P}}_{m}},t_{e})=(i_{e^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\circ i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}^{-1}(c_{e}^{\beta_{j}}),i_{e^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\circ i_{e^{\prime},w^{\prime}}^{-1}(t_{w}^{\prime}))\in\mathbb{X}_{b}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{X}_{b}^{\prime}.

Test 3.
For each collection of admissible marked peripheral structures
\underline{\mathcal{P}_{m}}
we do the following: in each black vertex
b
we have, for each edge
e
incident to
b
,
two pairs

(c^{\prime}_{e},t_{e}^{\prime})\textrm{~and~}(c_{e}^{\underline{\mathcal{P}}_{m}},t_{e}).

Where
(c^{\prime}_{e},t_{e}^{\prime})
is defined in (
9
). We determine if there is a collection of automorphisms
\alpha_{b}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}_{b}^{\prime}\right)}
for each black vertex
b
such that for every pair we have

(\alpha_{b}(c^{\prime}_{e}),\alpha_{b}(t_{e}^{\prime}))=(c_{e}^{\underline{\mathcal{P}}_{m}},t_{e}).

This amounts to finding some
\alpha_{b}\in\mathrm{GL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
that sends a tuple of vectors to another tuple of vectors and is standard linear algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
.

If we can find such admissible markings and such automorphisms of the black vertex groups, then we will have constructed orientation-preserving isomorphisms of the graph of groups of the desired form. On the other hand, the nonexistence of such markings and automorphisms implies that there are no isomorphisms
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
that restrict to fibre preserving isomorphisms of the vertex groups and edge groups. By Proposition
3.14
, this then implies that no isomorphism
\mathbb{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
of the desired form exists.
∎

Noting that a graph automorphism can give us two different graph of groups structures on the same underlying graph gives:

Corollary 3.17
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be the canonical graph of groups decomposition of a piecewise trivial suspension
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
with underlying graph
X
. Then there is an algorithm that decides if an automorphism
s:X\to X
of graphs can be extended to fibre preserving automorphism
\Sigma=(s,(\varphi_{v}),(\varphi_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))\in\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.

Recall that
\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
is the set of fibre and orientation preserving graph of groups automorphisms that induce a trivial automorphism of graph
X
underlying
\mathbb{X}
. Recall that
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\leq\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
is the subgroup that acts trivially on the edge groups (see Section
2.3
).

Lemma 3.18
.

We can compute a complete set
\{\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{k}\}\subset\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
-coset representatives.

Proof.

Let
w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
be a white vertex and let
t_{w}
be the generator of the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{w}
given by the orientation. For each
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
fix some
c_{e}
such that
{\left\langle c_{e},t_{w}\right\rangle}
generates the the image of the edge group
\mathbb{X}_{e}
in the unique incident white vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{w}
and
c_{e}
lies in the fibre.

The restriction
\varphi_{w}
of any element
\Phi=(Id,\varphi_{v},\varphi_{e},\gamma_{e})\in\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
to a white vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{w}
must map
t_{w}
to itself and must send
c_{e}
to the conjugate
\gamma_{e}^{-1}c_{e}^{\pm 1}\gamma_{e}
.
Consider now the set
\{1,-1\}^{{{\textbf{E}(X)}}}
of functions from
{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
to
\{-1,1\}
. For every
\sigma\in\{1,-1\}^{{{\textbf{E}(X)}}}
, it is possible to determine whether there is a fibre and orientation preserving
\Psi=(Id,\psi_{v},\psi_{e},\delta_{e})\in\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
such that in the white vertex groups we have equality between conjugacy classes

[\psi_{w}(c_{e})]=[c_{e}^{\sigma(e)}],

Indeed, this is precisely an instance of the classical Whitehead problem on the fibre in
\mathbb{X}_{w}
, which is a free group. The
\delta_{e}
in
\Psi
can then be chosen to be the appropriate conjugators.

Next, determining whether such a collection of vertex group automorphisms can then be extended to an element of
\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
amounts to finding automorphisms of the black vertex groups (which are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) that complete the diagram (
7
).

This collection of found automorphisms gives a complete set of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
-coset representatives in
\delta_{0}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.
∎

Corollary
3.17
, Lemma
3.17
, and definitions immediately imply the following.

Corollary 3.19
.

We can compute a complete list of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
-coset representatives in
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.

4
Piecewise trivial suspensions are hereditarily algorithmically tractable

This section is devoted to a few uniform algorithmic properties of the class of unipotent linear suspensions or piecewise trivial suspensions.

A finitely presented group is
coherent
if any finitely generated subgroup is finitely presented. A class of group is
effectively coherent
if, from a presentation of the group and a finite set of elements, an algorithm computes a finite presentation of the generated subgroup.

For example, free abelian groups are an effectively coherent class of groups: given a finite subset of size
n
in a free abelian group
A
, the Smith normal form of the given homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to A
reveals a basis for the generated subgroup.

Free groups are nevertheless the archetype of an effectively coherent class of groups: any subgroup of a free group is free, but the task is to compute a basis. Stallings foldings provide an algorithmic and elegant way for this.

In a work that deepens the foldings technique, Kapovich Weidmann and Miasnikov proved that fundamental groups of so-called benign graphs of groups are effectively coherent
[
KWM05
]
.

Free-by-cyclic groups are coherent by a deep result of Feighn and Handel
[
FH99
]
.

We will prove here that unipotent linear suspensions of finitely generated free groups are effectively coherent (Proposition
4.5
) by proving the benign character of their structural graph of groups decompositions.

Following
[
Tou18
]
, we say that a class of groups is
hereditarily algorithmic tractable, if
the class is closed for taking finitely generated subgroups, is effectively coherent,
the presentations of the groups in the class being furthermore recursively enumerable, and if the class has a uniform solution to the generation problem, and to the conjugacy problem.

In other words, there is an algorithm that given a group presentation in the class, and any finite subset of the group, produces a finite presentation of the generated subgroup, indicates if it is the whole group, and tells whether the given elements are conjugate.

This combination of algorithmic problems is useful to treat and compare decomposition in graphs of groups in which the elements of the class are vertex groups, see for instance
[
Tou18
,
DT19
,
DT21
]
. Through Propositions
4.6
,
4.7
, and
4.10
, we will prove the following.

Theorem 4.1
.

The class of finitely generated subgroups of a given globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension
of a free group is a hereditarily algorithmically tractable class.

An immediate application of this work is that we will be able to fulfill the hypotheses of Proposition
3.7
and Corollary
3.10
and therefore algorithmically construct the canonical structural splittings of suspensions as well as of subgroups of suspensions, which in turn is foundation for the computations in Sections
5
and
6
.

4.1
Effective coherence, effective generation, subgroup membership

Recall a definition introduced by Kapovich, Weidmann, and Miasnikov
[
KWM05
]
.

Definition 4.2

(Benign graphs of groups,
[
KWM05
, Definition 5.6]
)
.

A finite graph of groups
\mathbb{A}
is
benign
if the following
conditions are satisfied:

(i)

For each vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(A)}}
and an edge
e\in{{\textbf{E}(A)}}
with
o(e)=v
there is an algorithm with the
following property. Given a finite set
X\subset A_{v}
and an
element
a\in A_{v}
the algorithm decides whether

I={\left\langle X\right\rangle}\cap a\alpha_{e}(A_{e})

is empty. If
I\neq\emptyset
, the
algorithm produces an element of
I
.

(ii)

Every edge group
A_{e}
of
\mathbb{A}
is slender.

(iii)

Every edge group
A_{e}
of
\mathbb{A}
has solvable uniform
membership problem, i.e. there is an algorithm which, given a
finite subset
X\subset A_{e}
and an element
a\in A_{e}
decides
whether or not
a\in{\left\langle X\right\rangle}
.

(iv)

For each vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(A)}}
and edge
e\in{{\textbf{E}(A)}}

with
o(e)=v
there is an algorithm with the following
property. For any finite subset
X\subset A_{v}
the algorithm computes a
finite generating set for the subgroup
\alpha_{e}(A_{e})\cap{\left\langle X\right\rangle}.

Observe that conditions
(i)
to
(iv)
are conditions on vertex
groups, edge groups and the attachment maps of their adjacent edge groups. From an algorithmic viewpoint, we prefer to state this definition in the context of graphs of groups, however, it can also be stated in the context of
G
-trees.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a finite graph of groups, such that any vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}

is of the form
{\mathbb{F}(v)}\oplus{\left\langle z_{v}\right\rangle}
, where
{\mathbb{F}(v)}
is a
free group and any attached image of edge group
\alpha_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\leq\mathbb{X}_{v}
is of the form

{\left\langle c_{v},z_{v}\right\rangle},c_{v}\in{\mathbb{F}(v)}
, then
\mathbb{X}
is benign: properties (
i
) and
(
iv
) of Definition
4.2
hold.

Proof.

Consider the vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}={\mathbb{F}(v)}\oplus{\left\langle z\right\rangle}
, and an
adjacent edge
e
, with attaching map
\alpha_{e}:\mathbb{X}_{e}\to\mathbb{X}_{v}
. Consider the standard projection

\pi_{{\mathbb{F}(v)}}:{\mathbb{F}(v)}\oplus{\left\langle z\right\rangle}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{F}(v)}
. Given any finite set

S\subset{\mathbb{F}(v)}\oplus{\left\langle z\right\rangle}
it is possible to compute the image

\bar{S}=\pi_{{\mathbb{F}(v)}}(S)
. By hypotheses the groups
\alpha_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})

that occur in Definition
4.2
are subgroups of the
form
{\left\langle c,z\right\rangle}
where
c\in{\mathbb{F}(v)}
. In particular such groups
contain
\ker(\pi_{{\mathbb{F}(v)}})
. Therefore

I={\left\langle S\right\rangle}\cap a\alpha_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\neq\emptyset
if and only if

\bar{S}\cap a{\left\langle c\right\rangle}\neq\emptyset
, which is routine to check in
a free group. Because
g\in a{\left\langle c,z\right\rangle}
if and only if
[a^{-1}g,c]=1
, an
element of the intersection can be found by enumerating
{\left\langle S\right\rangle}

and checking whether the commutator vanishes. (
i
) of Definition

4.2
therefore holds.

We now show that property (
iv
) of Definition

4.2
holds.
Write

S=(f_{1}z^{n_{1}},\ldots,f_{k}z^{n_{k}})

and perform
Nielsen reduction (see
[
LS01
, §I.2]
)
on the tuple
(f_{1}z^{n_{1}},\ldots,f_{k}z^{n_{k}})
ignoring the

z
-letters. This will give a tuple

R=(r_{1}z^{m_{1}},\cdots,r_{j}z^{m_{j}},1\cdot z^{m_{j+1}},\ldots,1\cdot z^{m_{k}})

with
r_{i}\neq 1
for
i\leq j
(we allow
j=0
). Let

e=\gcd(m_{j+1},\ldots,m_{k})
then we
have

{\left\langle S\right\rangle}={\left\langle r_{1}z^{m_{1}},\cdots,r_{j}z^{m_{j}}\right\rangle}\oplus{\left\langle z^{e}\right\rangle}

with
\pi_{{\mathbb{F}(v)}}({\left\langle S\right\rangle})={\left\langle r_{1},\cdots,r_{j}\right\rangle}={\left\langle\bar{S}\right\rangle}
, where the

r_{i}
form a basis of
{\left\langle\bar{S}\right\rangle}
. It is possible to decide the
minimal exponent
l
such that
{\left\langle\bar{S}\right\rangle}\cap{\left\langle c\right\rangle}={\left\langle c^{l}\right\rangle}

and to find the explicit (possibly empty) product (or word)

c^{l}=W(r_{1},\cdots,r_{j})
. It follows
that

{\left\langle S\right\rangle}\cap{\left\langle c,z\right\rangle}={\left\langle W(r_{1}z^{m_{1}},\cdots,r_{j}z^{m_{j}}),z^{e}\right\rangle}={\left\langle c^{l}z^{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{j}},z^{e}\right\rangle},

with
e

possibly zero and with
l=0
if and only if

{\left\langle c\right\rangle}\cap{\left\langle\bar{S}\right\rangle}=\{1\}
. Since every step was computable,
(
iv
) of Definition
4.2
holds.

∎

We will use the benign property with the following result of Kapovich Weidmann and Miasnikov.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
KWM05
, Theorems 5.8 (b)) and 5.13]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a benign graph of groups such that every vertex
group is effectively
coherent
and has solvable uniform membership problem. Then

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{A},a_{0})
is also effectively coherent and has solvable
uniform membership problem. Moreover, the algorithms for these two problems can be constructed from those given by the assumption.

From this theorem we deduce the following, about piecewise trivial suspensions of free groups.

Proposition 4.5
.

If
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
is a piecewise trivial suspension
globally fibered with fiber a finitely generated free group, then it is
effectively coherent and has effectively
solvable uniform membership problem.

Proof.

Given the abelian splitting
of

{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
, properties (
ii
) and (
iii
) of
Definition
4.2
follow immediately. Let us now focus
on groups of the form
{\mathbb{F}(v)}\oplus{\left\langle z\right\rangle}
, where
{\mathbb{F}(v)}

is free. Properties (
i
) and (
iv
) follow from
Proposition
4.3
. The result now follows from
Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Treating a finite subset of a subgroup of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
as generating a subgroup of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
, we immediately obtain:

Proposition 4.6
.

The class of finitely generated subgroups of piecewise trivial suspensions globally fibered, with fiber a finitely generated free group, is effectively coherent.

We can also solve the generation problem.

Proposition 4.7
.

The class of finitely generated subgroups of piecewise trivial suspensions globally fibered, with fiber a finitely generated free group, has a uniform solution to the generation problem.

More generally, if a class of groups is (uniformly) effectively
coherent, and has a uniform (over the groups in the class) solution
to the uniform (over the f.p. subgroups in a group) membership
problem, then it has a uniform solution to the generation problem
for its subgroups.

Proof.

Let us prove the second assertion. Given a subgroup
H
given by generators
\mathcal{S}_{H}
, and a family
\mathcal{S}_{K}
of elements, one can compute a presentation of
H
and a presentation of
\langle\mathcal{S}_{K}\rangle
, one can then use the uniform membership problem for f.p. subgroups to determine whether
\mathcal{S}_{K}<H
and
\mathcal{S}_{H}<\langle\mathcal{S}_{K}\rangle
. The first assertion then follows from Proposition
4.6
.
∎

4.2
Consequences of effective coherence: computing canonical splittings and solving the conjugacy problem in subgroups

We are now in a position to compute canonical splittings of finitely generated subgroups of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
. Since effective coherence lets us algorithmically obtain a presentation for finitely generated subgroups and since subgroups of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
are without 2-torsion and have solvable word problem, we can apply
[
Tou18
, Corollary 1.1]
to get:

Corollary 4.8
.

Given a finite generating set of a subgroup
H
of a piecewise trivial suspension, we can compute a Grushko decomposition and presentations of the indecomposable free free factors. In particular, we can decide if
H
is freely indecomposable.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
S=\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}\}
be a collection of elements in a piecewise trivial suspension
\Gamma={{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
. Then we can compute a Grushko decomposition of
H={\left\langle S\right\rangle}
and for each one-ended free factor of
H
, we can compute the canonical splitting
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}
given by Corollary
3.10
.

Proof.

Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
4.8
enable us to get presentations for the freely indecomposable free factors of
H
or to assert that
H
is in fact a free group.

Note that the free group
F
is hereditarily algorithmically tractable and that it is possible to compute centralizers of cyclic subgroups in subgroups of
F
. It follows that the requirements of Corollary
3.10
are fulfilled for the one-ended free factors of
H
, and therefore that it allows to compute the canonical splitting
\widehat{\mathbb{X}}
.
∎

Bogopolski, Martino, Maslakova, and Ventura
[
BMMV06
]
proved that the conjugacy problem in [finitely-generated free]-by-cyclic groups is solvable. We can extend this result to finitely generated subgroups.

Proposition 4.10
.

The Conjugacy Problem is uniformly solvable for the class of finitely generated subgroups of a piecewise trivial suspension of a finitely generated free group.

Given that we have a canonical tree for such a group, it is not surprising that the proof will split in two cases, the case of elliptic elements (in which the trivial action of the normalizer of edge groups helps to reduce to the vertex case), and the case of hyperbolic elements. Although the proof in this later case invokes computations with normal forms, and Proposition
5.6
which is only proved later, we note that this Proposition
4.10
is not actually used anywhere else in this paper so there is no circular logic. Proposition
5.6
is a statement about the normal forms that we develop in Section
5
.

Proof.

We are given a piecewise trivial suspension as in the statement, a finite set
S
of elements in it, and we denote by
H
the subgroup generated by
S
. By Corollary
4.8
we can decide whether
H
is freely decomposable, and if so, find a Grushko decomposition of
H
. Through a classical reduction to free factors (see for instance
[
LS01
]
), it is enough to prove that the conjugacy problem is solvable in the case where
H={\left\langle S\right\rangle}
is one-ended. Hence, we assume in the following that
H
is one-ended.

Compute (by Proposition
4.9
) the canonical splitting of
H
from Corollary
3.10
, and denote it by
\mathbb{X}
.

We are now given
g,g^{\prime}\in H
and must decide whether they are conjugate. Given the splitting constructed in
3.10
and our understanding of free abelian groups and conjugacy in the vertex groups, which are all either free, free abelian of rank 2, or a direct product of a free group and a cyclic group, we can decide if
g,g^{\prime}
are in reduced form, and if not perform elementary reductions to obtain words of shorter syllable length. In this manner we can decide whether
g
and
g^{\prime}
are hyperbolic or elliptic. Obviously, if they are not both hyperbolic or both elliptic, they cannot be conjugate.

If
g,g^{\prime}
are both hyperbolic elements, Proposition
5.6
can be used to decide whether they are conjugate.

If
g,g^{\prime}\in H
are elliptic, then since
\mathbb{X}
is computed, we can decide whether
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate into the same vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}
. If for each vertex
v
of
\mathbb{X}
, one of them is not conjugate into
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, then they cannot be conjugate to one another. Hence we may assume that we found
v
such that both
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate into
\mathbb{X}_{v}
. We may as well assume that they are both in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, or (by a slight abuse of notation and a choice of lift of
v
in the dual Bass-Serre tree
T
), both in
H_{v}
(the stabilizer of
v\in T
in
H
).

Let
T_{g}=\{x\in T|g\cdot x=x\}
be the pointwise fixed subtree of
g
, and
T_{g^{\prime}}
defined analogously. By hypothesis,
v\in T_{g}\cap T_{g^{\prime}}
. If there exists
h\in H
such that
hgh^{-1}=g^{\prime}
