, then
h\cdot T_{g}=T_{g^{\prime}}
.

If
g
is not conjugate into any images of edge groups, then
T_{g}=v
, then any such conjugator
h
must be in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
.
In this case, the existence of such
h
is decided by a solution to the conjugacy problem in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, a free group or a trivial suspension of a free group.

We may therefore assume that
g
and
g^{\prime}
are conjugate to the image of a certain edge group, adjacent to
v
.
Observing that
H=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)\leq\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
is a free factor, if
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
, then there must be some reduced word
w=a_{0}e_{1}a_{1}\cdots e_{n}a_{n}
with the
e_{i}
being symbols in
({{\textbf{E}(X)}})^{\pm 1}
and
a_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{v_{i}}
, for which

g^{\prime}=w^{-1}gw=(a_{n}^{-1}\bar{e}_{n}\cdots\bar{e}_{1}a_{0}^{-1})g(a_{0}e_{1}a_{1}\cdots e_{n}a_{n})\,\in\mathbb{X}_{v}.

(10)

For the right-hand side expression to cancel down to
g^{\prime}
we need
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
to be a loop based at
v
, and the word
w
defines an element of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
. Furthermore, in the Bass-Serre
T
, then (
10
) says that
g
stabilizes
v
and
w\cdot v
. Since
d(v,w\cdot v)=n
, and since the action on
T
is 4-acylindrical,
n\leq 4
. We distinguish two cases.

Case 1:
If
v
is an abelian black vertex, then we can assume
a_{0}
is trivial and
\bar{e}_{1}ge_{1}=g_{1}
lies in lies in the image of an edge group in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
. Now if
e_{2}=\bar{e}_{1}
then
a_{1}
needs to centralize
\bar{e}_{1}ge_{1}
which means
a_{1}
is in the image of the same edge group; contradicting that
w
is reduced. Otherwise,
g_{1}
is conjugate into the image of the edge group associated to
e_{2}
. By our description of the canonical splitting of
H
, this is only possible if
g_{1}
is a non-trivial element of the centre of the white vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
, in which case
a_{1}^{-1}g_{1}a_{1}=g_{1}
. Let
g_{2}=\bar{e}_{2}g_{1}e_{2}
then
g_{2}\in\mathbb{X}_{v_{2}}
which, again, is a black abelian vertex group so
a_{2}^{-1}g_{1}a_{2}=g_{2}
, so we can assume
a_{2}
is trivial and since
w
. If
v_{2}=v
and
n=2
we have
g^{\prime}=\bar{e}_{2}\bar{e}_{1}ge_{1}e_{2}
.

If
n>2
then
n=4
as all loops in
X
must have even length. Let
g_{3}=\bar{e}_{3}g_{2}e_{3}=\bar{e}_{3}\bar{e}_{2}\bar{e}_{1}ge_{1}e_{2}e_{3}
. We must have that
g_{3}
is conjugate in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{3}}
to the image of the edge group associated to
e_{4}
, which is distinct from the image of the edge group associated to
e_{3}
in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{3}}
. Again this is only possible if
g_{3}
is in the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{v_{3}}
, which is a non-abelian white vertex group. Now, the equality
g_{3}=\bar{e}_{3}\bar{e}_{2}g_{1}e_{2}e_{3}
in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
translates to the fact that there are vertices
w,w^{\prime}
distance 2 apart and some non-trivial element
h\in H
that lies in the centre of
H_{w}
and
H{w^{\prime}}
, but since we know that central elements in white vertices fix the ball of radius 2 about those vertices, we get a contradiction to 4-acylindricity.

It follows that in Case 1, it is enough to take all loops
\ell=e_{1}e_{2}
of length 2 based at
v
in
X
and verify if
g^{\prime}=\bar{\ell}g\ell
, to decide whether
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate.

Case 2:
If
v
is a non-abelian white vertex, then a similar analysis to Case 1 above lets us conclude that either

g^{\prime}=a_{2}^{-1}\bar{e}_{2}\bar{e}_{1}a_{0}^{-1}ga_{0}e_{1}e_{2}a_{2}

where
g
is conjugated in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
by
a_{0}
into the image of the edge group associated to
e_{1}
and
g^{\prime}
is conjugated in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
by
a_{2}^{-1}
into the image of the edge group associated to
e_{2}
, or

g^{\prime}=a_{4}^{-1}\bar{e}_{4}\bar{e}_{3}\bar{e}_{2}e_{1}a_{0}^{-1}ga_{0}e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}a_{4},

where
g
is conjugated in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
by
a_{0}
into the image of the edge group associated to
e_{1}
and
g^{\prime}
is is conjugated in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
by
a_{4}^{-1}
into the image of the edge group associated to
e_{4}
, and in particular
\bar{e}_{2}\bar{e}_{1}a_{0}^{-1}ga_{0}e_{1}e_{2}
is in the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{v_{2}}
. Note that for
H
, in a non-abelian white vertex group, the normalizer of the image of an edge group acts trivially on that subgroup by conjugation.

It follows that in Case 2, to decide if
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate, it is enough to consider all loops
\ell
of length 4 based at
v
, to decide if there are elements
a,a^{\prime}
that conjugate
g,g^{\prime}
(respectively) into the appropriate edge groups and check whether
g^{\prime}=a^{\prime-1}\bar{\ell}a^{-1}ga\ell a^{\prime}
.

Thus in both cases the problem reduces to deciding whether
g,g^{\prime}
are conjugate into the edge groups (which is a trivial task for abelian black vertex groups) and then if these conjugates, which are unique, are ”immediately” conjugate by the Bass-Serre relations, i.e. a finite composition (at most 4) of the edge group monomorphisms specified in
\mathbb{X}
and their inverses bring the conjugate of
g
to the conjugate of
g^{\prime}
.
∎

The following restatement of what was shown in the proof above will be useful.

Lemma 4.11
.

g,h\in\mathbb{X}_{v}
are conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
if and only if either

1.

g
and
h
are conjugate in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, or

2.

g
is conjugate in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
to
g^{\prime}\in\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
,
h
is conjugate in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
to
h^{\prime}\in\tau_{e^{\prime}}(\mathbb{X}_{e^{\prime}})
. The conjugates
g^{\prime},h^{\prime}
are unique and there is some
\ell
that is an edge path in
X
based at
v
of length at most 4 such that
g^{\prime}=\ell g^{\prime}\bar{\ell}
in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})

5
The mixed Whitehead problem in globally fibered piecewise trivial suspensions.

The action of a group
G
on itself by conjugation extends on the set of all tuples, which is
G^{(\mathbb{N})}=\sqcup_{n>0}G^{n}
(all our tuples are ordered). If
T\in G^{(\mathbb{N})}
, we write
[T]
for its conjugacy class, that is, the orbit of
T
under the action of
G
by conjugation.

The mixed Whitehead problem under a subgroup
H
of
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(G\right)}
or of
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
is the following decision problem. Given an integer
k
, and for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
two tuples
S_{i}\in G^{(\mathbb{N})}
and
T_{i}\in G^{(\mathbb{N})}
, decide whether there exists an automorphism
\sigma\in H
such
\forall i,\,[\sigma(S_{i})]=[T_{i}]
.
If there exists such
\sigma
, we say that
\underline{S}=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
and
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\dots T_{k})
are
mixed Whitehead equivalent
under
H
. This problem was emphasized and studied by Bogopolski and Ventura
[
BV11
]
, initially for torsion-free hyperbolic groups.

In
[
DT19
]
the mixed Whitehead problem is used for comparing attaching maps of edge groups in vertex groups in certain graphs of groups. Typically, if one is given a graph, a group for each vertex and for each edge, and two families of attaching maps from edge groups to vertex groups, one has two graphs of groups; the mixed Whitehead problem helps to decide whether these two graphs of groups are isomorphic as such.
More specifically, suppose for example that we have a graph. with two vertices
v,w
and two edges
e_{1},e_{2}
from
w
to
v
. Assume that the group of
w
is a given group
H
, and the edge groups are
H_{1},H_{2}
with given attaching maps to
H
. Let
G
be the group of
v
. We need attaching maps
H_{i}\to G
. Consider two pairs of monomorphisms
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}:H_{1}\to G
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}:H_{2}\to G
. The choices
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})
and
(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})
define two graphs of groups. In order to
decide whether they
are (globally) isomorphic,
we want to decide whether there exists an automorphism
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(G\right)}
such that
\alpha_{i}
is conjugate in
G
to
\sigma\circ\beta_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. This is the mixed Whitehead problem over
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(G\right)}
for the tuples that are images of a choice of generating tuples in
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.

In the refinement
[
DT21
]
that is designed to study the conjugacy problem in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(F_{n}\right)}
, the vertex and edge groups have a fibre and an orientation. Typically, the group
G
that appears there is isomorphic to
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
for
\alpha
a polynomially growing automorphism of a free group. In this particular context, we need to solve the mixed Whitehead problem over the subgroup of
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
that preserves the fibre and the orientation.

The goal of this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 5.1
.

If
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}=F\rtimes\mathbb{Z}
is a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension with fiber a free group
F
, then the mixed Whitehead problem for
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
under
{{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}
is decidable.

Given
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
, by Proposition
4.9
, we can compute its structural tree to get
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
and express any
g\in{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
as some word

g=g_{1}\,e_{1}\,g_{2}\,e_{2}\,\cdots\,e_{n}\,g_{n+1}

where the
g_{i}
are elements of the vertex groups that are isomorphic to
F_{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
(for different
n
, depending on the vertices). In this sense, elements of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
can be thought of as tuples of elements of the “simpler” vertex groups. Unfortunately, this approach is too naive.

In Subsection
5.1.1
we present normal form conventions that enable us to “coordinatize” elements
g\in{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
, or elements of one-ended subgroups of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
, using a tuple
DC(g,e)
of double cosets, a tuple of integers
\mathrm{pow}(g,e)
of powers of generators of cyclic groups, and some power
t^{k}
of the stable letter. These normal forms rely on a basepoint
b
of
\mathbb{X}
, that defines
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, and on some edge
e
called a polarizing edge, whose importance will only become fully apparent in Subsection
5.4.5
.

In subsection
5.1.2
we introduce short positions, which are a strengthening of cyclically reduced conjugates. Although the concept of cyclically reduced is well-understood in free groups or simple graphs of groups such as amalgamated products or HNN extensions, the concept of cyclically reduced in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is not satisfactory when the axis of translation of a hyperbolic element in the dual Bass-Serre tree doesn’t contain a lift of the base point
b
. This leads us to “changing basepoints”, i.e. to consider
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
instead of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, where both
b,v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
. This is achieved by noting that

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v),\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)\leq\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})

are conjugate subgroups. By exploiting the 4-acylindricity of the canonical trees for
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
and its one-ended subgroups, we can show that hyperbolic elements have a finite number of distinguished conjugates, called conjugates in short position and that these can be computed. On the one hand, this result covers the conjugacy problem for hyperbolic elements in one-ended subgroups of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
. On the other hand, short positions play another important role. Given two tuples of tuples
\underline{S}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
and
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, the mixed Whitehead problem is actually an orbit problem for
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
as we need to find not only
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}\right)}
but also conjugators
g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}
such that
g_{i}\sigma(S_{i})g_{i}^{-1}=T_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
. Short positions will play a role in restricting the conjugators we need to consider.

In general, for a free group
F
there is no reasonable way in which
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
will act on a double coset space
H\backslash F/K
and no known algorithms that decide if there is an automorphism that sends a double coset to some target double coset. In Section
5.2
we associate a pair of subgroups to the double cosets that occur in
DC(g,e)
. The reason for doing so is that there is an algorithm due to Gersten (see Theorem
5.25
) that can decide if there is an automorphism of a free group that sends a subgroup of a free group to another subgroup. We then introduce linkage configurations which is a way of collecting all the linkages from a sequence
DC(g,e)
(as well as their duals) that will be used later on to decide if it is possible to bring all the double cosets, in the right order, arising from words in the tuples in
\underline{S}
to corresponding cosets arising from the content of
\underline{T}
.

In Section
5.3
we describe slight modifications to the tuples in
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
that will make them easier to work with. These amount to ensuring that the first entry of a tuple is hyperbolic, if possible, and conjugating in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
so that the first entry is also in centred short position.

In Section
5.4
we verify that a special kind of action of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
on tuples of tuples behaves well with respect to choices of normal forms and other conventions. In particular, we show that a variation of Gersten’s algorithm: the mixed Whitehead problem for subgroups of free groups (see Theorem
5.26
) can be used to simultaneously send all double cosets that occur in
\underline{S}
to the double cosets that occur in
\underline{T}
.

In Section
5.5
we finally show, through a series of reductions, how to solve the fibre and orientation preserving mixed Whitehead problem in
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
by using all the material computations developed up to that point. Finally in Section
5.6
we give the proof of Theorem
5.26
, which is substantial and was delayed so as not to break the flow of our arguments.

5.1
Describing elements

5.1.1
Normal forms

For this subsection, we will exceptionally consider
G
to either be a piecewise trivial suspension or a finitely generated one-ended subgroup of a piecewise trivial suspension. We have a canonical graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
and for some
b\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
we identify
G=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, where
b
is chosen to be a white vertex, or equivalently, that
\mathbb{X}_{b}
is non-abelian. We also fix a
breadth first search
(BFS) spanning tree
\tau\subset X
based at
b
, this means that for any
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
,
\tau
contains a shortest path from
b
to
v
.

Consider a word in the generators of the Bass group representing an element in
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{X}},b)
:

w=g_{1}\,e_{1}\,g_{2}\,e_{2}\,\cdots\,e_{n}\,g_{n+1}\in\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})

(11)

with
g_{j}\in\mathbb{X}_{\tau(e_{j-1})}
if
j>1
and
g_{j}\in\mathbb{X}_{i(e_{j})}
if
j\leq n
. We start by giving vocabulary to describe a few characteristics of
w
and the element it defines. Its
underlying
X
-path
(in
X
) is the path
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
, from
b
to
b
. The word
w
is
reduced
if its
X
-path has minimal length among all words representing the same element. Its
syllable length
is the number of edges that appear in the
X
-path for any reduced word.

Even among reduced words, the choice of elements
g_{i}
that appear in (
11
) are not unique. A normal form is a function that assigns to every element of
g\in G
of syllable length
n
a tuple
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n+1})
of elements lying in the appropriate vertex groups, i.e. it is a function that assigns to every
g\in G
a specific representative word.

The vertex groups of
\mathbb{X}
are either isomorphic to
F_{v}
, or
F_{v}\oplus{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
, or
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{Z}
, where
F_{v}
is a free group of rank
n_{v}
(isomorphic to a subgroup of the fiber
F
, but not necessarily in
F
, in the case where
G
is not the entire globally fibered ambient group). A choice of ordered basis for each
F_{v}
induces the
shortlex
well-order
\leq_{v}
on
F_{v}
. If
\mathbb{X}_{v}\simeq F_{v}\oplus{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
then we write
(w,t_{v}^{n})<(u,t_{v}^{m})
if and only if either
|n|<|m|
or, if
|n|=|m|
then
|m|/m<|n|/n
, or if
n=m
then
w<_{v}u
. In this manner, we have a well-order on each non-abelian vertex group.

For all edges
e
with
\tau(e)=v
, we denote
I_{e}=\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\leq\mathbb{X}_{v}
. Similarly, if
v=i_{e}(e)
, we denote by
O_{e}=i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\leq\mathbb{X}_{v}
. Note that
O_{e}=I_{\bar{e}}
. A pair of edges
(e,e^{\prime})
such that
\tau(e)=i(e^{\prime})
is called a
turn
. An element represented by a word
w
as in (
11
) is an element of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
if and only if
i(e_{1})=b
and each
(e_{i},e_{i+1})
is a turn where indices
i,i+1
are taken are modulo
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
. The
double coset space associated to the turn
(e,e^{\prime})
is
I_{e}\backslash\mathbb{X}_{v}/O_{e^{\prime}}
and we define

DCR(e,e^{\prime})=\{\bar{g}\in\mathbb{X}_{v}:\bar{g}\textrm{ is minimal w.r.t $<$ in its $I_{e}\backslash\mathbb{X}_{v}/O_{e^{\prime}}$ double coset}\},

to be the set of
double coset representatives for the turn
(e,e^{\prime}).

We will now define normal forms. First, we note that
\mathbb{X}
will always have an underlying bipartite graph with black and white vertices. Furthermore, if
v
is a black vertex then
\mathbb{X}_{v}
will be abelian (either isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
) and it will coincide with the images of all incident edge groups. In particular, we can always use the Bass group relations to rewrite
w
as in (
11
) so that
g_{2j}=1
for all
1\leq j\leq n/2
.

Convention 5.2
.

When writing elements of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
as in (
11
), we will always ensure that
b
is a white vertex with
\mathbb{X}_{b}
non-abelian and we will ensure that
g_{i}
that lie in abelian vertex groups are trivial.

Given a word
w
as in (
11
), due to not being “sandwiched” between two letters from
{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
, there is some ambiguity in the choice of double coset space for the elements
g_{1},g_{n+1}
. We remedy this by picking an edge
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
such that
i(e)=b
. Let
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
be written as a reduced word as in (
11
) then the
tuple of double cosets for
g
polarized by
e
is the tuple

DC(g,e)=(I_{\bar{e}}g_{1}O_{e_{1}},1,I_{e_{2}}g_{3}O_{e_{3}},1,\ldots,I_{e_{n}}g_{n+1}O_{e}),

in particular we take the first and last cosets to lie in the double coset spaces associated to the turns
(\bar{e},e_{1})
and
(e_{n},e)
. This choice of
e
will become important later on.

We also have the corresponding sequence of
double coset representatives for
g
polarized by
e
.

DCR(g,e)=(\bar{f}_{1},1,\bar{f}_{3},\ldots,\bar{f}_{n+1}).

We note that, as indicated by the notation,
DCR(g,e)
and
DC(g,e)
depend only on
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
and not on the choice of word used to represent
g
.
Let
\mathbb{X}_{v}=F_{v}\oplus{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
be a non-abelian vertex group with infinite cyclic centre generated by
t_{v}
. Since
G
is a one-ended subgroup of a piecewise trivial suspension, Proposition
3.9
item
1
implies that the element
t_{v}
lies in
O_{e}
for all
e
such that
i(e)=v
.

For any
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, expressed as a word
w
of the form of
11
, let
\bar{f}_{i}
be the
i
-th entry in the tuple
DCR(g,e)
, and for all edge
e^{\prime}\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
, let
c_{e^{\prime}}
is the
<
-minimal generator of the cyclic group
I_{e^{\prime}}\cap F_{\tau(e^{\prime})}
. The previous observation that
t_{i(\bar{e^{\prime}})}
lies in
O_{\bar{e^{\prime}}}=I_{e^{\prime}}
, allows to express
g
as a new word

\bar{w}=c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{3}\cdots e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}\bar{f}_{n+1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k}.

(12)

The word
\bar{w}
given in (
12
) is called the
normal form for
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
polarized by
e
the tuple of exponents

\mathrm{pow}(g,e)=(d_{0},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n},d_{n+1},k)

is called the
power vector
and the exponent
k
is called the
abscissa
, where we set
k=0
if
t_{b}=1
. In summary.

Lemma 5.3

(Normal form polarized at
e
)
.

Given
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
and some
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
such that
i(e)=b
the
X
-path underlying
g
, the tuples
DCR(g,e)
and
\mathrm{pow}(g,e)
, and the abscissa of
g
are well-defined.

5.1.2
Short positions

We say that
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is
cyclically reduced
if it has minimal syllable length among all its conjugates in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
. For graphs of groups that are more complicated than amalgamated products or HNN extensions, it may be that
g
is cyclically reduced, but its syllable length exceeds the translation length of the action of
g
on the dual Bass-Serre Tree. By our convention that
\tau
is a BFS-tree we have that
g
has a family of cyclically reduced conjugates of the form

Eg_{v}\bar{E}

where
E
is a path from
b
to some white vertex
v
in
\tau
and
g_{v}\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)\leq\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
. Furthermore, the syllable length of
g_{v}
coincides with the translation length of
g
. We say that
g_{v}
is a
recentering of
g
at
v
. We note that the axis of translation of
g
in the Bass-Serre tree must contain a lift of
v
.

We assume that
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is a hyperbolic element and therefore that any recentering
g_{v}
is also hyperbolic. We will switch the basepoint to
v
and use the notation developed in Section
5.1.1
in this new context.

We say that
h\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
is
centred
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short
if it is cyclically reduced, has syllable length equal to its translation length, and can be written in normal form

h=e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}\cdots.

It is obvious that any hyperbolic element recentered at
v
is conjugate in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
to an element in prefix-short form. Two prefix-short elements

e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}\cdots,e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d^{\prime}_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}\cdots

are
elliptically-conjugate
if they are conjugate by an element of
O_{e_{1}}\leq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
and we say they lie in the same
elliptic-class
.

We say that
h\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short
if there exists a path
E\subset\tau
from
b
to
v
such that

h=Eg_{v}\bar{E}

and
g_{v}
is centred
e_{1}e_{2}
prefix-short. It is clear that every hyperbolic
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
has at least one prefix-short conjugate.

If
h\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
is centred
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short, we say it is in
centred short position
if its
e_{1}
-polarized normal form is

•

h=e_{1}e_{2}\bar{c}_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}f_{2}c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d}t_{v}^{k}
, with
d_{2}=0
and
d
minimal (w.r.t some fixed well-order on
\mathbb{Z}
) among all other conjugates of
h
that are in
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short position with
d_{2}=0
, if
h
has translation length 2

•

h=e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}e_{3}e_{4}c_{e_{4}}^{d}\bar{f}_{4}\cdots
, with
d_{2}=0
and
d
minimal (w.r.t some fixed well-order on
\mathbb{Z}
) among all other conjugates of
h
that are in
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short position with
d_{2}=0
, if
h
has translation length 4 or more.

Although they are not explicitly invoked in the definition, the prefix
e_{1}e_{2}
as well as the basepoint
v
are completely specified. To show that this is canonical we have the following:

Lemma 5.4
.

The exponent
d
given in the definition of centred short position determines the first 6 edges of the segment
[\tilde{v},h\cdot\tilde{v}]
of the axis of
h
for the action on the Bass-Serre tree dual to
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
.

Proof.

First, consider the case where
h
has translation length 2. Then the axis of
h
is the same as the axis of
h^{3}
, passing to
e_{1}
-polarized normal forms we have

h^{3}=e_{1}e_{2}\bar{f}_{2}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e}^{d^{\prime}}\bar{f}_{2}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d^{\prime\prime}}\bar{f}_{2}t_{v}^{3k},

where
{c_{e_{2}}}^{d^{\prime}}
is obtained by migrating
c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d}
across
e_{1}e_{2}
, and it is clear that the exponent
d^{\prime}
determines the first 6 edges of the path
[\tilde{v},h^{4}\tilde{v}]
and therefore of the axis of
h
.
Similarly, if
h
has translation length 4 or more we can take
h^{2}
and pass to
e_{1}
-polarized normal form

h^{2}=e_{1}e_{2}\bar{f}_{2}e_{3}e_{4}c_{e_{4}}^{d}\bar{f}_{4}e_{5}e_{6}c_{e_{6}}^{d_{6}}\bar{f}_{6}\cdots,

and again it is apparent that the first 6 edges of the path
[\tilde{v},h^{4}\tilde{v}]
and therefore of the axis of
h
are determined by the exponent
d
.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
be a hyperbolic element whose syllable length coincides with its translation length. Then there is a unique element of
\mathbb{X}_{v}\leq\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
that conjugates
g
to centred short position.

Proof.

In
e_{1}
-polarized normal form we have
g=c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}e_{2}{c_{e_{2}}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{2}\cdots
conjugating by
f_{1}\in\mathbb{X}_{V}
and then by the element of
a\in O_{e_{1}}\leq\mathbb{X}_{v}
such that
ae_{1}e_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}{c_{e_{2}}}^{-d_{2}}
gives us a normal form

g_{1}=(a^{-1}\bar{f}_{1}^{-1}c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-d_{0}})g(c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}a)=e_{1}e_{2}\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}\cdots

whose ”
f_{1}
-syllable” and
"d_{2}"
-exponent are trivial. We further note that any conjugation by an element of
\mathbb{X}_{v}\setminus O_{e_{1}}
will undo this condition.

Now there is a subgroup
Z_{e_{1}}\leq O_{e_{1}}
such that
ze_{1}e_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}=\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}z^{\prime}
, which is either trivial or cyclic, that can affect the exponent
d
given in the definition of centred short position while keeping the exponent
d_{2}=0
. I.e.
Z_{e_{1}}
is a group of elements that fix an arc of length 4 in the Bass-Serre tree.

Let
k\in Z_{e_{1}}
be the conjugator that gives the minimal exponent
d
. We have that
p=kc_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}a
is a conjugator that brings
g
to centred short position. Note that the initial conjugator
c_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}a
is well defined up to left multiplication by some element
z\in Z_{e_{1}}
. It follows that in all cases, the double coset representative
\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}
is common to all possible centred short positions.

To see that it is unique, suppose there was another
p^{\prime}
such that
(p^{\prime})^{-1}gp^{\prime}
was in centred short position. Then

p^{-1}p^{\prime}(e_{1}e_{2}\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}*\cdots)(p^{\prime})^{-1}p^{-1}=e_{1}e_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}*^{\prime}\cdots,

where, both
h=e_{1}e_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}*
and
h^{\prime}e_{1}e_{2}\bar{f}_{2}^{\prime}*^{\prime}
are in centred short position with the same minimal exponent
d
. Now note that
\mathrm{axis}(h^{\prime})=(p^{-1}p^{\prime})\cdot\mathrm{axis}(h)
and so by Lemma
5.4

(p^{-1}p^{\prime})
fixes a segment of length 6, which by 4-acylindricity implies that
p^{\prime}=p
. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally we say that a hyperbolic
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is in
short position
if

g=Eg_{v}\bar{E}

where
E
is a path in
\tau
from
b
to
v
and
g_{v}
is in centred short position. We call the vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
the
anchor vertex of the short position
and the first edge
e_{1}
of
g_{v}
the
polarizing edge of the short position
. Thus, given some
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, by going through the
{\left\langle g\right\rangle}
-orbits of the white vertices of
\mathrm{axis}(g)
and applying the corollary above gives:

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
G=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
be a one-ended subgroup of a piecewise trivial suspension. Let
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
be hyperbolic with translation length
n
then its conjugacy class contains at most
n/2
elements in short position and the normal forms, anchor vertices and polarizing edges, of these elements can be computed.

5.2
Tuples of cosets and linkage configurations in piecewise trivial suspensions

We now return our focus to a globally fibered piecewise trivial suspension
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}=F\rtimes_{\alpha}{\left\langle t\right\rangle}=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
. In the notation of subsection
5.1.1
we will assume every white vertex group is decomposed as
\mathbb{X}_{v}=F_{v}\oplus{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
where
F_{v}=F\cap\mathbb{X}_{v}
(which is well-defined since
F\triangleleft{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}})
), thus elements in the fibre will always be smaller with respect to
<
than other elements. In particular, if we write

g=a_{1}\bar{f}_{1}b_{1}e_{1}e_{2}a_{3}\bar{f}_{3}b_{3}\cdots e_{n}a_{n+1}\bar{f}_{n+1}b_{n+1}

where
\bar{f}_{i}
is the
i
th entry in
DCR(g,e)
for some
e
with
i(e)=b
, then the representation of this form that minimizes the tuple
(a_{1},b_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1},b_{n+1})
is precisely the normal form polarized by
e
. When it is not necessary to see the exponents in the power vector, we will call an expression

g=f_{1}e_{1}e_{2}f_{3}\cdots e_{n}f_{n+1}t^{k}

in
reduced fiber and abscissa form
provided
g_{f}=f_{1}e_{1}e_{2}f_{3}\cdots e_{n}f_{n+1}\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)\cap F
is a reduced word. This initial subword
g_{f}
is called the
fibre part
and is well-defined by our normal form conventions. We record some more observations.

Lemma 5.7
.

The fibre part
g_{f}
of
g\in{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
is well-defined. Furthermore if
g,g^{\prime}\in{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
and
g_{f}=g^{\prime}_{f}
then

DC(g,e)=DC(g^{\prime},e)\textrm{~and~}pow(g,e)=pow(g^{\prime},e)

for all
e
with
i(e)=v
.

We wish to describe how automorphisms act on the double cosets that appear in the sequences
DC(g,e)
. Even in a free group, we shouldn’t expect automorphisms to act in any sensible way on a double coset space. That said, it will turn out that the constraints of the Bass diagram will lead to a sensible action on the double coset spaces we are interested in.

We will also develop an algorithmic theory to compute orbits of our double cosets. Our approach is to reduce the double coset orbit problems to something related to orbit problems of finitely generated subgroups of free groups (see Theorem
5.25
.) This next subsection establishes the dictionary between double cosets and subgroups of free groups.

5.2.1
Linkages of double cosets

Turns were defined in Subsection
5.1.1
. We now define the entrance of a turn and the linkage of a double coset at a given turn. Before continuing we will make a slight modification to our notation.

Convention 5.8

(Double cosets for elements of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
)
.

For the rest of Section
5
we will assume that the double cosets that appear in
DC(g,e)
are actually double cosets in the fibre part
F_{v}=\mathbb{X}_{v}\cap F
. In particular, if we write
I_{e}\bar{f}O_{e^{\prime}}
then
I_{e}={\left\langle c_{e}\right\rangle}
and
O_{e^{\prime}}={\left\langle c_{\bar{e}^{\prime}}\right\rangle}
, where
c_{e},c_{\bar{e}^{\prime}}\in F_{v}
are, as usual, generators of the images of the corresponding incident edge groups.

We note that by Lemma
5.7
and the fact that our ordering convention forces double coset representatives to lie in
F_{v}
considering double cosets to lie in the fibre part of the vertex groups causes no loss of information.

Whenever
f\in F_{v}
, and
(e,e^{\prime})
is a turn at
v
, the entrance of the turn is the element
c_{e}
(it generates
I_{e}<\mathbb{X}_{v}
). We define the linkage
{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
and the dual linkage
(\mathscr{l}_{*})_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
to be the following subgroups of
\mathbb{F}_{v}
:

\begin{array}[]{cclcl}{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}&=&\langle I_{e},fO_{e^{\prime}}f^{-1}\rangle&&\\
(\mathscr{l}_{*})_{e,f,e^{\prime}}&=&\mathscr{l}_{\bar{e}^{\prime},f^{-1},\bar{e}}&=&\langle O_{e^{\prime}},f^{-1}I_{e}f\rangle\end{array}

If the double coset appears in the reduced form of an element
g
in the fibre, the dual linkage is the linkage of a coset appearing in the reduced form of
g^{-1}
.

Double cosets determine linkages, as stated in the following Lemma, which is immediate.

Lemma 5.9
.

If
f
and
f^{\prime}
are in the same double coset

I_{e}xO_{e^{\prime}}
then
{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}={\mathscr{l}}_{e,f^{\prime},e^{\prime}}
.

In particular, if
f
is in the fibre of
G
, all of the reduced words representing it share the same sequence of edges, of entrances, and of linkages.

However, in principle, different elements
f
can lead to the same linkage while being in different double cosets.
It is thus worth warning that a given linkage can have different duals if it can be defined by elements in different double cosets.

We propose a converse to Lemma
5.9
, in which double cosets are characterized by a pair of linkages that are dual to one another.

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider a pair of linkages
\mathscr{l}=\mathscr{l}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}=\mathscr{l}_{e,h,e^{\prime}}
and a dual pair
\mathscr{l}_{*}=\mathscr{l}_{\bar{e}^{\prime},f^{-1},\bar{e}}
, and
\mathscr{l}_{*}^{\prime}=\mathscr{l}_{\bar{e}^{\prime},h^{-1},\bar{e}}
.

If one has equality of pairs

(\mathscr{l},\mathscr{l}_{*})=(\mathscr{l}^{\prime},\mathscr{l}_{*}^{\prime})

then the double cosets
I_{e}fO_{e^{\prime}}
and
I_{e}hO_{e^{\prime}}
are equal.

Proof.

The result will then follow from this statement.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\tau(e_{-})=i(e_{+})=v
and, to simplify notation, let
{\left\langle c_{e_{-}}\right\rangle}=I_{e_{-}}
and
{\left\langle c_{e_{+}}\right\rangle}=O_{e_{+}}.

If
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{v}
are such that

\langle c_{e_{-}},\,h^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime-1}\rangle\,=\,\langle c_{e_{-}},\,hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\rangle,\;\hbox{ and }\;\langle c_{e_{+}},\,h^{\prime-1}c_{e_{-}}h^{\prime}\rangle\,=\,\langle c_{e_{+}},\,h^{-1}c_{e_{-}}h\rangle

then
h\in\langle c_{e_{-}}\rangle h^{\prime}\langle c_{e_{+}}\rangle
.

Proof.

Start with the equality
\langle c_{e_{-}},\,h^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime-1}\rangle\;=\;\langle c_{e_{-}},\,hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\rangle
.

These two groups are subgroups of
\mathbb{F}_{v_{i}}
, hence free.

If they have rank one, then by maximality of the edge groups,
h^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime-1}=c_{e_{-}}^{\pm 1}=(hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1})^{\pm 1}
. It follows that
h^{-1}h^{\prime}
centralizes
c_{e_{+}}
(it cannot conjugate it to its inverse). Therefore it is in
O_{e_{+}}
.

If they are rank two, one has that

hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\in I_{e_{-}}\,h^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime-1}\,I_{e_{-}}.

Let us write
hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\in I_{e_{-}}\,h^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime-1}\,c_{e_{-}}^{-r}
. Let
h^{\prime\prime}=c_{e_{-}}^{r}h^{\prime}
. We have that
hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\in I_{e_{-}}\,h^{\prime\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime\prime-1}
and also that
\langle c_{e_{-}},\,h^{\prime\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime\prime-1}\rangle\;=\;\langle c_{e_{-}},\,hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}\rangle
, and we still want to show that
h\in I_{e_{-}}h^{\prime\prime}O_{e_{+}}
.

Let us write that, for some integer
k
,
hc_{e_{+}}h^{-1}=c_{e_{-}}^{k}\,h^{\prime\prime}c_{e_{+}}h^{\prime\prime-1}
:

c_{e_{-}}^{k}=hc_{e_{+}}\;h^{-1}h^{\prime\prime}\;c_{e_{+}}^{-1}\;h^{\prime\prime-1},

c_{e_{-}}^{k}=([h^{\prime\prime-1}h,\;c_{e_{+}}])^{h^{\prime\prime-1}}.

In a free group, a non-trivial commutator is not a proper power

[
Sch59
]
,
[
Bau60
, Lemma 36.4]
,
[
DH91
]
,
hence
|k|\leq 1
.

If
k=0
, then
h^{\prime\prime-1}h
commutes with
c_{e_{+}}
hence is in
O_{e_{+}}
, which is what we wanted to show.

If
|k|=1
, then
c_{e_{-}}
is conjugate to a commutator of an certain element with
c_{e_{+}}
. This is a property of the turn
(e_{-},e_{+})
and in this case we say that
(e_{-},e_{+})
is a
dangerous turn.

We claim that in this case,
(\bar{e}_{+},\bar{e}_{-})
(which is also a turn at the same vertex) is not dangerous. Indeed, if it was dangerous too, it would mean that
c_{e_{+}}
is conjugate to a commutator of an element and
c_{e_{-}}
too. To get the contradiction, it suffices to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.12
.

In a free group, if
a
is conjugate to
[b,c]
and
b
is conjugate to
[a,d]
, then
a=b=1
.

Proof.

Iwasawa and Magnus proved that free groups are residually nilpotent, meaning that any element survives in a nilpotent quotient (see
[
Rob95
, §6.1.9, §6.1.10]
.
Any such a quotient satisfies the law that all iterated commutators
[[\dots[\cdot,\cdot],\cdot],\cdot\dots]
of certain depth must vanish. However,
a
and
b
can be written as commutators of arbitrary depth, by iterative substitution.
∎

Now that we know that the turn
(\bar{e}_{+},\bar{e}_{-})
is not dangerous, we may apply our analysis above on the dual configuration to get that
h^{-1}\in O_{e_{+}}(h^{\prime}_{i})^{-1}I_{e_{-}}
, which is equivalent to what we wanted to show.
∎

∎

5.2.2
The linkage configuration at a vertex induced by a path and a sequence of double cosets

Given a loop
(e_{1},\dots e_{n})
based at
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
and a sequence of double cosets over this loop

(I_{\bar{e}_{0}}f_{1}O_{e_{1}},1,I_{e_{2}}f_{3}O_{e_{3}},\dots,1,I_{e_{n}}f_{n+1}O_{e_{0}}),

where
e_{0}
is some polarizing edge, we can construct the associated linkage tuple

\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}=({\mathscr{l}}_{1},\dots{\mathscr{l}}_{n+1})=({\mathscr{l}}_{\bar{e}_{0},f_{1},e_{1}},1,\ldots,{\mathscr{l}}_{e_{n},f_{n+1},e_{0}}).

We reverse this path to get
\bar{e}_{n},\ldots,\bar{e}_{1}
and the corresponding
dual coset tuple

(O_{e_{0}}f_{n+1}^{-1}I_{e_{n}},1,\ldots,O_{e_{3}}f_{3}^{-1}I_{e_{2}},1,O_{e_{1}}f_{1}^{-1}I_{\bar{e}_{0}})=\\
(I_{\bar{e}_{0}}f_{n+1}^{-1}O_{\bar{e}_{n}},1,\ldots,I_{\bar{e}_{3}}f_{3}^{-1}O_{\bar{e}_{2}},1,I_{\bar{e}_{1}}f_{1}^{-1}O_{e_{0}})

and the corresponding dual linkage tuple that works out to

\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}=(({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{n+1},\ldots,({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{1}),

i.e. reading
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}
and
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}_{*}}
in opposite directions we get pairs of dual linkages of the double cosets
I_{e_{j-1}}f_{j}O_{e_{j}}.
We note that if a sequence of double cosets came from
DC(g,e)
then the dual sequence would come from
DC(g^{-1},e).
By Lemma
5.9
and Proposition
5.10
, given the underlying loop and polarizing edge, the dual pair
(\underline{{\mathscr{l}}},\underline{{\mathscr{l}}_{*}})
fully determines the tuple of double cosets and vice-versa.

Consider the two pairs

(\bar{e}_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{n},e_{n+1}),({\mathscr{l}}_{1},\dots{\mathscr{l}}_{n+1})\textrm{~and~}(\bar{e}_{n+1},\bar{e}_{n},\ldots,\bar{e}_{1},e_{0}),(({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{n+1},\dots({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{1})

with common indices
1\leq i\leq n+1
, where
e_{0}
is the polarizing edge and
e_{n+1}=e_{0}
. Consider
v
a vertex in
X
, and let
J_{v}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
be the set of indices
j\leq n
such that
\tau(e_{j})=v
, and
J^{*}_{v}
the set of indices for which
\tau(\bar{e}_{j})=v
. For each oriented edge
e
in
X
,
J_{v,e}
is the subset of
J_{v}
for which
e_{j}=e
and, similarly,
{J^{*}}_{v,e}=\{j\in{J^{*}}_{v}:\bar{e}_{j}=e\}
.

For each
J_{v,e}
(possibly empty), one associates the following
(|J_{v,e}|)
-tuple
\mathcal{P}_{v,e}=({\mathscr{l}}_{{j+1}}:j\in J_{v,e})
, where the
{\mathscr{l}}_{j+i}
are entries from
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}
. We note that
\mathcal{P}_{v,e}
consists of entries from
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}
of the form
{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
, where
f,e^{\prime}
are arbitrary. Similarly, we define
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{v,e}=(({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{j}:j\in J^{*}_{v,e})
, where the
({\mathscr{l}}_{*})_{j+i}
are entries from
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}_{*}}
. We note that entries of
\mathcal{P}^{*}_{v,e}
consist of linkages of the form
{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
where
f,e^{\prime}
are arbitrary.

The triple
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(g,e_{0})=(c_{e},\mathcal{P}_{v,e},\mathcal{P}^{*}_{v,e})
is the
linkage configuration of the
e_{0}
-polarized element
g
at
v
for
e
. The following will be useful to remember in the proof of Proposition
5.10
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Every entry
{\mathscr{l}}_{e,f,e^{\prime}}
in
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}
and
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}_{*}}
that is associated to a turn
(e,e^{\prime})
at
v
is in
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}
.

Note that the order of appearance of each linkage is part of the data of
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(g,e_{0})
, i.e.
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(g,e_{0})
is not just a set of linkages.

Ordering the edges of
X
, one thus obtains, from a linkage tuple, and for each
v
, a tuple
\mathcal{Q}_{v}(g,e)
of conjugacy classes of linkage configurations at
v
:
\mathcal{Q}_{v}(g,e_{0})
is the tuple of the conjugacy classes of the tuple
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(g,e_{0})
,
e
ranging over the edges of
X
terminating at
v
, in the chosen order. We call it the
total linkage configuration
at
v
for the given sequence of double cosets.

5.3
Tuples of tuples and associated data

5.3.1
Elliptic, lineal and hyperbolic tuples, and their enrichments

We consider a tuple of
r-2
elements
S=(g_{2},\dots,g_{r})
of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
(we start at index
2
on purpose).

The tuple is said elliptic if there exists
\tilde{v}\in T
that is fixed by all
g_{i}
. It is said small elliptic if
\tilde{v}\in\tilde{\tau}
. Define its enriched tuple
S^{+}=(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{r})
by setting
g_{0}=g_{1}=g_{2}
.

If
S
is not elliptic, either there exists
i
a smallest index such that
g_{i}
is not elliptic, or all
g_{i}
are elliptic and there exists
(i,j)
a smallest pair in lexicographical order so that
g_{i}
and
g_{j}
do not fix the same vertex (there exists such a pair because pairwise intersecting convex sets in trees have global intersection). Let
g_{0}=g_{i}
in the first case and
g_{0}=g_{i}g_{j}
in the second case. It is a hyperbolic element.

Again there are two cases: either all elements of
S
commute with
g_{0}
or one of them does not.

In the first case, we say that the tuple is lineal and we set its enriched tuple
S^{+}=(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{r})
by setting
g_{1}=g_{0}
.

In the second case, we say that the tuple is hyperbolic, and we set its enriched tuple
S^{+}=(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{r})
by setting
g_{1}=g_{0}^{g_{i}}
where
i
is the first index such that
g_{i}
does not commute with
g_{0}
.

5.3.2
Small tuples

An enriched tuple
(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{r})
is
small
if:

•

if it is elliptic: the elements have a common fixed point is in
\tilde{\tau}
.

•

if it is lineal:
g_{0}
is cyclically reduced in short position.

•

if it is hyperbolic:
g_{0}
is cyclically reduced in short position, and
g_{1}
has minimal syllable length among its conjugates by powers of
g_{0}
.

In the first case, there exists a simple path
(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k})
in
\tau
starting at
b_{0}
, such that for each
i
, there exists
h_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{\tau(e_{k})}
for which

g_{i}\,=\,(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k})h_{i}(\bar{e}_{k}\bar{e}_{k-1}\dots\bar{e}_{0}).

In the second case,
g_{0}
(hence the tuple) preserves a line
L
in
T
that intersects
\tau
. Let
\tilde{v}_{0}
in this line closest to
\tilde{b}
, and
v_{0}
its image in
X
.
The element
g_{0}
can thus be expressed as
(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k})h_{0}(\bar{e}_{k}\bar{e}_{k-1}\dots\bar{e}_{0})
for
(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k})
a simple path in
\tau
from
b
to
v_{0}
, and
h\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})
is in centred short position and
v_{0}
is the anchor vertex of the short position.

From Proposition
5.6
, we easily obtain:

Proposition 5.14
.

Given a tuple, it is decidable whether it is an elliptic, lineal or hyperbolic tuple, and a small conjugate of the enriched tuple is computable. If it is not elliptic a finite list of all small conjugates of the enriched tuple is computable.

5.3.3
Centred non-elliptic tuples

Given a small tuple
S
of elements in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
, we define its
central vertex

v_{S}\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
as follows. If the leading entry
g_{0}
is elliptic then every entry in
S
is conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
into some
\mathbb{X}_{v_{T}}
, so
v_{T}
is the central vertex. Otherwise, if
S
is lineal or hyperbolic, recalling the terminology of Subsection
5.1.2
, we take
v_{S}
to be the anchor of the short position of the leading entry
g_{0}
. If
S
is lineal or hyperbolic we further define the
polarizing edge

e_{S}\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
to be the polarizing edge of the short position of the leading entry
g_{0}
.

Now given a small tuple
S
of elements in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
we can conjugate it in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
be the element
E
given by the path in the spanning tree
\tau
from
b
to
v_{S}
such that
g_{0}=E(g_{0})_{v_{S}}\bar{E}
, where
(g_{0})_{v_{S}}
is in centred short position.

It follows that the conjugate
\bar{E}SE
is now a tuple of elements in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{S})
, its leading entry is in centred short position, and
e_{S}
can be read off as the first symbol occurring in the leading entry. We call the conjugate of
\bar{E}SE
the
the re-centring
S
and we say that
\bar{E}SE
a
centred tuple
. Proposition
5.14
immediately implies that for any tuple
S
we can compute the complete set of all its centred tuples in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
.

The following result will have the immediate benefit of providing reassurance that taking conjugates outside
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},b)
is sensible.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
g,h\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
.
g
and
h
are conjugate in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
if and only if they are conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proof.

On the one hand we have that
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)\leq\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
, on the other hand, for a spanning subtree
\tau
of
X
, we have a commuting diagram

\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})/_{{\left\langle{\left\langle{{\textbf{E}(\tau)}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle}}
\leq
\sim

where the horizontal arrow is an isomorphism. If two elements in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
are conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
then the image of the conjugator
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})/_{{\left\langle{\left\langle{{\textbf{E}(\tau)}}\right\rangle}\right\rangle}}
gives a conjugator in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
.

∎

5.3.4
Centred elliptic tuples and variations

If
S=(g_{0},\ldots,g_{r})
is an elliptic tuple then its
central vertex

v_{s}\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
is the vertex such all the
g_{i}
are simultaneously conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
into
\mathbb{X}_{v_{s}}
. We say
S
is centred if its entries are in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{s}}
. If
S
is centred and all its entries are simultaneously conjugate into the image of the same edge group, then we will require the
centring
of
S
to be a conjugate in which all entries lie in the image of some common edge group and we call the
variations
of a centred
S^{\prime}
to be all the tuples
(g^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,g^{\prime}_{r})
such that

g_{i}^{\prime}=\ell g_{i}\bar{\ell}\in\mathbb{X}_{v_{s}}

where
\ell
is a loop based at
v_{s}
of length at most 4. (See Lemma
4.11
.)

We define the
linkage configuration of
S
to be the tuple

\mathcal{Q}(S)=({\left\langle(g_{0})_{f}\right\rangle},\ldots,{\left\langle(g_{r})_{f}\right\rangle})\in F\cap\mathbb{X}_{v_{s}}

of cyclic subgroups of free groups generated by fibre parts.

Proposition 5.16
.

Given an elliptic tuple, it is possible to compute its centring and all its variations.

5.4
The actions of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}

The mixed Whitehead problem involves the action of
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left((\right)}\mathbb{X})
as well as the action of
({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}})^{k}
on a tuple of tuples. We start by studying the action of
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
on centred tuples.

5.4.1
Preliminaries

Recall that
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
surjects on
{\mathrm{Out_{fo}}\left({{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\right)}
and that by Corollary
3.19
we can compute a complete list
\nu=\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{v}\}
of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
-coset representatives. If for each
j\in\{1,\dots,v\}
, we define
\underline{S}_{j}=\nu_{j}(\underline{S})=(\nu_{j}(S_{1}),\ldots,\nu_{j}(S_{k}))
, then we immediately get a first reduction, before diving into the main argument:

Lemma 5.17
.

\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are mixed Whitehead equivalent under
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\right)}
if and only if there is

j\in\{1,\dots,v\}

such that
\underline{S}_{j}
and
\underline{T}
are Whitehead equivalent under
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
. In particular, the mixed Whitehead problem under
{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left({{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}}\right)}
reduces to the mixed Whitehead problem under
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.

Observe that
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
preserves the paths (hence the syllable lengths), and the abscissa. Note that, according to our convention, these paths are to be understood as paths in
X
, not in
T
.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
\alpha\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
is seen as a homomorphism of the Bass group, then it preserves the
X
-paths and abscissa of elements in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
for all
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
. If
w
is reduced, then
\alpha(w)
is reduced and of the same syllable length. If
g
is cyclically reduced, then
\alpha(g)
is cyclically reduced.

Proof.

Any fibre and orientation-preserving automorphism induces the identity on the cyclic quotient of the suspension, hence preserves the abscissa.

Being in
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
,
\alpha
is of the form
\alpha=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
. It sends each edge
e_{i}
to
\gamma_{\overline{e_{i}}}^{-1}e_{i}\gamma_{e_{i}}
in the Bass group. In particular, it preserves the paths.

Also, it sends the word
g_{1}e_{1}g_{2}\cdots e_{n}g_{n+1}
to
h_{1}e_{1}h_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{n}h_{n+1}
where
h_{i}=\gamma_{e_{i-1}}\phi_{v_{i}}(g_{i})\gamma_{\overline{e_{i}}}^{-1}
, if
1<i<n+1
.
In particular, it is in the same vertex groups as
g_{i}
.
If the word
h_{1}e_{1}h_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{n}h_{n+1}
fails to be reduced, there exists
i
in
\{2,\dots,n-1\}
such that
e_{i+1}=\bar{e}_{i}
, and
h_{i}\in\tau_{e_{i}}(\mathbb{X}_{e_{i}})<\mathbb{X}_{v_{i+1}}
. The Bass diagram implies that
g_{1}e_{1}g_{2}\cdots e_{n}g_{n+1}
is not reduced either.

If
g
is cyclically reduced, consider
\alpha(g)
, and assume by contradiction that it is not cyclically reduced. There is a shorter conjugate, and its image by
\alpha^{-1}
(also in
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
, inducing an isometry of the Bass-Serre tree),
is a conjugate of
g
that is represented by a word that is reduced by the previous argument, and shorter: a contradiction.
∎

5.4.2
Action on linkages, and on double cosets

Convention 5.19
.

For the remainder of this section, we assume any tuple
T
of elements is centred and its elements lie in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{T})
where
v_{T}
is the central vertex of the centred tuple. The leading entry of
T
is in centred short position and the first edge in its prefix is
e_{T}
. We will always use
e_{T}
-polarized normal forms to work with the element of
T.

Let us compute the action of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
on the linkage tuples. Observe that it is equivalent to consider
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
. First of all, we will want to fix an edge
e
and consider
e
-polarized normal forms. Obviously, this only makes sense for elements of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
with
i(e)=v
. Given
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},i(e))
and
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
an element of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
we define the
e
-polarized action of
\phi
to be

\phi\cdot_{e}g=({\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\phi)(g),

where for this section
{\mathrm{ad}_{x}}(y)=x^{-1}yx
. Note that
({\mathrm{ad}_{x}}\circ{\mathrm{ad}_{y}})(z)={\mathrm{ad}_{x}}(y^{-1}zy)=x^{-1}y^{-1}zyx={\mathrm{ad}_{yx}}(z)
, which due to the fact that our conjugation convention gives a right action but functions act on the left.

Lemma 5.20
.

The
e-
polarized action is a well-defined group action.

Proof.

Let
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\alpha_{e}))
,
\psi=(Id_{X},(\psi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\beta_{e}))
and let
\theta=\psi\circ\phi=(Id_{X},(\psi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
. On the one hand, we have

\psi(\phi(e))=\psi(\alpha_{\bar{e}}^{-1})\beta_{\bar{e}}^{-1}e\beta_{e}\psi(\alpha_{e})\Rightarrow\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}=\psi(\alpha_{\bar{e}}^{-1})\beta_{\bar{e}}^{-1}.

(See also the Bass diagram (
3
).) On the other hand, we have

({\mathrm{ad}_{\beta_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\psi)\circ({\mathrm{ad}_{\alpha_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\phi)={\mathrm{ad}_{\beta_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ{\mathrm{ad}_{\psi(\alpha_{\bar{e}}^{-1})}}\circ\psi\circ\phi={\mathrm{ad}_{\psi(\alpha_{\bar{e}}^{-1})\beta_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\psi\circ\phi.

It follows that

(\psi\circ\phi)\cdot_{e}(g)=({\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\psi\circ\phi)(g)=\psi\cdot_{e}(\phi\cdot_{e}g),

as required.
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
, and let its reduced fiber form be

g=\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}\bar{f}_{2}\cdots e_{n}f_{n+1}t^{k},

where
t
is our oriented generator of the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{v}
and write
v_{i}=\tau(e_{i-1})=i(e_{i})
, so that
f_{i}\in\mathbb{X}_{v_{i}}\cap F
.
Let
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
an element of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
. Let
e
be such that
i(e)=v
.
The image of
\phi\cdot_{e}g
has the same
X
-path and the same sequence of turns as
g
.

The linkage tuple of
\phi\cdot_{e}g
with respect to the
e
-polarization has
i
-th coordinate that is equal to
(\phi_{v_{i}}(\langle I_{e_{i-1}},f_{i}O_{e_{i}}f_{i}^{-1}\rangle))^{\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}}
, which is
\phi_{v_{i}}(\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}(g)_{i})^{\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}}
.

Proof.

First note that
\phi(t)=t
since
\phi
is orientation preserving and
t
is a generator of the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{b}
. The equality of the paths is immediate, given that
\phi
induces
Id_{X}
, hence the sequence of turns is the same too.

We write

\phi\cdot_{e}g=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(g)\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi_{v_{1}}(f_{1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1}\cdot e_{1}\cdot\gamma_{e_{1}}\,\phi_{v_{2}}(f_{2})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{2}}^{-1}\cdot e_{2}\cdots\\
e_{n}\cdot\gamma_{e_{n}}\cdot\phi_{v_{n+1}}(f_{n+1})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}\,t^{\pm k}

(13)

Considering the sequence
DC(\phi\cdot_{e}g,e)
we see that we can express
\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}(\phi(g))_{i}
as

\underline{{\mathscr{l}}}(\phi(g))_{i}=\langle I_{e_{i-1}},\gamma_{e_{i-1}}\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}^{-1}\,\cdot\,O_{e_{i}}\,\cdot\,\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})^{-1}\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}\rangle,

for
1\leq i\leq n+1
and where, for the sake of defining cosets in the context of
e
-polarization, we define
e_{0}=\bar{e}
and
e_{n+1}=e
. The Bass diagram (for
e_{i-1}
and
\bar{e}_{i}
) ensures:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}^{-1}O_{e_{i}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}&=&\phi_{v_{i}}(O_{e_{i}})&(\hbox{image of }O_{e_{i}})\\
I_{e_{i-1}}&=&\gamma_{e_{i-1}}\phi_{v_{i}}(I_{e_{i-1}})\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}&(\hbox{image of }I_{e_{i-1}}).\end{array}

(14)

It follows that

\langle I_{e_{i-1}},\gamma_{e_{i-1}}\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}^{-1}O_{e_{i}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i}}\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})^{-1}\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}\rangle=\\
\langle\phi_{v_{i}}(I_{e_{i-1}}),\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})\phi_{v_{i}}(O_{e_{i}})\phi_{v_{i}}(f_{i})^{-1}\rangle^{\gamma_{e_{i-1}}^{-1}},

which is what we wanted.

∎

For all vertex
v
and edges
e_{-},e_{+}
such that
\tau(e_{-})=i(e_{+})=v
, we consider the following action of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
on the set
I_{e_{-}}\backslash\mathbb{F}_{v}/O_{e_{+}}
of double cosets
I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
in
\mathbb{F}_{v}
.

Convention 5.22
.

Let
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
, let
w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
be a white vertex, and let
e_{-},e_{+}
edges such that
\tau(e_{-})=i(e_{+})=w
. Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{v}
. and consider the set of double cosets
I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
in
\mathbb{F}_{v}
. We set

\phi\cdot(I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}})\;=\;I_{e_{-}}\,\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}\,O_{e_{+}}.

One easily checks from the composition formula in Section
2.3
that this defines an action of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
on
I_{e_{-}}\backslash\mathbb{F}_{v}/O_{e_{+}}
. By Lemma
5.21
, it is the natural action of the thus obtained automorphisms of
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
on the double cosets defined by polarized reduced forms:

Proposition 5.23
.

Consider
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)
which, in
e
-polarized normal form, is written
g=f_{1}e_{1}f_{2}\cdots e_{n}f_{n+1}t^{k}
, and
\phi\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
. For all
j
, denote
v_{j}=i(e_{j})
. Then for all
i
, there exists
f^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{v_{i}}
such that

\phi\cdot_{e}g=({\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}}}\circ\phi(g))=f^{\prime}_{1}e_{1}f^{\prime}_{2}e_{2}\cdots e_{n}f^{\prime}_{n+1}t^{k},

and the double coset
I_{e_{i-1}}f^{\prime}_{i}O_{e_{i}}
is
\phi\cdot(I_{e_{i-1}}f_{i}O_{e_{i}})
. Equivalently if
DC(g,e)=(I_{\bar{e}}f_{1}O_{e_{1}},1,\ldots,1,I_{e_{n}}f_{n+2}O_{\bar{e}})
then

\phi\cdot DC(g,e)=DC(\phi\cdot_{e}g,e)=\left(\phi\cdot(I_{\bar{e}}f_{1}O_{e_{1}}),1,\ldots,1,\phi\cdot(I_{e_{n}}f_{n+2}O_{e})\right).

Proof.

The expression of
\phi\cdot_{e}g
has already been computed in (
13
) in the proof Lemma
5.21
, where setting
e_{0}=\bar{e}
and
e_{n+1}=e
we have

\phi\cdot e(g)=\gamma_{e_{0}}\phi_{v_{1}}(f_{1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1}\cdot e_{1}\cdot\gamma_{e_{1}}\phi_{v_{2}}(f_{2})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{2}}^{-1}\cdot e_{2}\cdots e_{n}\cdot\gamma_{e_{n}} \phi_{v_{n+1}}(f_{n+1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{n+1}}^{-1}\;t^{\pm k}.

We further note that in the word above the factors
(\gamma_{e_{2i-1}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{2i}}^{-1}),i=1,\ldots,n/2
that appear in the abelian vertex groups can be migrated through either
e_{2i-1}
or
e_{2i}
without affecting the double coset. The proposition now follows.
∎

5.4.3
The action of automorphisms on sequences of double cosets, and a linkage configuration

Proposition 5.24
.

Consider two double coset sequences
DC(g,e_{0})
and
DC(g^{\prime},e_{0})^{\prime}
over the same
X
-path with polarizing edge
e_{0}
. Consider the linkage configurations induced for each vertex and oriented edge in
X
by these sequences,
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}=\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(g,e_{0})
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{v,e}(g^{\prime},e_{0})
.

There exists an automorphism
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),\gamma_{e})\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
that sends
DC(g,e)
to
DC(g^{\prime},e)^{\prime}
(for the action defined by Convention
5.22
and as in Proposition
5.23
) if, and only if, for each
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
and each
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
, there exists a
\phi_{v}
and
\gamma_{e}
, such that
\phi_{v}(\mathcal{Q}_{v,e})=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}_{v,e})^{\gamma_{e}}
.

Proof.

Assume
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
sends
DC(g,e)
to
DC(g^{\prime},e)
.
Consider a linkage
\mathscr{l}
that is associated, or dually associated, to a double coset
I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
in
DC(g,e)
, and
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}
the corresponding linkage for the image
I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}
by
\phi
of this double coset.

Write
\mathscr{l}=\langle c_{e_{-}},fc_{e_{+}}f^{-1}\rangle
, and
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}=\langle c_{e_{-}},f^{\prime}c_{e_{+}}f^{\prime-1}\rangle
. We want to show that
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l})
is conjugate to
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}
by an element that only depends on
\phi
and
e_{-}
.

We know (by Proposition
5.23
) that
\phi\cdot I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}=I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}
, but it is also, by definition of the action
I_{e_{-}}\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}O_{e_{+}}
.

It follows that
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}=\langle c_{e_{-}},\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}c_{e_{+}}(\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1})^{-1}\rangle
.

By the Bass diagrams (as recorded in (
14
) in the proof of Lemma
5.21
), we also know that
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l})=\langle c_{e_{-}}^{\gamma_{e_{-}}},\phi_{v}(f)c_{e_{+}}^{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}}\phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\rangle
.

This means that
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l})=\langle c_{e_{-}},\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)c_{e_{+}}^{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}}\phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\gamma_{e_{-}}^{-1}\rangle^{\gamma_{e_{-}}}
, which is a conjugate of

\mathscr{l}^{\prime}
by
\gamma_{e_{-}}
, as required.

We now show the converse. Suppose we are given automorphisms
\phi_{v}
for each vertex of
X
and an element
\gamma_{e}
for each edge of
X
. By hypothesis,

\phi_{v}(\mathcal{Q}_{v,e})=(\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}^{\prime})^{\gamma_{e}}
.
Observe the first coordinate of
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}^{\prime}
: one has
\phi_{v}(c_{e})=c_{e}^{\gamma_{e}}
. This ensures that the tuple
(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))
satisfies the Bass diagrams for all edges. This therefore defines an automorphism
(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),Id_{e},\gamma_{e})\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.

Consider the
j
-th double coset in the tuple
DC(g,e)
, and denote it
I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
. and denote by
I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}
the
j
-th double coset of
DC(g^{\prime},e)
(observe that it must be a double coset for the same pair of groups because the underlying path is the same).

We want to show that
\phi\cdot I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
is equal to
I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}
. Recall that by definition
\phi\cdot I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}=I_{e_{-}}\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}O_{e_{+}}
. So we immediately rephrase our goal as to show equality between

I_{e_{-}}\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}O_{e_{+}}\;\hbox{ and }\;I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}.

The double coset
I_{e_{-}}fO_{e_{+}}
defines the two linkages
\mathscr{l}_{j}
and
(\mathscr{l}_{*})_{j}
, which, with the notations of Section
5.2.2
are respectively part of
the linkage configurations
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e_{-}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{v,\bar{e}_{+}}
(see Lemma
5.13
.)

Let
\mathscr{l}^{\prime}_{j},({\mathscr{l}^{\prime}}^{*})_{j}
be the (mutually dual) linkages of
I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}
for the corresponding index for
DC(g^{\prime},e)
.

By assumption on the preservation of linkage configurations,
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l}_{j})=(\mathscr{l}^{\prime}_{j})^{\gamma_{e_{-}}}
and
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l}^{*}_{j})=({\mathscr{l}^{\prime}}^{*}_{j})^{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}}
.

Let us write these equalities in more detail. Recall that
\mathscr{l}_{j}=\langle c_{e_{-}},fc_{e_{+}}f^{-1}\rangle
, and that, by the Bass diagrams, we have
\phi_{v}(c_{e_{-}})=c_{e_{-}}^{\gamma_{e_{-}}}
, and
\phi(c_{e_{+}})=c_{e_{+}}^{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}}
.

We have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l}_{j})&=&\langle c_{e_{-}}^{\gamma_{e_{-}}},\phi_{v}(f)c_{e_{+}}^{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}} \phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\rangle\\
&=&\langle c_{e_{-}},\;\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}\,c_{e_{+}}\,{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}} \phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\gamma_{e_{-}}^{-1}\rangle^{\gamma_{e_{-}}}\end{array}

However, as already mentioned,
\phi_{v}(\mathscr{l}_{j})=(\mathscr{l}^{\prime}_{j})^{\gamma_{e_{-}}}
. Therefore

\mathscr{l}^{\prime}_{j}\;=\;\langle c_{e_{-}},\;\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}\,c_{e_{+}}\,{\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}} \phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\gamma_{e_{-}}^{-1}\rangle.

In the same way, considering the duals, one checks that

{\mathscr{l}^{*}}^{\prime}_{j}\;=\;\langle c_{\bar{e}_{+}},\;\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}\phi_{v}(f)^{-1}\gamma_{e_{-}}^{-1}\,c_{e_{-}}\,{\gamma_{e_{-}}} \phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}\rangle.

It remains to apply Proposition
5.10
to obtain, as desired, that

I_{e_{-}}\gamma_{e_{-}}\phi_{v}(f)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{+}}^{-1}O_{e_{+}}\;=\;I_{e_{-}}f^{\prime}O_{e_{+}}.

∎

5.4.4
Orbits in vertices I: subgroups, linkages

Suppose we are given two centred tuples of tuples
\underline{S}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
and
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
. Denote by
e_{i}
the polarizing edge
e_{S_{i}}
and
v_{i}
the central vertex
v_{S_{i}}
. Consider the action

\left.\begin{array}[]{l}\phi\cdot\underline{S}=(\phi\cdot S_{1},\ldots,\phi\cdot S_{k})\\
\phi\cdot S_{i}=\begin{cases}(\phi\cdot_{e_{i}}S_{i1},\ldots,\phi\cdot_{e_{i}}S_{il_{i}})&\text{if $S_{i}$ is not elliptic,}\\
(\phi_{v_{i}}(S_{i1}),\ldots,\phi_{v_{i}}(S_{il_{i}}))&\text{if $S_{i}$ is elliptic.}\end{cases}\end{array}\right.

(15)

Our first goal is to decide if this action can bring all the double cosets in
\underline{DC}(S_{i})
to
\underline{DC}(T_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq k
for the
S_{i},T_{i}
that are non-elliptic, where

\underline{DC}(T_{i})=DC(T_{i1},e_{T_{i}})\odot\cdots\odot DC(T_{il_{i}},e_{T_{i}}),

where
\odot
denotes concatenation of tuples.

We recall the following theorem of Gersten improving the classical Whitehead algorithm.

Theorem 5.25

(See
[
Ger84
, Theorems W and M]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finitely generated free group, and
A,B
be two tuples of conjugacy classes of finitely
generated subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
then,

1.

There is an effective procedure for determining if there is
some
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
such that
\alpha A=B
.

2.

The stabilizer of
B
in
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
is finitely
presented and a finite presentation can be effectively
determined.

We need the following refinement, in which the conjugators for certain subgroups are required to be equal.

Theorem 5.26

(Mixed whitehead problems for subgroups of free groups)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a finitely generated free group, and

A=([A_{1}],\dots[A_{r}]),B=([B_{1}],\dots,[B_{r}])

be two tuples of
conjugacy classes of tuples of
finitely
generated subgroups of
\mathbb{F}
then,

1.

There is an effective procedure for determining if there is
some
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
such that, for all
i
,
[\alpha A_{i}]=[B_{i}]
(as conjugacy classes of tuple of subgroups).

2.

The stabilizer of
B
in
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}\right)}
is finitely
presented and a finite presentation can be effectively
determined.

Due to its specific independence with the current notations and objects, we differ the proof of Theorem
5.26
to Section
5.6
. We finish now our study of the Mixed Whitehead Problem assuming that Theorem
5.26
is established.

Corollary 5.27

(Aligning coset tuples and elliptic tuples)
.

Given two centred tuples of tuples
\underline{S}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
and
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
, it is decidable whether there exists some
\phi\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
such that

\underline{DC}(\phi\cdot S_{i})=\underline{DC}(T_{i})

for
i=1,\ldots,k
and
S_{i},T_{i}
are non-elliptic, and such that for elliptic tuples

[\phi\cdot S_{i}]_{v_{i}}=[Ti]_{v_{i}}

where the action is as defined in (
15
) and where
[S_{i}]_{v_{i}}
denotes conjugacy classes in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{i}}
. Furthermore, a finite generating set of the stabilizer
A_{\underline{T}}\leq\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
of all the tuples
\underline{DC}(T_{i})
and
[T_{j}]
for
T_{i}
non-elliptic and
T_{j}
elliptic (respectively) can be computed.

Proof.

This problem is solved by using an instance of the mixed Whitehead Problem for subgroups for each free group
F_{v},v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
. For a given vertex
v
the tuple of tuples
\mathcal{T}_{v}(\underline{S})
for a tuple of tuples
\underline{S}
is constructed as follows:

1.

Let
I_{v}
denote the indices
i
such that
S_{i}
is elliptic and the central vertex
v_{S_{i}}=v
, and let
J
denote the indices of the non-elliptic tuples.

2.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{v}=(\mathcal{Q}(S_{i}):i\in I_{v})
, be the tuple of linkage configurations of elliptic tuples centred at
v
.

3.

Denote by

\mathcal{H}_{v,e}(S_{i})=\bigodot_{j=1}^{l_{j}}\left(\mathcal{P}_{v,e,j}\odot\mathcal{P}_{v,e,j}^{*}\right)

where
\mathcal{Q}_{v,e}(S_{ij},e_{j})=(c_{e},\mathcal{P}_{v,e},\mathcal{P}_{v,e}^{*})
is the linkage configuration for the entry
S_{ij}
of
S_{i}
and
\odot
denotes
concatenation of tuples.

4.

Set
\mathcal{H}_{v,e}=(c_{e})\odot\left(\bigodot_{i\in J}\mathcal{H}_{v,e}(S_{i})\right)
then make the tuple of tuples of subgroups

\mathcal{H}_{v}=(\mathcal{H}_{v,e}:e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}).

5.

The required tuple of tuples

\mathcal{T}_{v}=\mathcal{E}_{v}\odot\mathcal{H}_{v}.

By Theorem
5.26
the Mixed whitehead problem for tuples of tuples of subgroups of
F_{v}
is decidable so we can decide
\mathcal{T}_{v}(\underline{S})
and
\mathcal{T}_{v}(\underline{T})
are equivalent via some
\phi_{v}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F_{v}\right)}
. The issue remains that even we preserved the conjugacy classes of subgroups
{\left\langle c_{e}\right\rangle}
or entries
{\left\langle(T_{ij})_{f}\right\rangle}
in
\phi_{v}(\mathcal{E}_{v})
, we may have sent the certain of the
c_{e}
or
(T_{ij})_{f}
to conjugates of their inverses. However, because we can compute a finite generating set of the stabilizer of
\mathcal{T}_{v}(\underline{T})
, we can determine if there is an element of that stabilizer that also preserves the sign of those elements. Once we have done this and amended
\phi_{v}
we can then compute a generating set of the finite index subgroup of the stabilizer of
\mathcal{T}_{v}(\underline{T})
that also preserves the signs of individual elements.

The result now follows by applying Proposition
5.24
for corresponding pairs of non-elliptic tuples and by the definition of the Mixed Whitehead problem applied corresponding pairs of elliptic tuples.
∎

5.4.5
Orbits in vertices II: actions on power tuples.

Fix an
X
-loop
\rho=e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
based at
v
a polarizing edge
e
with
i(e)=v
and a sequence of cosets
D=(I_{\bar{e}}f_{1}O_{e_{1}},1,I_{e_{2}}f_{3}O_{e_{3}},\ldots,I_{e_{n}}f_{n+1}O_{e})
. We call
\mathcal{E}_{(D,p,e)}=\mathbb{Z}^{n/2+3}
the
embedding space
for
(D,\rho,e)
. There is a bijection from
\mathcal{E}_{(D,\rho,e)}
to the set:

\mathcal{G}(D,\rho,e)=\{g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v)|g\textrm{~has underlying $X$-path $\rho$ and~}DC(g,e)=D\}

given by the inverse maps:

\displaystyle(d_{0},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n+1},k)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{3}\cdots e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}\bar{f}_{n+1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k}

\displaystyle\mathrm{pow}(g,e)

\displaystyle\mapsfrom

\displaystyle g

Where the
\bar{f}_{i}
are the coset representatives from
DCR(g,e)
. This next lemma justifies the use of polarized actions.

Lemma 5.28

(Prefix-short preservation)
.

If
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},i(e_{1}))
is centred
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short and
\phi=(Id_{X},(\phi_{v}),(Id_{e}),(\gamma_{e}))\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
. Then
\phi\cdot_{e_{1}}g
is also centred
e_{1}e_{2}
-prefix-short. In other words the
e_{1}
-polarized action of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
acts on the set of centred
e_{1}e_{j}
-prefix-short elements.

Proof.

Let
g=e_{1}e_{2}f_{2}\cdots
and applying
\phi
to each symbol gives

\phi\cdot_{e_{1}}g=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(g)\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}=\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}(\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1}e_{1}\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{\bar{e_{2}}}^{-1}e_{2}\gamma_{e_{2}}\phi_{v_{2}}(f_{2})\cdots)\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1}.

Since
v_{1}=\tau(e_{1})
is abelian and coincides with the images of incident edge groups we can migrate
\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{\bar{e_{2}}}^{-1}
across
e_{2}
to get

\phi\cdot_{e_{1}}g=e_{1}e_{2}f_{2}^{\prime}\cdots,

as required.
∎

Corollary 5.29
.

For any
\phi\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
and
g\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},i(e))
,
g
is
ee_{j}
-prefix short, for some other
e_{j}\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
if and only if
\phi\cdot_{e}g
is
ee_{j}
-prefix short.

With this detail established we now have:

Proposition 5.30
.

Let
D
be a tuple of cosets, and let
A_{D}\leq\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
be a subgroup of automorphisms that preserves the cosets in
D
via the action in Convention
5.22
. Then
A_{D}
acts on
\mathcal{G}(D,\rho,e)
and also, viewing
\mathcal{E}_{D,\rho,e}
as an abelian group, there is a homomorphism

\epsilon_{D,\rho,e}:A_{D}\to\mathcal{E}_{D,\rho,e}

such that

\mathrm{pow}(\phi\cdot_{e}g,g)=\mathrm{pow}(g,e)+\epsilon_{D,\rho,e}(\phi).

Proof.

The proof amounts to applying
\phi\cdot_{e}
and keeping track of exponents. Applying
\phi
to every symbol and making obvious cancellations gives:

\phi\cdot_{e}g=\phi\cdot_{e}(c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{3}\cdots e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}\bar{f}_{n+1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k})\\
=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}})\phi(\bar{f}_{1})\phi(e_{1})\phi(e_{2})\cdots\phi(e_{n})\phi(c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}})\phi(\bar{f}_{n+1})\phi(c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}})\phi(t_{b}^{k})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}\\
=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(\bar{f}_{1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1}e_{1}\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{2}}^{-1}e_{2}\gamma_{e_{2}}\cdots\\
\gamma_{\bar{e}_{n}}^{-1}e_{n}\gamma_{e_{n}}\gamma_{e_{n}}^{-1}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}\gamma_{e_{n}}\phi(\bar{f}_{n+1})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}\gamma_{\bar{e}}\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}t_{b}^{k}\\
=c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}(\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(\bar{f}_{1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1})e_{1}(\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{2}}^{-1})e_{2}\gamma_{e_{2}}\cdots\gamma_{\bar{e}_{n}}^{-1}e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}(\gamma_{e_{n}}\phi(\bar{f}_{n+1})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1})c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k}

Now note that for every odd index
i
, noting that the vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{\tau(e_{i})}
must be abelian and coincides with images of incident edge groups we have

e_{i}(\gamma_{e_{i}}\gamma_{\bar{e}_{i+1}}^{-1})e_{i+1}=e_{i}e_{i+1}c_{e_{i}+1}^{m_{i}},

for some
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. This gives

\phi\cdot_{e}g=c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}}(\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(\bar{f}_{1})\gamma_{\bar{e}_{1}}^{-1})e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{m_{2}}\cdots e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{m_{n}}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}}(\gamma_{e_{n}}\phi(\bar{f}_{n+1})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1})c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k}

Finally note that our assumption that
\phi
preserves
D
implies that since
\bar{f}_{i+1}\in{\left\langle c_{e_{i}}\right\rangle}\bar{f}_{i+1}{\left\langle c_{\bar{e}_{i+1}}\right\rangle}
then we must have that
\phi(\bar{f}_{i+1})=c_{e_{i}}^{n_{i}}\bar{f}_{i+1}c_{\bar{e}_{i+1}}^{n_{i+1}}
for some
n_{i},n_{i+1}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
provided we set
e_{0}=\bar{e}
and
e_{n}=e
. Applying the identities

c_{\bar{e}_{i+1}}^{n_{i+1}}e_{i+1}e_{i+2}=e_{i+1}e_{i+2}c_{e_{i+2}}^{n_{i+1}}

finally gives

\phi\cdot_{e}g=c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{0}+n_{0}}\bar{f}_{1}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}+n_{1}+m_{1}+n_{2}}\bar{f}_{3}\cdots e_{n}c_{e_{n}}^{d_{n}+n_{n-1}+n_{n}+m_{n-1}}\bar{f}_{n+1}c_{\bar{e}}^{d_{n+1}+n_{n+1}}t_{b}^{k}.

Thus, if
g
is not
ee_{j}
-prefix clean we have

\mathrm{pow}(\phi\cdot_{e}g,e)=\mathrm{pow}(g,e)+\underbrace{(n_{0},m_{1}+n_{1}+n_{2},\ldots,m_{n-1}+n_{n-1}+n_{n},n_{n+1})}_{\epsilon_{D,\rho,e}(\phi)},

since
\epsilon_{D,\rho,e}(\phi)
doesn’t depend on
\mathrm{pow}(g,e)
, but only on
D,\rho,e,\phi
.

If
g
is
ee_{j}
prefix clean, then note that since
e_{1}=e
we have

\phi\cdot_{e}e_{1}e_{2}c_{e_{2}}^{d_{2}}\bar{f}_{3}\cdots=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}e_{1}\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{e_{2}}^{-2}e_{2}\cdots=e_{1}\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{e_{2}}^{-2}e_{2}\cdots=e_{1}e_{2}\cdots

In particular if
g
is
ee_{j}
prefix clean then the
n_{0}
-entry in
\epsilon_{D,\rho,\epsilon}(\phi)
is trivial.
∎

Up to this point, we have been focusing mainly on fibre parts of words we will now consider conjugation by elements that are not in the fibre.

Proposition 5.31
.

Let
D
be a tuple of cosets,
e
some polarizing edge and
\rho
some
X
-loop based at
i(e)=v
that is compatible with
D
. Then the edge group
\mathbb{X}_{e}
acts on
\mathcal{G}(D,\rho,e)
via

a\cdot g=i_{e}(a)gi_{e}(a)^{-1},g\in\mathcal{G}(D,\rho,e),a\in\mathbb{X}_{e}

and this action induces a homomorphism
\alpha_{D,\rho,e}:\mathbb{X}_{e}\to\mathcal{E}_{D,\rho,e}
such that

\mathrm{pow}(a\cdot g,g)=\mathrm{pow}(g,e)+\alpha{D,\rho,e}(a).

Proof.

By the definition of
e-
polarized normal forms, conjugation of
g
by
i_{e}(a)=c_{\bar{e}}\in i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
doesn’t change
DC(g,e)
. If
g
is
ee_{j}
-prefix clean then we can migrate the initial
c_{\bar{e}}^{n_{0}}
symbol through the
ee_{j}
prefix and stay in prefix clean form. It’s easy to verify that the new power vector is obtained from the old power vector by adding some
\epsilon_{D,\rho,e}(a)
. The other generator of
i_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
is
t_{v}
, the generator of the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{v}
. The action by conjugation by
t_{v}
on the fibre
F\leq{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
is precisely the multiple Dehn twist
\alpha
which is given by the
{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
substitution
e\mapsto ec_{e}^{n_{e,\alpha}}
. In particular conjugation by
t_{v}
can be realized by an element of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
so Proposition
5.30
implies the result for conjugation by powers of
t_{v}
, and the result follows.
∎

Corollary 5.32

(Action on embedding space)
.

Let
A_{D}\leq\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
be a subgroup of automorphisms that preserve the cosets in
D
. Then
\mathbb{X}_{e}\times A_{D}
acts on
\mathcal{G}(D,\rho,e)
via

(a,\phi)\cdot g=i_{e}(a)(\phi\cdot_{e}g)i_{e}(a)^{-1}

and this action gives rise to a homomorphism

\chi_{D,\rho,e}:\mathbb{X}_{e}\times A_{D}\to\mathcal{E}_{D,\rho,e}

such that

\mathrm{pow}((a,\phi)\cdot g,e)=\mathrm{pow}(g,e)+\chi_{D,\rho,e}(a,\phi)

(16)

5.5
Solving the fiber and orientation preserving Mixed Whitehead problem

In this section, we describe and prove the correctness of our algorithm. Suppose we are given two tuples of tuples
\underline{S}=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
and
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
of elements from
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}
. The fibre and orientation preserving Mixed Whitehead problem is the decision problem of whether
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
in the same orbit under the action of
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
given by

(\phi,g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\cdot(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})=(g_{1}^{-1}\phi(T_{1})g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}^{-1}\phi(T_{k})g_{k}).

(17)

We first note that
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are in the same orbit if and only if their augmentations
\underline{S}^{+}=(S_{1}^{+},\ldots,S_{k}^{+})
and
\underline{T}^{+}=(T_{1}^{+},\ldots,T_{k}^{+})
are in the same orbit. So, without loss of generality, we will assume that
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are tuples of augmented tuples, if they aren’t then we replace their entries by augmentations (which can be done algorithmically).

By Lemma
5.17
this problem could be reduced to a finite computable set of instances of similar orbit problems under
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
. Specifically,
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are in the same
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
-orbit if and only if for one of the representatives
\nu_{i}
of a coset of
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\backslash\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
we have that
\underline{\nu_{i}(S)}
is in the same
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
-orbit as
\underline{T}
.

Now given some tuple
T
of elements, we denote by
\mathcal{T}
the set of all the
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
-conjugates of
T
that are in centred short position (recall Section
5.3.3
), if
T
is hyperbolic or lineal, otherwise
\mathcal{T}
is the set containing the centring of
T
and all its variations, should any exist (recall Section
5.3.4
.) With this notation, we define two sets of tuples of tuples

\underline{\mathcal{S}}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{v}\mathcal{S}_{i1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{ik}

where
\{\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{v}\}
is the computable set of representatives given by Lemma
5.17
and
\underline{\nu_{i}(S)}=(S_{i1},\ldots,S_{ik});i=1,\ldots,v
. We also define

\underline{\mathcal{T}}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{T}_{k}

where
\underline{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{k})
.

On the one hand,
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
actually extends to a group of automorphisms
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
. On the other hand, Lemma
5.15
implies that we can extend our consideration to conjugation in the Bass group. Therefore our original
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are in the same
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
-orbit if and only if there exists at least one pair of tuples of tuples
\underline{S}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\underline{T}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
that are in the same
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times(\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})^{k})
-orbit.

In Section
5.4
, we considered polarized actions which are not exactly the same as the action given in (
17
). However, since the Mixed Whitehead problem involves post-conjugation by group elements, this does not
