change the problem. To be clear: our original
\underline{S}
and
\underline{T}
are in the same
\delta{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times({{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}^{k})
-orbit if and only if there exists at least one pair of tuples of tuples
\underline{S}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\underline{T}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
that are in the same
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times(\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})^{k})
-orbit where the new action is given by:

(\phi,g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\cdot(S^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,S^{\prime}_{k})=\left(g_{1}^{-1}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{1})g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}^{-1}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{k})g_{k}\right).

(18)

Where the action is given by (
15
). This is because if
S^{\prime}_{j}
is hyperbolic or lineal, then
\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{j}=\gamma_{\bar{e}}\phi(S^{\prime}_{j})\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}
.

By Lemma
5.28
, prefix short forms are preserved by polarized actions. So suppose that
\underline{S}^{\prime}
and
\underline{T}^{\prime}
are in the same
\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}\times(\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})^{k})
orbit, under the new action. Then there is some
\phi\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
such
\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i}
is conjugate in
\mathbf{Bass}(\mathbb{X})
to
T^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
. Now non-elliptic tuples in
\phi\cdot\underline{S}^{\prime}
may no longer be centred short position, but their leading entries are in prefix clean position. It follows that there are elements in
a_{i}\in i_{e_{i}}(\mathbb{X}_{i_{(}e_{i})})
such that the leading entry of

a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}

is in short position for all appropriate indices. We’ll now show why the entire tuple
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
is in centred short position (and not only its leading entry). If that isn’t the case, then we would need to further conjugate
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
by some element in the centralizer of the first entry to get to short position of the tuple. If
S^{\prime}_{i}
is lineal, doing so doesn’t change any of the entries in
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
, so
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
is already in short position. Otherwise,
S^{\prime}_{i}
was already chosen so that its second entry had shortest syllable length among all conjugates by the centralizer of the first entry since applying
\phi
and conjugation by
a_{i}
did not change syllable lengths, further conjugation by an element of the centralizer of the first entry of
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
will increase the syllable length of the second entry, so in this case as well
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
is already in short position.

Now all the tuples in
\underline{T}^{\prime}
are also in centred short position. Thus either
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}=T^{\prime}_{i}
, otherwise
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}
is equal to one of the other finitely many short conjugates of
T^{\prime}_{i}
. In particular there must be some other
\underline{T}^{\prime\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
such that
a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}=T^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.

If
S^{\prime}_{i}
is elliptic and
\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i}
is conjugate to
T^{\prime}_{i}
then either
\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i}
is conjugate to
T^{\prime}_{i}
by an element of
\mathbb{X}_{v_{S^{\prime}_{i}}}
or by some element of the form
\mathbb{X}_{v_{S^{\prime}_{i}}}\ell
where
\ell
is a loop in
X
based at
v_{S^{\prime}_{i}}
. By our definition of variations and by Lemma
4.11
, there is some
\underline{S}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\phi\cdot S^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is conjugate to
T^{\prime}_{i}
by an element of
\mathbb{X}_{v_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}}}
.

Therefore our original instance of the fibre and orientation preserving Mixed Whitehead problem has a positive solution if and only if there exist
\underline{S}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{S}}
and
\underline{T}^{\prime}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
, some
\phi\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
and elements
a_{i}
where
a_{i}\in F_{v_{i}}
if
S^{\prime}_{i}
is elliptic and
a_{i}\in i_{e_{i}}(\mathbb{X}_{e_{i}})
if
S^{\prime}_{i}
is lineal or hyperbolic such that

a_{i}(\phi\cdot S^{\prime}_{i})a_{i}^{-1}=T^{\prime}_{i}.

(19)

Note that by Proposition
5.31
conjugation by such elements
a_{i}
for non-elliptic tuples leave invariant
DC(\phi\cdot S_{i})
. We therefore finally apply Corollary
5.27
to
\underline{S}^{\prime}
and
\underline{T}^{\prime}
. This gives us an automorphism
\phi_{1}\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
such that for elliptic tuples we already have
[\phi_{1}\cdot S^{\prime}_{i}]_{v_{i}}=[T^{\prime}_{i}]_{v_{i}}
, so (
19
) has a solution for elliptic tuples.

Without loss of generality the first
h
entries
S^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,S^{\prime}_{h}
of
\underline{S}^{\prime}
are precisely the non-elliptic tuples, the same must be true for
\underline{T}^{\prime}
. For a tuple of elements
T_{i}=(T_{i1},\ldots,T_{il_{i}})
we define the embedding space

\mathcal{E}_{T_{i}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{l_{i}}\mathcal{E}_{DC(T_{ij},e_{i}),\rho_{ij},e_{i}}

where
\rho_{ij}
is the
X
-path underlying
T_{ij}
. For the tuple of tuples
\underline{T}^{\prime}
we define the embedding space

\mathcal{E}(\underline{T}^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{h}\mathcal{E}_{T_{i}}.

Now by Corollary
5.27
we can decide if there is some
\phi_{1}\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
such that in addition
\underline{DC}(S^{\prime}_{i},e_{i})=\underline{DC}(T^{\prime}_{i},e_{i})
in which case we would also have
\mathcal{E}(\phi_{1}\cdot\underline{S}^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}(\underline{T}^{\prime})
.

Consider now analogous mappings

\mathrm{pow}(T_{i})=\bigodot_{j=1}^{l_{i}}\mathrm{pow}(T_{ij},e_{i})\textrm{~and~}\mathrm{pow}(\underline{T}^{\prime})=\bigodot_{i=1}^{h}\mathrm{pow}(T_{i}),

where
\odot
denotes concatenation of tuples. By Corollary
5.32
there is an action of
A_{\underline{T}^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{h}}
, where
A_{\underline{T}^{\prime}}
is as given in Corollary
5.27
, obtained by assembling the actions on embedding spaces and a corresponding homomorphism

\chi:A_{\underline{T}^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{h}}\to\mathcal{E}(\underline{T}^{\prime}),

that satisfies the analogue of (
16
).

It finally follows that our instance of the Mixed Whitehead problem has a solution if and only if for the fixed
\phi_{1}\in\delta_{1}{\mathrm{Aut_{fo}}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
that we computed above, there is some
(\phi_{2},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in A_{\underline{T}^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{h}}
such that

\mathrm{pow}(\underline{T}^{\prime})=\mathrm{pow}(\phi_{1}\cdot\underline{S}^{\prime})+\chi(\phi_{2},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}).

Since we have an explicit generating set of

A_{\underline{T}^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{X}_{e_{h}}
and can compute
\chi
the problem reduces to deciding whether given a generating set for a subgroup
{\left\langle h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\right\rangle}=H\leq\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and two elements
\underline{s}
and
\underline{t}
whether there is
\underline{h}\in H
such that
\underline{t}=\underline{s}+\underline{h}
. This amounts to elementary linear algebra over
\mathbb{Z}
and is readily decidable.

It therefore follows that the fibre and orientation preserving Mixed Whitehead problem is decidable in
{{\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}}}.

5.6
The proof of Theorem
5.26

We finally come back to Theorem
5.26
, for which we promised a proof.

Let fix a finite basis
X
of
F=F(X)
, then there is a one-to-one correspondence between subgroups of
F
and
core graphs with a base vertex
v
(see
[
KM02
, Theorem 5.1]
or
[
Sta83
]
.) If
H\leq{\mathbb{F}(n)}
is finitely generated, then the corresponding based core graph is also finite.
An unbased core graph
is a directed graph with labels in
X
which is folded (in the sense of
[
KM02
, Definition 2.3]
) without vertices of valence one. By
[
KM02
, Proposition 7.7]
there is a bijective correspondence between conjugacy classes of subgroups of
F
and unbased core graphs.
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F\right)}
acts on this set of graphs as follows: given an automorphism
\alpha
and an unbased graph
\mathcal{G}\left(H\right)
, the image
\alpha(\mathcal{G}\left(H\right))
is computed using the following steps.

(I)

For each edge labelled
y
subdivide it into a path graph along which we read
\alpha(y)
.

(II)

Perform a sequence of Stallings folds, until the graph is folded.

(III)

Perform a
coring
operation, i.e. repeatedly contract edges adjacent to vertices of valence 1 to points until none such remain.

Details are given in
[
DF05
, §9.2]
. Note that each of these operations can be realized as a sequence of continuous quotient maps between graphs sending vertices to vertices. In particular each vertex of
\mathcal{G}\left(H\right))
has a well-defined image in
\alpha^{\prime}(\mathcal{G}\left(H\right))
. A crucial fact is that the individual folds or edge contractions in steps
II
and
III
can be done in any order and the end result will be the same. We will use Gersten’s Theorem
5.25
presented earlier.

As mentioned earlier, an actual subgroup
H\leq F
is given by a
core graph with a base vertex
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(H\right),v\right)}
which is a directed folded graph with labels in
X
all of whose vertices, except possibly the base vertex
v
, have valence one. The elements of
H
are precisely the reduced words one reads of reduced loops starting and ending at the base vertex
v
.

5.6.1
Construction of an auxiliary
F(X,K)
with its tuples of conjugacy classes of subgroups

For the remainder of this section we fix tuples
A=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r})
and
B=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{r})

of tuples of subgroups of
F=F(X)
(i.e. each
A_{i}
is a
tuple of subgroups). We assume that
|A_{i}|=|B_{i}|
, and by convention, we assume that
|A_{i}|\geq 2
for all
i
. If it happens that one is interested in
A_{i}
being a singleton, one may take a pair of identical copies of the same subgroup so that this convention is satisfied. This duplication does not add any further constraint, hence is done without loss of generality.

For each
i
, let
K_{i}
be a set of symbols so that
|K_{i}|=|A_{i}|=|B_{i}|
. Let
K=K_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup K_{r}
and consider
F=F(X)
as a free factor of
F(X,K)
.

We list
K_{i}=\{\kappa_{i1},\ldots,\kappa_{in_{i}}\}
as a set of symbols double indexed by positive integers such that
K_{i}\cap X=\emptyset
and such that
i\neq j\Rightarrow K_{i}\cap K_{j}=\emptyset
. A
K_{i}
-star
is a directed labelled tree with a root vertex
v_{i}
and for each symbol in
\kappa_{ij}\in K_{i}
there is an edge outgoing from
v_{i}
with label
\kappa_{ij}
.

We encode the tuples
A_{i}=(A_{i1},\ldots,A_{in_{i}})
and
B_{i}=(B_{i1},\ldots,B_{in_{i}})
of subgroups of
F
using a
tuple-graph
, denoted
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
and
\mathcal{G}\left(B_{i}\right)
, respectively. They are constructed by taking a
K_{i}
-star, the disjoint union of the core graphs with a base vertex
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(A_{ij}\right),u_{ij}\right)}
(respectively
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(B_{ij}\right),u_{ij}\right)}
), and identifying the vertex of the
K_{i}
-star with incoming edge labelled
\kappa_{ij}
to the base vertex
u_{ij}
(respectively
v_{ij}
). The key fact is that although
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
is not a based graph, due to its specific structure, and edges with special labels
\kappa_{ij}
it is possible to recover the core graphs with base vertex
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(A_{ij}\right),u_{ij}\right)}
or
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(B_{ij}\right),v_{ij}\right)}
and thus the ordered tuple of subgroups
A_{i}
or
B_{i}
respectively.

A tuple-graph always has a distinguished vertex
v_{i}
, namely the only vertex with outgoing edges labelled by the elements
K_{i}
. Denote by
\dot{A}_{i}=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right),v_{i})\leq F(X,K)
the subgroup consisting of the elements that are represented as the words read along closed loops in
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
based at
v_{i}
and define
\dot{B_{i}}
analogously. Note that the conjugacy classes
[\dot{A}_{i}]
and
[\dot{B}_{i}]
correspond exactly to the tuple graphs
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
and
\mathcal{G}\left(B_{i}\right)
respectively.

Fix the following tuples of subgroups:

\displaystyle\widehat{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\dot{A}_{1},\ldots,\dot{A}_{r},F(X),F(K_{1}),\ldots,F(K_{r}),R_{1},\ldots R_{r})

(20)

\displaystyle\widehat{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\dot{B}_{1},\ldots,\dot{B}_{r},F(X),F(K_{1}),\ldots,F(K_{r}),R_{1},\ldots R_{r}),

(21)

where
R_{i}\leq F(K_{i})
is a rigid subgroup, in the sense that
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(K_{i})\right)}}(R_{i})\leq{\mathrm{Inn}\left(F(K_{i})\right)}
, for example
R_{i}={\left\langle c_{i}\right\rangle}
where
c_{i}
is a C-test element as given in
[
Iva98
]
. These are precisely the objects that Theorem
5.25
is about.

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
\beta\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}
send
\widehat{A}
to
\widehat{B}
or fix
\widehat{B}
. Then we have the following restrictions

1.

\beta|_{F(X)}={\mathrm{ad}_{w}}\circ\alpha
, where
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}
and
w\in F(X,K)
, and

2.

\beta|_{F(K_{i})}={\mathrm{ad}_{w_{i}}}
for some
w_{i}\in F(X,K)
.

Proof.

By hypothesis
\beta
maps
F(X)
to a conjugate
w^{-1}F(X)w
and similarly
\beta(F(K_{i}))={w_{i}}^{-1}F(K_{i})w_{i}
. This implies that the restriction
{\mathrm{ad}_{w^{-1}}}\circ\beta|_{F(X)}
is given by an automorphism
\alpha:F(X)\to F(X)\leq F(X,K).
This proves the first point.

For the second point, note that we also require that
\beta
maps
R_{i}
to a conjugate of
R_{i}
since the restriction
{\mathrm{ad}_{w_{i}}}\circ\beta|_{F(K_{i})}
is an automorphism of
\alpha_{i}
of
F(K_{i})
and therefore
{\mathrm{ad}_{w_{i}}}\circ\beta(R_{i})=g_{i}^{-1}R_{i}g_{i}
for some
g_{i}\in F(K_{i})
. The hypothesis that
R_{i}
is rigid implies that
{\mathrm{ad}_{w_{i}}}\circ\beta|_{F(K_{i})}
actually restricts to an inner automorphism on
F(K_{i})
. This proves the second point.
∎

5.6.2
From a solution in
F(X)
to a solution in
F(X,K)

Proposition 5.34
.

If there is an
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}
such that
[\alpha(A_{i})]=[B_{i}]

then there is an automorphism
\beta\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}
such that

\beta(\widehat{A})=\widehat{B}.

Proof.

The
\alpha
given in the hypothesis extends to an automorphism
\widehat{\alpha}
of
F(X,K)
that fixes the elements of the free factor
F(K)
elementwise. Let
w_{i}\in F(x)
be the elements such that
w_{i}\alpha(A_{i})w_{i}^{-1}=B_{i}
.

The conjugacy class
[\dot{A}_{i}]
corresponds to the (unbased) tuple graph
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)=\mathcal{G}\left(\dot{A}_{i}\right)
. Denote by
v_{i1},\ldots,v_{in_{i}}
the vertices that are the basepoints of the based subgraphs
{\left(\mathcal{B}\left(A_{ij}\right),v_{ij}\right)}\subset\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
coming from the entries
A_{ij}
of the tuple
A_{i}
. Every edge of
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
with a label in
X
lies in one of these subgraphs. Applying
\widehat{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right)
is achieved by subdividing every edge with label
x\in X
to an appropriately oriented path with label
\alpha(x)
, leaving the edges with label
K_{i}
unchanged, and finally folding and coring. The same result can be achieved by a tuple graph for
(\alpha(A_{i1}),\ldots,\alpha(A_{in_{i}}))
. This is not quite the target tuple we want, but we can remedy this by conjugating the special symbols the
K_{i}
by the appropriate elements of
F(X)
. We achieve this with the automorphism
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}
that is defined on the basis
X\cup K
as follows:

\beta^{\prime}(z)=\begin{cases}z&\textrm{if~}z=x\in X\\
w_{i}zw_{i}^{-1}&\textrm{if~}z=\kappa_{ij}\in K_{i}\end{cases}.

The effect of first applying
\beta^{\prime}
to each
\widehat{\alpha}(\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right))
and then folding and coring is depicted in Figure
1
. Thus, taking
\beta=\beta^{\prime}\circ\widehat{\alpha}
, we have
[\beta(\dot{A}_{i})]=[\dot{B}_{i}]
. Furthermore, it is immediate from the construction that
\beta
maps
F(X)
to itself and the restrictions
\beta|_{F(K_{i})}(g)={\mathrm{ad}_{w_{i}^{-1}}}(g)
. So, in particular,
\beta
maps the
F(K_{i})
to themselves and the subgroups
R_{i}
to conjugates in
F(X,K)
.
∎

Figure 1:
The effect of applying
\beta^{\prime}
to
\widehat{\alpha}(\mathcal{G}\left(A_{i}\right))
, is shown in four steps. Each arrow is a continuous map, we use the same notation to designate a vertex and its image.

5.6.3
From a solution in
F(X,K)
to a solution in
F(X)

Before stating and proving Proposition
5.36
, let us prove a lemma about foldings.

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a directed graph with labels in
X\cup K
that is constructed as follows. Take some
K_{i}
-star, whose vertices of valence 1 are denoted
u_{1},\ldots,u_{n_{i}}
. Then take a disjoint union of directed graphs with basepoints
(\mathcal{G}_{1},v_{1}),\ldots,(\mathcal{G}_{n_{i}},v_{n_{i}})
with labels in
X
, all of which have non-trivial fundamental groups. Also, take
n_{i}
copies of some path (a directed labelled graph homeomorphic to a line segment)
\gamma
with labels in
X\cup K
with initial vertex
u_{\gamma}
and terminal vertex
v_{\gamma}
. Suppose furthermore that the word read along
\gamma
is reduced. Finally from
\mathcal{G}
by joining each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
to the
K_{i}
-star by a copy of
\gamma
by identifying
u_{i}
with the copy of
u_{\gamma}
and the copy of
v_{\gamma}
with
v_{i}
. See Figure
2

If
\gamma
contains any edges with a label that is not in
X
, then
\mathcal{G}
cannot be brought to an unbased core graph that is the tuple graph of some tuple
(H_{1},\ldots,H_{n_{i}})
of subgroups of
F(X)
by a sequence of folds or coring operations.

Proof.

Let the word read along
\gamma
as we read from
u_{\gamma}
to
v_{\gamma}
be written as

z_{1}\cdots z_{r},

where
z_{i}\in(X\cup K)^{\pm 1}
. Suppose towards a contradiction that some
z_{i}\not\in X^{\pm 1}
but that
\mathcal{G}
after folding and coring can be brought to the tuple graph of some tuple of subgroups of
F(X)
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that the last symbol
z_{r}
read along
\gamma
lies in
K
. Indeed we are supposing that there is some symbol labelling an edge of
\gamma
that is in
K
. We can redefine the graphs
(\mathcal{G}_{1},v_{1})
, by adjoining the maximal terminal segments of the copies of
\gamma
only containing symbols from
X
and redefining
\gamma
so that it is the minimal initial segment containing all symbols in
K
. The resulting graph is exactly the same. One of the properties of a tuple graph is that the only edges with labels in
K
are the ones contained in the
K_{i}
-star. By hypothesis, the paths that are copies of
\gamma
contain edges with labels in
K
, there must therefore be some folding to identify all of these edges with the edges in the
K_{i}
-star.

We start by performing folds within the graphs
\mathcal{G}_{i}
. Since the terminal edge in
\gamma
has a label in
K
, there is no possible folding between the edges in the
\mathcal{G}_{i}
s and edges in terminal segments of copies of
\gamma
. We may then proceed to coring. At this point we can now assume that the only possible foldings occur between the initial edge of some copy of
\gamma
and some edge of the
K_{i}
-star.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
z_{1}=\kappa_{in_{i}}^{-1}
and this means that at this point there is at most one pair of edges that can be folded. We will now consider the folding process step-by-step. Denote by
\gamma_{i}
the copy of
\gamma
attached to
u_{i}
, then we first fold the first edge of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
with the edge of the
K_{i}
-star labelled
\kappa_{in_{i}}
. If there are no other possible folds then the resulting graph is not a tuple graph as required, so we must continue folding. Without loss of generality
z_{1}=\kappa_{i1}
, and the terminal segment of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
starts being partially identified with an initial segment of
\gamma_{1}
this gives equalities

z_{3}=z_{1},z_{4}=z_{2},\ldots

(22)

for each edge of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
that gets folded with an edge of
\gamma_{1}
. Note, however, that (
22
) implies that if
\gamma^{+}
is the initial segment of
\gamma_{1}
consisting of edges that get identified with edges of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
. If
\gamma^{+}
has label

z_{1}\cdots z_{\ell}

then we have

z_{i}=\begin{cases}{\kappa_{in_{i}}}^{-1}&\textrm{if $i$ is odd}\\
\kappa_{i1}&\textrm{if $i$ is even.}\end{cases}

for all
1\leq i\leq\ell+2
. Furthermore, by choice of
\ell
we must have that
z_{\ell+3}\neq z_{\ell+1}
, which includes the possibility that
z_{\ell+3}
does not exist (which would happen if the terminal segment of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
completely folded into
\gamma_{1}
and
|\gamma|=\ell+2
.)

Suppose now that we have folded up to identifying the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
edge in
\gamma_{1}
(labelled by
z_{\ell}
) up with the
(\ell+2)^{\mathrm{th}}
edge of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
. There are two possibilities:

•

If
z_{\ell+3}
exists, i.e.
|\gamma|\geq\ell+3
, then there are no more possible folding operations and the resulting graph cannot be a tuple graph (see Figure
2
).

•

If
z_{\ell+3}
does not exist, i.e. the terminal segment of
\gamma_{n_{i}}
has completely folded into
\gamma_{1}
, then we are in a situation where the graph obtained by folding and coring
\mathcal{G}_{n_{i}}
is attached to a vertex in
\gamma_{1}
between edges with labels
z_{\ell}
and
z_{\ell}+1
, both of which lie in
K^{\pm 1}
, and there is no more possible folding. The resulting graph cannot be a tuple graph either.

In all cases, we arrive at a contradiction so the result holds.
∎

Figure 2:
The possibly unfolded graph
\mathcal{G}
of Lemma
5.35
and attempting to fold it down to a tuple graph. The thickened segment represents the edges that got folded with edges of
\gamma_{n_{i}}.

We may now prove the main proposition of this subsection.

Proposition 5.36
.

Let
A,B
be two tuples of conjugacy classes of tuples of subgroups of the free group
F(X)
, and let
F(X,K)
be the extension as above, and
\dot{A}_{i},\dot{B}_{i}
the corresponding groups of tuple graphs (they are subgroups of
F(X,K)
).

Assume that
\beta\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}
is such that

•

\beta(F(X))=F(X)
,

•

for all
i
,
[\beta(F(K_{i}))]=[F(K_{i})]
and
[\beta(R_{i})]=[R_{i}]
,

•

for all
i
,
[\beta(\dot{A}_{i})]=[\dot{B}_{i}]
.

Then
\beta|_{F(X)}:F(X)\to F(X)
is an automorphism that sends the
F(X)
-conjugacy class (of tuples of subgroups)
[A_{i}]
to
[B_{i}]
.

Observe that
\beta|_{F(X)}
is indeed an automorphism of
F(X)
by assumption.

Proof.

Proving the proposition amounts to proving that the element that conjugates
\beta(\dot{A}_{i})
to

\dot{B}_{i}
can be taken in
F(X)
.

For each index
i
, there is a right coset
F(K_{i})x_{i}
such that
\beta(F(K_{i}))=F(K_{i})^{x_{i}}
.

By the rigidity property of
R_{i}
, one has actually an element
k_{i}\in F(K_{i})
such that, for all index
j
,
\beta(\kappa_{ij})=\kappa_{ij}^{k_{i}x_{i}}
. Let us write
w_{i}=k_{i}x_{i}
.

Applying Lemma
5.35
to
\gamma=w_{i}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}\left(B_{i}\right)
, we obtain that
w_{i}\in F(X)
.

It is therefore sufficient to prove that
\beta(\dot{A}_{i})=(\dot{B}_{i})^{w_{i}}
.
This is the next lemma.

Lemma 5.37
.

\beta(\dot{A}_{i})=(\dot{B}_{i})^{w_{i}}
.

Proof.

Knowing that
w_{i}\in F(X)
, we can consider the image of
\dot{A}_{i}
: it is generated by the groups
w_{i}^{-1}\kappa_{ij}w_{i}\beta(A_{j})w_{i}^{-1}\kappa_{ij}^{-1}w_{i}
. We also know that
\beta(A_{j})<F(X)
since
\beta
preserves
F(X)
.

Let
g_{i,j,k}
(
k
ranging in some set
S_{i,j}
) be generators of
w_{i}^{-1}\kappa_{ij}w_{i}\beta(A_{j})w_{i}^{-1}\kappa_{ij}^{-1}w_{i}
.

All the considered generators
g_{i,j,k}
are in
F(X)^{\kappa_{ij}^{-1}w_{i}}
(for various
j
). Foldings in the word
g_{i,j,k}(\neq 1)
will never cancel the two letters

\kappa_{ij}
and
\kappa_{ij}^{-1}
since all other letters are in
X
, and thus reveal a common prefix
w_{i}^{-1}
and suffix
w_{i}
.

Conjugating
\beta(\dot{A}_{i})
by
w_{i}^{-1}
produces a group generated by the
\kappa_{ij}C_{ij}\kappa_{ij}^{-1}
(for
j
ranging in the indices of the tuple
A_{i}
) with
C_{ij}<F(X)
.
We must show that the generated group is
\dot{B}_{i}
.

By assumption on
\beta
, each
\kappa_{i,j}C_{ij}\kappa_{i,j}^{-1}
must be in a conjugate of
\dot{B}_{i}
and their union generates this conjugate. Thus, let
y
be such that for all
j
,
C_{ij}^{\kappa_{ij}^{-1}y^{-1}}<\dot{B}_{i}
, and the union (over
j
) generate
\dot{B}_{i}
.

Recall that by convention, there are at least two indices
j
in the tuple
A_{i}
. Write
y
as a reduced word over
X\cup K
, and select
j
such that
y\kappa_{ij}
is reduced (there exists such
j
). Then considering reduced words
c\in C_{ij}
(in
X
only), the words
y\kappa_{ij}c\kappa_{ij}^{-1}y^{-1}
are reduced and are all in
\dot{B}_{i}
. Folding them on the graph
\mathcal{G}\left(B_{i}\right)
, it easily follows that
y\in\dot{B}_{i}
(i.e. its folding finishes back to the vertex
v_{i}
, the only vertex on which the next edge labelled
\kappa_{ij}
can fold). Hence
C_{ij}^{\kappa_{ij}^{-1}}<\dot{B}_{i}
, and the union over
j
generate
\dot{B}_{i}
. In other words
\beta(\dot{A}_{i})=(\dot{B}_{i})^{w_{i}}
, which we wanted to show.

∎

∎

Propositions
5.34
and
5.36
make the following an application of Gersten’s theorem
5.25
(first point).

Corollary 5.38
.

Let
F
be a free group, then given two tuples of conjugacy classes of tuples of finitely generated subgroups, one can decide whether an automorphism of
F
sends the first to the second.

5.6.4
From generators of stabilizers in
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}
to generators of stabilizers in
{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}

Let
B
be a tuple of conjugacy classes of tuples of subgroups of the free group
F(X)
, and let
F(X,K)
and
\widehat{B}
be the extensions as above (
\widehat{B}
is a tuple of conjugacy classes of subgroups containing
F(X)
, whose list is in equation
21
).

We are interested in
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})

(which we can compute by Theorem
5.25
), and its relation with
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B)
.

As an auxiliary tool, we will consider

{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))<{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B}).

Proposition 5.39
.

Let
B
be a tuple of conjugacy classes of tuples of subgroups of the free group
F(X)
, and let
F(X,K)
and
\widehat{B}
be the extensions as above (
\widehat{B}
is a tuple of conjugacy classes of subgroups).

Let
\{\beta_{j},\;j\in J\}
be a generating set of
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})
, that contains the elements
{\rm ad}_{x},x\in X
.

Then for all
j\in J
, there exists
\gamma_{j}\in F(X,K)
such that

{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j}\in{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))<{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(F(X)),

and
\{{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j},\;j\in J\}
is a generating set of

{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))
.

Observe that the condition that the given generating set contains the elements
{\rm ad}_{x},x\in X
is not restrictive since all these automorphisms preserve trivially
\widehat{B}
, since the latter is a tuple of conjugacy classes.

Proof.

By definition of
\widehat{B}
, the automorphisms of
F(X,K)
preserving
\widehat{B}
must preserve the conjugacy class of
F(X)
.
Therefore there exists
\gamma_{j}
such that
{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j}\in{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(F(X))
.
We may take
\gamma_{j}=1
for all the
\beta_{j}
that are of the form
{\rm ad}_{x},x\in X
.

Moreover, as we recalled, all inner automorphisms of
F(X,K)
preserve trivially
\widehat{B}
. It follows that

{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j}\in{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))<{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(F(X)).

It remains to prove that the obtained automorphisms
\{{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j},\;j\in J\}
generate the group

{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))
.

Let
\alpha_{j}={\rm ad}_{\gamma_{j}}\circ\beta_{j}
, to ease notations.

Consider an element
w
of
{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))
.
Since
\{\beta_{j},\;j\in J\}
is a generating set of
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})
, we may write
w
as

w=\beta_{a_{1}}\beta_{a_{2}}\dots\beta_{a_{s}}

with
a_{i}\in J
for all
i
. Consider than
w^{\prime}=\alpha_{a_{1}}\alpha_{a_{2}}\dots\alpha_{a_{s}}
. Substituting
\alpha_{a_{i}}
by
{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{a_{i}}}\circ\beta_{a_{i}}
and rearranging, it is elementary that there exists
\gamma
such that
w^{\prime}={\rm ad}_{\gamma}\circ w
.

On one hand,
w^{\prime}(F(S))=F(S)
because it is a composition of the
\alpha_{j}
. On the other hand,
w(F(S))=F(S)
by assumption on
w
. It follows that
{\rm ad}_{\gamma}
preserves
F(S)
. However, since
F(S)
is malnormal in
F(S,K)
, we can conclude that
\gamma\in F(S)
.

Since
{\rm ad}_{x},x\in X
are elements of the given generating set, by our choice of
\gamma_{j}
, they are also elements of
\{\alpha_{j},\;j\in J\}
. It is therefore possible to write

{\rm ad}_{\gamma}
as a word in the
\alpha_{j}
, and finally
{\rm ad}_{\gamma^{-1}}\circ w^{\prime}\in\langle\alpha_{j},\;j\in J\rangle
. Since
{\rm ad}_{\gamma^{-1}}\circ w^{\prime}=w
we have proved that
w\in\langle\alpha_{j}\;j\in J\rangle
.

We therefore have established the equality

{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))=\langle\alpha_{j}\;j\in J\rangle.

∎

Observe now the following.

Lemma 5.40
.

If
w\in{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))
,
then

w|_{F(X)}\in{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B).

If
w_{0}\in{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B)
,
then there exists

w\in{\rm Stab}_{{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X,K)\right)}}(\widehat{B})}(F(X))
,
for which
w|_{F(X)}=w_{0}
.

Proof.

The first part is a rewriting of the definitions, and for the second part, extend
w_{0}
as the identity on
K
.
∎

Corollary 5.41
.

Given
B
as in Proposition
5.39
, a generating set of the group
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B)

is computable.

Proof.

Construct
F(X,K)
and
\widehat{B}
, compute, by Gersten’s theorem
5.25
a generating set of
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B)
,
apply Proposition
5.39
, this provides, by Lemma
5.40
a generating set of
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathrm{Aut}\left(F(X)\right)}}(B)
.
∎

By Corollary
5.38
, and Corollary
5.41
, we obtain Theorem
5.26
, as promised.

6
Effective Minkowskian congruences

If
G
is a group,
P
is a characteristic subgroup of
G
if it is invariant by automorphisms of
G
. We then write
P\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}G
, and it induces a homomorphism
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/P\right)}
.

If
P_{0}
is a finite index subgroup of a finitely generated group
G
, by taking the intersection of all the (finitely many) subgroups of
G
of the same index as
P_{0}
, one obtains an associated characteristic finite index subgroup.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
P\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}G
be a finite index characteristic subgroup. We
say
P
-congruences separate the torsion of
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
if every finite order outer automorphism

[\alpha]\in{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
has non-trivial image
[\bar{\alpha}]
through the
natural map

{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/P\right)}.

In that case, the finite quotient
G/P
is a
Minkowskian congruence.

A class of groups is effectively Minkowskian if each group admits a finite Minkowskian congruence that is computable from a presentation.

The proposed terminology comes from a classical theorem of Minkowski: if
p\geq 3
, the kernel of the quotient
GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z})\to GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free. In other words
\mathbb{Z}^{n}/(3\mathbb{Z})^{n}
is a Minkowskian congruence for
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and free abelian groups form an effectively Minkowskian class.

The following is a useful remark.

Lemma 6.2
.

If
P\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}G

and
H\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}P
is a
characteristic subgroup of
P
, then

H\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}G
and if
P
-congruences separate the torsion in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
, then
H
-congruences separate the torsion in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}
.

Proof.

Any automorphism of
G
induces an automorphism of
P
, hence preserves
H
.
There is a natural map
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/H\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/P\right)}
which commutes with
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G\right)}\to{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/P\right)}
. Any finite order outer automorphism of
G
survives in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/P\right)}
, hence also in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G/H\right)}
.
∎

The aim of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 6.3
.

Finitely generated subgroups of piecewise trivial suspensions of free groups, are effectively Minkowskian.

Recall that we have two levels of decomposition for such a group, from top to bottom:

•

the Grushko decomposition in free product,

•

for each freely indecomposable free factor, the canonical decomposition given by Corollary
3.10
:
an acylindrical bipartite graph-of-groups with edge groups either cyclic or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and white vertex groups that are either finitely generated free or direct product of finitely generated free groups with an infinite cyclic group (a canonical trivially partially suspended decomposition).

By Proposition
4.9
such decompositions can be found effectively. In order to prove Theorem
6.3
we will show the two following theorems.

Theorem 6.4
.

A free product of freely indecomposable, residually finite, effectively Minkowskian, torsion-free groups and free groups is effectively Minkowskian.

Theorem 6.5
.

Any freely indecomposable group that has a canonical trivially partially suspended decomposition, as defined above, is effectively Minkowskian.

In the free product case, we will use Hensel and Kielak’s version of the Nielsen realization theorem for free products: a finite order automorphism of a free product is realized as a graph-of-group automorphism that takes advantage of a symmetry of a certain graph of groups and induces finite order outer automorphisms of the fixed vertex groups.

In the one-ended case (Theorem
6.5
), this is more involved. We will first construct a clean covering of the given graph of groups, inspired by
[
Wis00
]
. The cover will be taken to be characteristic so that automorphisms of the ambient group
\Gamma
induce automorphisms of the finite-index subgroup
\Xi
associated with the cover.

Then we will take a quotient of
\Xi
, which we will call a vertex filling, to obtain a virtually free group
V
, which is a graph of finite groups over the same underlying graph, in which sufficiently many witnesses of the non-triviality of the finite order outer automorphisms will have survived.

Finally, we will take a quotient of
V
to a finite group
Q_{0}
, in which all the conjugacy classes of finite order elements remain disjoint. By studying the images of the witnesses in the induced quotient
Q
of
\Gamma
, we will finally establish that
Q
is in fact a Minkowskian congruence, thus giving us the desired result.

6.1
Effective Minkowskian property for free products

In this section, we prove Theorem
6.4
. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{k}
be non-trivial, torsion-free freely indecomposable groups that are effectively Minkowskian and residually finite.
The group
L_{r}
is a free group of rank
r
. We consider the free product
\Gamma=G_{1}*G_{2}\dots G_{k}*L_{r}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

There exists a finite list of finite quotients of
\Gamma
such that,
for each automorphism
\alpha
of
\Gamma
that is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
and has finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, there is
\alpha^{\prime}
conjugate to
\alpha
in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, a finite quotient
\Gamma\to\bar{\Gamma}
in the list, whose kernel is preserved by
\alpha^{\prime}
, and on which
\alpha^{\prime}
descends as an automorphism
\bar{\alpha^{\prime}}\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\bar{\Gamma}\right)}
whose outer class is non-trivial (in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\bar{\Gamma}\right)}
).

We will distinguish several cases according to the properties of
\alpha
. First, observe that
\alpha
must permute (and can possibly preserve) the conjugacy classes of the groups
G_{i}
. For each
i
, let
G_{i}\to\overline{G_{i}}^{M}
be a Minkowskian finite quotient.

Lemma 6.7
.

If there is an index
i\leq k
, and
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
such that
\alpha(G_{i})^{\gamma_{i}}=G_{i}
, and if
{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{i}}\circ\alpha|_{G_{i}}:G_{i}\to G_{i}
defines a non-trivial outer automorphism of
G_{i}
, then the quotient

\Gamma\to G_{i}\to\overline{G_{i}}^{M}

satisfies the conclusion of Proposition
6.6
for
\alpha
.

Proof.

Observe that
{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{i}}\circ\alpha
is the same outer class as
\alpha
, hence has finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
. It follows that in its restriction to
G_{i}
(that it preserves) it has finite order up to conjugation by an element normalizing
G_{i}
, thus in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(G_{i}\right)}
. By assumption, it is non-trivial. Applying the Minkowski property for
G_{i}
, it survives in the quotient
\overline{G_{i}}^{M}
.
∎

In the next lemma, consider for each
i
a non-trivial finite quotient by a characteristic subgroup
G_{i}\to\bar{G_{i}}^{c}
. This exists by residual finiteness, and one chooses them so that
\bar{G_{i}}^{c}\simeq\bar{G_{j}}^{c}
if and only if
G_{i}\simeq G_{j}
.

Construct the free product quotient of
\Gamma
:

\Gamma\to\overline{\Gamma}^{vf}\;=\;\bar{G_{1}}^{c}*\bar{G_{2}}^{c}*\dots\bar{G_{k}}^{c}*L_{r}.

Observe that
\overline{\Gamma}^{vf}
is virtually free. Make the further quotient to a finite group

\overline{\Gamma}^{vf}\to\overline{\Gamma}^{dp}\;=\;\bar{G_{1}}^{c}\times\bar{G_{2}}^{c}\times\dots\bar{G_{k}}^{c}.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\alpha
be an automorphism of
\Gamma
with an index
i
for which for which
[\alpha(G_{i})]\neq[G_{i}]
. Then
\alpha
descends to the quotient
\overline{\Gamma}^{dp}
as a non-trivial outer automorphism.

Proof.

First, it is clear that all automorphisms of
\Gamma
descend to
\overline{\Gamma}^{dp}
.
Let
g_{i}\in G_{i}
that survives in
\bar{G_{i}}^{c}
. Then
\bar{\alpha}
sends
\bar{g}_{i}
to a non-trivial element of another direct factor, hence a non-conjugate element; this proves the claim.
∎

Convention 6.9
.

From now on,
\alpha
is an automorphism of
\Gamma
that is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, and of finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, and such that, for all indices
i
, there exists
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
for which
{\rm ad}_{\gamma_{i}}\circ\alpha|_{G_{i}}:G_{i}\to G_{i}
is the identity of
G_{i}
.

To make sense of such
\alpha
we need the following statement.

Lemma 6.10
.

There are only finitely many conjugacy classes of elements as given in Convention
6.9
in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
.

Moreover, for each such
\alpha
, there exists
L^{\prime}_{r}<\Gamma
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that

•

\Gamma
splits as a free product
G_{1}*\dots G_{k}*L^{\prime}_{r}
,

•

{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha(L^{\prime}_{r})=L^{\prime}_{r}

•

{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha|_{L^{\prime}_{r}}
is non-trivial and of finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(L^{\prime}_{r}\right)}
.

Proof.

By
[
HK18
, Corollary 6.1]
all such outer automorphisms must fix a barycentre of a simplex in the relative outer-space of the free product. In other words, the group
\Gamma
splits as a graph of groups with trivial edge groups and infinite vertex groups that are conjugate to
G_{i}
, in such a way that
\alpha
induces an automorphism of graph of groups.

This allows us to write an element
{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha
of the outer class of
\alpha
, as a graph-of-group automorphism of some fixed free-splitting graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
. Let it be
{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha=(\alpha_{X},\alpha_{v},\alpha_{e},\gamma_{e})
. By assumption on
\alpha
, one may take
\alpha_{v}=Id
for all vertices and
\alpha_{e}=Id
for all edges as well. Also, the graph automorphism
\alpha_{X}
must induce the trivial permutation on the non-free vertices of the graph (i.e. those that are attached to a non-trivial group).

Write
{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
to ease notation.

It follows that
(\alpha^{\prime})^{s}=(\alpha_{X}^{s},(Id_{v})_{v},(Id_{e})_{e},(\gamma_{e}^{s})_{e})
. Since
\gamma_{e}
has infinite order,
3
3

3

The result would hold with little modification if each free factor of
\Gamma
had only finitely many torsion elements – in particular for the free product of finite groups

if
[\alpha^{m}]=[Id]
, then
\gamma_{e}=1
.

Thus
{\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma}}\circ\alpha=(\alpha_{X},Id_{v},Id_{e},1)
. Let
L^{\prime}_{r}
be the free factor of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})
defined by the graph
X
. We see that
L^{\prime}_{r}
is preserved by
\alpha^{\prime}
, and that
\alpha^{\prime}
induces a finite order automorphism of
L^{\prime}_{r}
, which is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(L^{\prime}_{r}\right)}
(otherwise
\alpha^{\prime}
would be trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
.

It follows that there cannot be more conjugacy classes of automorphisms
\alpha
(in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
) than the number of orbits of cells in the relative outer space multiplied by the maximal order of graph automorphism groups of thus appearing graphs.
∎

We may thus proceed to analyze the automorphisms
\alpha
satisfying Convention
6.9
one by one.

Lemma 6.11
.

If
\alpha
is an automorphism satisfying Convention
6.9
, then there exists a finite quotient of
\Gamma
in which
\alpha
descends as a non-trivial outer automorphism.

Proof.

Taking the notations of the previous lemma, it suffices to notice that there is a finite quotient of
L^{\prime}_{r}
on which
\alpha^{\prime}
descends as a non-trivial outer automorphism. This is the Minkowskian property for free groups, proved in
[
DT21
, Corollary 3.2]

∎

We thus showed Lemma
6.11
that all automorphisms satisfying the convention
6.9
are separated in a finite quotient, and Lemma
6.10
, that there are only finitely many conjugacy classes of them. We also showed (Lemma
6.7
,
6.8
) that there are finitely many finite quotients separating all finite order outer automorphisms that do not satisfy the convention.

Proposition
6.6
is thus established.

It is therefore sufficient to prove the following, in order to obtain Theorem
6.4
. Let
\alpha\in{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
and let
K\leq\Gamma
be a finite index subgroup. If
\alpha([K])=[K]
and
\alpha
descends to a non-trivial outer automorphism of
\Gamma/K
then we say
K
separates
\alpha
.

Lemma 6.12
.

If
K_{1}
separates
\alpha_{1}
and
K_{2}
separates
\alpha_{2}
, then there is a subgroup
K
that separates both
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
.

Proof.

We will consider
K
to be the intersection of all images of
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
by automorphisms (it is a finite intersection of subgroups sharing the same index in
\Gamma
). To show that
K
is as required, we need examine to how
\alpha_{1}
survives in
\Gamma/K
. First it induces an automorphism of
\Gamma/K
since the quotient is characteristic. Second,
\Gamma/K_{1}
is a quotient of
\Gamma/K
on which
\alpha_{1}
descends non-trivially non-trivially as an outer automorphism, thus it cannot be a trivial outer automorphism in
\Gamma/K
.
∎

6.2
Effective Minkowskian property for one-ended groups with canonical splittings.

In this section, we consider the Minkowskian property for groups for which a canonical decomposition as a graph-of-groups
\mathbb{X}
is given. In that case, all outer automorphisms can be expressed as elements of
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
, thus we clarify a possible congruence strategy to separate the torsion in
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
.

6.2.1
From graphs-of-groups to vertex groups

The following Proposition isolates two origins of torsion in
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
, either witnessed by permuting vertices or by restricting to a non-trivial finite order outer automorphism of a vertex group.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be the fundamental group of a
graph of group
\mathbb{X}
, and let
\phi=(\phi_{X},(\phi_{v})_{v},(\phi_{e})_{e},(\gamma_{e})_{e})\in\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
be non-trivial and of finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
. Suppose furthermore that for each non-abelian vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, the quotient by its centre is torsion-free.

Then if
\phi_{X}=Id
, for at least one vertex
v
,
\phi_{v}
defines a non-trivial, finite order element in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\mathbb{X}_{v}\right)}
.

Proof.

Assume the contrary: all
\phi_{v}
must be of the form
{\rm ad}_{g_{v}}
.
We choose a preferred vertex
v_{0}
, with non-abelian vertex group, and identify
\Gamma
with
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})
. After composition with an inner automorphism of
\Gamma
, we may assume that
g_{v_{0}}=1
, that is
\phi_{v_{0}}=Id
.

Since
\phi_{X}
is the identity, in the Bass group each edge
e
is sent, by
\phi
, on
\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}e\gamma_{e}
.

If we choose
\tau
a maximal subtree of
X
, one may change the expression of
\phi
without changing its image in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, so that, for each edge
e
in
\tau
pointing outward from
v_{0}
, the element
\gamma_{e}
is trivial. To see this, enumerate the edges in
\tau
respectively to the distance to
v_{0}
and modify iteratively as follows: for a given
e
, change
\gamma_{e}
by
1
, change
g_{\tau(e)}
by
g_{\tau(e)}\gamma_{e}^{-1}
, and all
\gamma_{\bar{e}^{\prime}}
for
e^{\prime}
exiting
\tau(e)
by
\gamma_{\bar{e}^{\prime}}\gamma_{e}^{-1}
(this was already done for
e^{\prime}=\bar{e}
!). It is an immediate computation to see that the Bass diagrams are satisfied, and the automorphism on
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})
is the same.
(For that, note that, in the Bass group, the element
ehe^{\prime}
is sent on
\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{-1}e\gamma_{e}g_{\tau(e)}^{-1}\,h\,g_{\tau(e)}\,\gamma_{e}^{-1}\gamma_{e}\,\gamma_{\bar{e}^{\prime}}^{-1}e^{\prime}\gamma_{e^{\prime}}
.)

If all
\phi_{v}
are the identity, then, since all the
\gamma_{e}
are trivial or have infinite order, it follows that
\phi
is trivial or has infinite order.

Consider
v_{1}
closest to
v_{0}
(in
\tau
) such that
\phi_{v_{1}}
is not the identity. It is
{\rm ad}_{g_{v_{1}}}
for some
g_{v_{1}}
non-central in
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
(in particular
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
is non-abelian). By assumption, no power of
g_{v_{1}}
is central. Let
p=e_{1}\dots e_{k}
be a path from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
in
\tau
, and consider

\gamma(g)=g_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{k}g\bar{e}_{k}\dots\bar{e}_{1}g^{\prime}_{0}

for
g\in\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
, and
g_{0},g^{\prime}_{0}
not in adjacent edge groups and not inverse of each other (which is possible by choice of
v_{0}
).

Using that
\gamma_{e_{i}}=1
, one can compute that the image of
\gamma(g)
by
\phi^{n}
is

\phi^{n}(\gamma(g))\;=\;g_{0}\,x_{1,n}\,e_{1}\,x_{2,n}\,e_{2}\dots x_{k,n}e_{k}\,g^{g_{v_{1}}^{n}}\,\bar{e}_{k}\,x_{k,n}^{-1}\,\bar{e}_{k-1}\dots x_{1,n}^{-1}\,g^{\prime}_{0}

where the
x_{i,n}=(\gamma_{\bar{e_{i}}})^{-n}
are in the vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{\tau(e_{i-1})}
.

For all
n
,
g_{v_{1}}^{n}
is, by assumption, non-central in its vertex group, therefore we may find

g
outside the centralizer of
g_{v_{1}}^{n}
,
so that

g^{g_{v_{1}}^{n}}
is different from
g
. It follows that, since the expression of
\phi^{n}(\gamma(g))
is in normal form,
that
\phi^{n}
is non-trivial. Since it is trivial on
\mathbb{X}_{v_{0}}
, but non-trivial on
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
it is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
. This is in contradiction with the assumption that
\phi
has finite order.
∎

In order to separate the torsion in the outer automorphism group, we will need particular quotients of our graphs of groups. The first step is to quotient the vertex groups to reach a virtually free group, which we achieve in Proposition
6.17
. This will be achieved in two steps, first producing a clean cover of the graph of groups (Proposition
6.14
), and second, filling appropriately the vertex groups. We will then proceed to use this quotient to separate conjugacy classes (Proposition
6.20
).

6.2.2
Clean covers and vertex fillings

Recall that an element of a free group

\mathbb{F}
is said to be
primitive
if belongs to some basis
of
\mathbb{F}
, or equivalently if it generates a cyclic free factor in some free product decomposition.

We consider graphs of groups in which vertex groups are either free groups or direct products of free groups with
\mathbb{Z}
, and edge groups are either cyclic or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and maximal, as in Corollary
3.10
. For short we will call them trivially partially suspended.

For such a graph-of-groups
\mathbb{X}
, we denote by
\mathbb{X}_{v}=\mathbb{F}_{v}\times\langle t_{v}\rangle
, with
\mathbb{F}_{v}
free, and
t_{v}
either of infinite order or trivial.

Following
[
Wis00
]
, we say that a graph-of-groups in this class is
clean
if, for all edges
e
, the intersection
\mathbb{F}_{\tau(e)}\cap\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
is a cyclic free factor in
\mathbb{F}_{\tau(e)}
.

Proposition 6.14
.

For any trivially partially suspended graph-of-groups (as defined above), there exists a computable characteristic finite index subgroup
\Gamma_{0}
of
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})
such that the graph of groups decomposition
\mathbb{Y}
of
\Gamma_{0}
given by its action on the Bass-Serre tree dual to
\mathbb{X}
is trivially partially suspended, and clean.

We will follow Wise’s argument for graphs of free groups, in
[
Wis00
]
. Before proving the proposition, we first need a few lemmas.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}
be a collection of elements of a free group

\mathbb{F}
, then there is a computable finite index normal subgroup
K\leq\mathbb{F}

such that each for any
f\in\mathbb{F}
the
intersection
K\cap\langle c_{i}^{f}\rangle
is a free factor of
K
.

Proof.

By the Marshall Hall Theorem
[
Hal49
]
for

i=1,\ldots,k
there are finite index subgroups
K_{i}\leq\mathbb{F}

such that
K_{i}\cap{\left\langle c_{i}\right\rangle}
is primitive. Since
[\mathbb{F}:K_{i}]<\infty
, the intersection

K=\bigcap_{f\in\mathbb{F},\\
i\in\{1,\dots k\}}K_{i}^{f}

only involves finitely many terms so it is a finite index normal
subgroup.

For each
i
and
f
, the group
\langle c_{i}^{f}\rangle\cap K_{i}^{f}
is a free factor in
K_{i}^{f}
, hence maximal-elliptic in some Bass-Serre
K_{i}^{f}
-tree associated to a free product of cyclic groups decomposing
K_{i}^{f}
. Therefore
\langle c_{i}^{f}\rangle\cap K
is maximal-elliptic in this tree and is a cyclic free factor of
K
.
∎

Let
K_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{f.i.}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{F}_{v}
be a finite
index subgroup and let
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
such that
\tau(e)=v
. In our notations,

\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\cap F_{n}={\left\langle c_{e}\right\rangle}
. Analogously to

[
Wis00
]
, we call each
K_{v}
-conjugacy class of a non-trivial intersection
with a conjugate
K_{v}\cap{\left\langle c_{e}^{f}\right\rangle}
an
e
-elevation
we
denote the set of
e
-elevations as

\mathrm{Elev}_{K_{v}}\left(e\right)=\{{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}\mid\exists f\in F_{v},{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}=K_{v}\cap{\left\langle c_{e}^{f}\right\rangle}\}.

Given an
e
-elevation
{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}\in\mathrm{Elev}_{K_{v}}\left(e\right)
we define its
length
to be the number
\ell_{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}

such that

\gamma^{f}=c_{e}^{\ell_{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}}

for some
f\in F_{v}
. An easy finite degree covering space
argument gives the following:

Lemma 6.16

(Index and lengths)
.

The sum of the lengths of elevations of an edge equals the
index. That is to say, if
\tau(e)=v

then

\sum_{[{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}]_{K_{v}}\in\mathrm{Elev}_{K_{v}}\left(e\right)}\ell_{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}=[\mathbb{F}_{v}:K_{v}].

We may now prove Proposition
6.14

Proof.

For each
v
, by Lemma
6.15
, there exists a
P_{v}
a normal subgroup of finite index in
\mathbb{F}_{v}
such that every elevation of adjacent edge group is a cyclic free factor in
P_{v}
. Let
l_{\gamma}
the length of the elevation
{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}
of an edge group, and
l_{0}
the least common multiple of all the
l_{\gamma}
, ranging over the images of all edge groups in all vertex groups. We apply Wise’s omnipotence Theorem
[
Wis00
, Theorem 3.5]
to the collection of conjugacy classes of the generators
\gamma
of elevations
{\left\langle\gamma\right\rangle}
: there exists a constant
L_{0}
such that for each tuple of elevations of edge groups, there exists a finite quotient of
P_{v}
in which the generator
\gamma
has order
L_{0}\times l_{0}/l_{\gamma}
. Let
K_{v}\leq P_{v}
be the kernel of this finite quotient. By choice of constants, viewing every
K_{v}\leq{\mathbb{F}(v)}
, we have that all elevations have length
L_{0}\times l_{0}=L
.

Now
\mathbb{X}_{v}
either has an infinite centre but isn’t cyclic, in which case it is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{F}(x)}\times{\left\langle t_{v}\right\rangle}
, or
\mathbb{X}_{v}
is a subgroup of a free group. In all cases,
t_{v}
is a generator of the centre of
\mathbb{X}_{v}
if
\mathbb{X}_{v}
isn’t cyclic and we allow
t_{v}=1
if
\mathbb{X}_{v}
is a subgroup of a free group. We consider the subgroup
H_{v}={\left\langle K_{v},t_{v}^{L}\right\rangle}\leq\mathbb{X}_{v}
. It has finite index in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
, and its non-trivial intersections with each conjugate of an edge group
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})^{g}
, for
g\in\mathbb{X}_{v}
, is
L\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})^{g}
(in the notation of abelian groups, as are the
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})^{g}
). In particular, each intersection is a characteristic subgroup of
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})^{g}
.

We will now construct the graph of groups
\mathbb{Y}
with the required properties by constructing a covering space of
\mathcal{X}
a standard graph of spaces underlying
\mathbb{X}
as in
[
SW79
]
. Recall that
X
is the graph underlying
\mathbb{X}
.
For each vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
let
E_{v}=\frac{[F_{v}:K_{v}]}{L}
, and for each edge
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
we have, by Lemma
6.16
, that if
i(e)=v
and
\tau(e)=i(\bar{e})=w
then

|\mathrm{Elev}_{K_{v}}\left(e\right)|=E_{v}\textrm{~and~}|\mathrm{Elev}_{K_{w}}\left(\bar{e}\right)|=E_{w}

For each vertex
v
, let

d_{v}=\frac{\mathrm{lcm}\left(E_{w},w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}\right)}{E_{v}},

so that for any
u,v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
we have

d_{u}E_{u}=d_{v}E_{v}.

We thus define a graph
Y
by taking as a set of vertices
d_{v}
copies of the vertex
v\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
, for each
v
. We call them lifts of
v
.

For each pair
(u,v)
related by an edge
\{e,\bar{e}\}
in
X
we thus define, as edges of
Y
,
d_{u}E_{u}
copies of the edge
\{e,\bar{e}\}
, attached to the vertices that are lifts of
u
and
v
so that there are
E_{u}
edges attached to each of the lifts of
u
, and
E_{v}
attached to each of the lifts of
v
. Since
d_{u}E_{u}=d_{v}E_{v}
is it possible to pair the edges.

Each lift of a vertex
u
is endowed with a copy of the group
H_{u}
. Each lift of an edge
e
is endowed with the group
L\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})
. The attaching maps are then natural, considering the elevations of the edges groups of
\mathbb{X}
in our set of vertex groups.

This thus defines a graph of groups
\mathbb{Y}
. It is easy to see that there is a natural homomorphism
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{Y},u_{0})\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},u)
for
u_{0}
a lift of
u
, and that it is injective. A classical consideration of graph of spaces associated to
\mathbb{Y}
, covering a graph of spaces associated to
\mathbb{X}
with
d_{u_{0}}E_{u_{0}}
-sheets, reveals that the image has finite index (see also
[
SW79
,
Wis00
]
).
Finally, intersecting the conjugates of the image of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{Y},u_{0})
gives a characteristic finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},u)
that is still clean, and trivially partially suspended.

∎

Proposition 6.17

(Virtually free vertex fillings)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a trivially partially suspended graph-of-groups, with conventions of notation as above.

Assume furthermore that
\mathbb{X}
is clean, and for each edge
e
, let
c_{e}
be a generator of
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\cap\mathbb{F}_{e}
(hence a primitive element).

Let, for each
v
,
N_{v}
be a finite index subgroup of
\mathbb{F}_{v}
.

Then, for each vertex
v
, there is a computable characteristic finite quotient
p_{v}:\mathbb{X}_{v}\to Q_{v}
for which
\ker(p_{v})\cap\mathbb{F}_{v}
is in
N_{v}
, and such that:
for each edge
e
, the maps
t:\mathbb{X}_{e}\to\mathbb{X}_{\tau(e)}\to Q_{\tau(e)}
and
i_{e}:\mathbb{X}_{e}\to\mathbb{X}_{i(e)}\to Q_{i(e)}
both factorize through a finite quotient
\mathbb{X}_{e}\to Q_{e}
that is isomorphic to the images in
Q_{\tau(e)}
and
Q_{i(e)}
.

In particular, there is a (computable) graph-of-group homomorphism
\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{X}_{Q}
, obtained by substituting the vertex and edge groups by
Q_{v},Q_{e}
, for which
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X}_{Q},v_{0})
is virtually free.

The argument presented below is taken from the proof of

[
CBW12
, Lemma 4.2]
.

Proof.

First, after possibly taking the intersection in
\mathbb{F}_{v}
of the conjugates of
N_{v}
, we may assume that each
N_{v}
is normal.

Consider the free product

\mathbb{F}_{*}=\bigast_{w\in{{\textbf{V}(X)}}}\mathbb{F}_{w}

which is itself a free group. Consider
\ker(\mathbb{F}_{*}\to\mathbb{F}_{v}\to\mathbb{F}_{v}/N_{n})
which is a finite index subgroup, take the intersection
K_{0}
of all of them for
v
ranging in
{{\textbf{V}(X)}}
, and take a characteristic finite index subgroup of
K_{0}
. It intersects each
\mathbb{F}_{v}
inside
N_{v}
.

We claim that there is
N
such that, for each vertex
v
, and for each incident edge group
e
,
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\cap K_{0}
has index index
N
in
\tau_{e}(\mathbb{X}_{e})\cap\mathbb{F}_{v}
.

We now note that by hypothesis on
\Xi=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X})
,
for any
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}
the generator
c_{e}
of

t(\mathbb{X}_{e})\cap\mathbb{F}_{\tau(e)}

is a primitive element of
\mathbb{F}_{\tau(e)}
, hence of
\mathbb{F}_{*}
as well. It, therefore, follows that for
any pair
e,f\in{{\textbf{E}(Y)}}
there is an automorphism

\phi\in{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{F}_{*}\right)}
such that
\phi(c_{e})=c_{f}
. Since

K_{0}\triangleleft_{\mathrm{c}}\mathbb{F}
is characteristic, the order of
c_{e}
and of
c_{f}
in the quotient
\mathbb{F}_{*}/K_{0}
are the same: there exists some

N\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for each
e\in{{\textbf{E}(X)}}

K\cap{\left\langle c_{e}\right\rangle}=c_{e}^{N}.

Recall that according to our notations, for each vertex
v
,
\mathbb{X}_{v}=\mathbb{F}_{v}\times\langle t_{v}\rangle
, with
t_{v}
of infinite order if
\mathbb{X}_{v}
is not free, and with
t_{v}=1
if
\mathbb{X}_{v}
is free.

Setting
K_{v}=K\cap\mathbb{F}_{v}\triangleleft\mathbb{F}_{v}
, and
Q_{v}=\mathbb{X}_{v}/(K_{v}\times\langle t_{v}^{N}\rangle)
, and
Q_{e}=\mathbb{X}_{e}/N\mathbb{X}_{e}
(as abelian groups) produces the desired graph of groups
\mathbb{X}_{Q}
, with all the natural attaching maps.

∎

6.2.3
Separation in virtually free vertex fillings

Show that certain conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
are separated in a preferred, characteristic, virtually free vertex filling of
\mathbb{X}
.
For that, we begin by expressing the combined effect of clean covers and vertex filling.

Proposition
6.18
below is of interest and close to what we need. However, we will need a refinement that is harder to parse, but of a similar spirit, that implies this proposition. We state the easier result in order to indicate the direction we are taking.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
\Gamma
be the fundamental group of a trivially partially suspended graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
. For any pair
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
of non-conjugate elements of
\Gamma
that are in vertex groups, but not conjugate to images of edge groups,
there exists a normal (characteristic) finite index subgroup
\Xi
of
\Gamma
,

m\geq 1
such that
\gamma_{1}^{m},\gamma_{2}^{m}
are in
\Xi
, and
there exists a virtually free quotient
V
of
\Xi
in which the elements
\gamma_{1}^{m}
and
(\gamma_{2}^{m})\in\Xi
map on non-conjugate elements of
V
.

As mentioned this statement, however archetypal, is not sufficient for our needs. Recall that prior to the proof of Proposition
3.16
we defined the
peripheral structure
of a vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v}
of a graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
to be the collection in
\mathbb{X}_{v}
of conjugates of the images of the incident edge groups. We will define a
peripheral automorphism
to be an automorphism of
\mathbb{X}_{v}
that preserves the peripheral structure. We will need a single quotient that separates multiple pairs simultaneously. For that, we need the following definition.

Definition 6.19
.

In a graph of group
\mathbb{X}
, equipped with a characteristic
quotient
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{X},v_{0})\to Q
, a pair of elements
g_{1},g_{2}
belonging to vertex groups
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}},\mathbb{X}_{v_{2}}
but not conjugate into the images of the incident edge groups, is said to be
strongly
Q
-conjugacy-separated
if

•

if
v_{1}=v_{2}
, then any of the pairs
(g^{\prime}_{1},g^{\prime}_{2})
that are images of
(g_{1},g_{2})
by a peripheral automorphism of the vertex group
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}}
, maps to a pair
(\bar{g}^{\prime}_{1},\bar{g}^{\prime}_{2})
of non-conjugate elements in
Q
,

•

if
v_{1}\neq v_{2}
, then any of their images by a pair of peripheral automorphisms of
\mathbb{X}_{v_{1}},\mathbb{X}_{v_{2}}
, maps to a pair of non-conjugate elements in
Q
.

Here is the result we want to establish.

Proposition 6.20
.

Let
\Gamma
be the fundamental group of a partially suspended graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
. For any pair
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
of non-conjugate elements of
\Gamma
that are in vertex groups, but not conjugate to images of edge groups,
there exists a computable characteristic finite index subgroup
\Xi
of
\Gamma
,

m\geq 1
such that
\gamma_{1}^{m},\gamma_{2}^{m}
are in
\Xi
, and
there exists a virtually free quotient
\Xi\to V
of
\Xi
in which, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the
pair
(\gamma_{1}^{m},(\gamma_{2}^{m})^{\gamma})
of elements of
\Xi
is strongly
V
-conjugacy-separated.

Proof.

Let
\Xi
be a characteristic (normal) finite index subgroup corresponding to a clean cover
\mathbb{Y}
of
\mathbb{X}
, as provided by Proposition
6.14
.

Consider
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
as in the statement. Since
\Xi
is of finite index, there exists an integer
m\geq 1
such that
\gamma_{1}^{m},\gamma_{2}^{m}
are in
\Xi
. Note that the conjugacy class of
\gamma_{2}^{m}
in
\Gamma
is contained in
\Xi
(by normality), and breaks into finitely many
\Xi
-conjugacy classes, given by
(\gamma_{2}^{m})^{\mu}\in\Xi
, for
\mu
ranging over a set
M
of representatives of right cosets of
\Xi
in
\Gamma
. Since
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are not conjugate in
\Gamma
, none of the
(\gamma_{2}^{m})^{\mu}
is conjugate to
\gamma_{1}
in
\Xi
.

We are looking for a virtually free vertex filling of
\mathbb{Y}
that strongly separates, for each
\mu\in M
, the conjugacy classes of
\gamma_{1}^{m}
and of
(\gamma_{2}^{m})^{\mu}
, and any of their automorphic images. Since there are only finitely many conjugacy classes
(\gamma_{2}^{m})^{\mu}
, we may argue independently for each of them, in order to find finite index subgroups in the vertex groups that suitably define a vertex filling, and then take their finite intersection in order to obtain a vertex filling suitable for each of them.
This reduces our study to the following statement, in which the notation is simplified.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
\mathbb{Y}
be a clean trivially partially suspended graph of groups, and
g_{0}
an element of a vertex group
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{0}}
, and
let
g_{1}
be an element of a vertex group
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{1}}
, such that neither
g_{i}
is conjugate to the other, neither to an adjacent edge group.

Then there exists a computable virtually free vertex filling of
\mathbb{Y}
that strongly separates the pair
(g_{0},g_{1})
.

Proof.

We distinguish whether
g_{1}
is in the same vertex group as
g_{0}
or not.

Assume first that they are in the same vertex group
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
.
Virtually free groups are conjugacy separable, in particular, one can separate the conjugacy class of all the elements of the vertex groups in finite quotients
[
Dye79
]
4
4

4

Stebe
[
Ste70
]
, Remeslennikov
[
Rem72
]
, and Wehrfritz
[
Weh73
]
proved with different methods the conjugacy separability of infinite order elements, while Dyer
[
Dye79
]
treated the case of finite order elements in virtually free groups, which is the case we are using.
.
Therefore, there exists a normal subgroup
N_{v}\triangleleft\mathbb{Y}_{v}
such that their image in
\mathbb{Y}_{v}/N_{n}

are still non-conjugate. It can be computed by enumeration. We choose
N_{v}
characteristic by taking the intersection of its images by automorphisms of
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
. Since it is an intersection of given index subgroups, it is finite index. Now, all automorphisms of
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
descend to the quotient
\mathbb{Y}_{v}\to\mathbb{Y}_{v}/N_{v}
. Therefore, since
g_{0},g_{1}
are conjugacy separated in one quotient, all the
\psi(g_{0}),\psi(g_{1})
, for
\psi
an automorphism of
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
, are conjugacy separated in
\mathbb{Y}_{v}/N_{v}
.
Applying Proposition
6.17
, one gets a virtually free quotient of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{Y},v_{0})
in which
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
maps on a group that still quotient further on
\mathbb{Y}_{v}/N_{n}
. The pair
(g_{0},g_{1})
has thus been strongly separated in a single virtually free vertex filling.

Assume that
g_{0},g_{1}
are not conjugate in the same vertex groups of
\mathbb{Y}
(but are respectively in vertex groups
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{0}},\mathbb{Y}_{v_{1}}
of
\mathbb{Y}
). By assumption, they are not conjugate in the edge groups adjacent to
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
.

By a result of Wilson and Zalesskii
[
WZ98
, Prop 2.5]
, finitely generated subgroups of virtually free groups are conjugacy distinguished: given two (or finitely many) of them that are non-conjugate, there is a finite quotient in which they remain non-conjugate. It can be computed by enumeration.

Applying this result to the two direct products
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{0}}
,
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{1}}
, their adjacent edge groups and the group generated by the elements
g_{0},g_{i}
, there exist finite quotients of
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{0}}
and of
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{1}}
, in which
g_{0}
is not conjugate in the images of any of the adjacent edge groups, and similarly for
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{i}}
. Let
N^{\prime}_{v_{0}},N^{\prime}_{v_{1}}
the respective kernels of these quotients, and
N_{v_{0}},N_{v_{1}}
the intersection of all their images by peripheral automorphisms. We can now apply Proposition
6.17
, to get a virtually free quotient of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{Y},v_{0})
, that has a graph of group decomposition with finite vertex groups, in which the images of
g_{0}
and
g_{i}
are in two distinct vertex groups, and in no adjacent edge group. This ensures that they are not conjugate in this virtually free quotient. A similar argument using the property that automorphisms of
\mathbb{Y}_{v_{i}}
commute with the quotient
\mathbb{Y}_{i}\to\mathbb{Y}_{i}/N_{v_{i}}
ensures that the same is true for all the pairs obtained as images by such automorphisms of vertex groups.

Hence, the pair
(g_{0},g_{1})
is strongly separated in the obtained virtually free vertex filling.

∎

As already mentioned, the Lemma ensures the Proposition.
∎

We end with the following corollary.
Recall that by the Nielsen Realization Theorem, each finitely generated free group, and immediately each direct product of a finitely generated free group with a cyclic group has only finitely many conjugacy classes of outer automorphisms of finite order.

By a result of Grossman
[
Gro74
]
, (see also Lubotzky
[
Lub80
]
), pointwise-inner automorphisms of a free group are inner. It follows that pointwise inner automorphisms of direct products of free groups with a cyclic group are inner. Hence for each non-trivial outer automorphism of a group that is free or a direct product of free and cyclic, there exists a conjugacy class of elements (actually infinitely many) that is sent on another by the automorphism.
Call them witnesses of non-triviality.

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
\Gamma
be the fundamental group of a trivially partially suspended graph of groups
\mathbb{X}
, let
\mathbb{Y}
be a characteristic clean cover of
\mathbb{X}
,
\Xi
its fundamental group, and
M
a set of right coset representatives of
\Xi
in
\Gamma
.

For each white vertex group
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
of
\mathbb{Y}
, let
\mathcal{S}^{0}_{v}
consist of a finite set of witnesses for a representative of each conjugacy class of non-trivial, finite order automorphism, that are in addition, not conjugate in any adjacent edge group. We choose
\mathcal{S}_{v}^{0}
in such a way that if
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Y}_{w}
by an isomorphism preserving the peripheral structure of the adjacent edge groups, then there is such an isomorphism sending
\mathcal{S}_{v}^{0}
to
\mathcal{S}_{w}^{0}
.

For each white vertex
v
in
Y
, let
\mathcal{S}_{v}
be the set of images of
\mathcal{S}_{v}^{0}
by the group of peripheral
automorphisms of
\mathbb{Y}_{v}
(for the edge groups structure).

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the union of the
\mathcal{S}_{v}
.

Then there exists a computable characteristic vertex filling virtually free group in which the elements of
\mathcal{S}
, and their conjugates by elements of
M
map on non-trivial torsion elements, and such that whenever two of them are non-conjugate in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{Y},v_{0})
, they are not conjugate in the virtually free quotient.

Proof.

There are only finitely many orbits (by automorphisms of vertex groups) of elements in
\mathcal{S}
, therefore one may apply Proposition
6.20
for each pair of those, and obtain a vertex filling suitable for this pair. Take the intersection of the kernels of these finitely many fillings. It is of finite index in each vertex group. Take the intersection of all images by automorphisms: it is still of finite index in each vertex group since it is an intersection of same index subgroups, and it is now characteristic. Take the quotient by this intersection. It quotients further on each of the vertex fillings.
Therefore, it strongly separate every pair, and is still a vertex filling by finite index subgroups in the vertex groups. Hence it is suitable for the conclusion.
∎

6.2.4
Separation of many conjugacy classes

The following observation does not depend on the previous context, but since it will be applied to our previous construction, we take matching notations. The argument
was inspired by
[
CBW12
, Lemmas 2.7, 3.1]
, but the arguments of the previous section let us avoid needing to deploy profinite machinery.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated group,
\Xi<\Gamma
a characteristic subgroup,
\Xi\to V
a characteristic virtually free quotient of
\Xi
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{V}
be the (finite) set of conjugacy classes of elements of finite order in
V
.

If
\xi\in\Xi
, we denote by
[\xi]_{V}
the
V
-conjugacy class of its image in the quotient
V
. We will be interested in elements that map on finite order elements in
V
, thus defining this way an element in
\mathcal{C}_{V}
.

Let
M
be a set of right coset representatives of
\Xi
in
\Gamma
.

Proposition 6.23
.

Let
\Gamma,\Xi\triangleleft\Gamma
, with
\Xi\to V
, and
\mathcal{C}_{V}
, and
M
be as above.

Then there exists a computable finite quotient
q:\Gamma\to Q
of
\Gamma
, with kernel in
\Xi
, whose restriction to
\Xi
factorizes through
V
, such that whenever
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
are such that, for all
\mu\in M
,
[\xi_{1}]_{V},[(\xi_{2})^{\mu}]_{V}
are in
\mathcal{C}_{V}
and are different, one has
[q(\xi_{1})]_{Q}\neq[q(\xi_{2})]_{Q}
.

The situation is illustrated in the diagram:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\Gamma&&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longrightarrow}}&&Q&{\scriptstyle\hbox{{\footnotesize question: }}[q(\xi_{1})]_{Q}\neq[q(\xi_{2})]_{Q}?}\\
\triangledown&&&&\vee\\
\Xi&\to&V&\to&Q_{0}&{\scriptstyle\hbox{{\footnotesize by construction: }}[q(\xi_{1})]_{Q_{0}}\neq[q({\xi_{2}}^{\mu})]_{Q_{0}}}\\
{\scriptstyle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}&&{\scriptstyle[\xi_{1}]_{V}\neq[\xi_{2}^{\mu}]_{V}}&\end{array}

Proof.

Recall that
V
is conjugacy separated, in particular, its finite order conjugacy classes are separated in finite quotients
[
Dye79
]
. We may thus find a finite quotient
Q_{0}
of
V
in which all finite finite order conjugacy classes of
V
map on different conjugacy classes. This defines a quotient map
\Xi\to Q_{0}
with kernel of finite index in
\Xi
. Up to reducing the kernel (thus keeping the separation property), we may as well choose it so that the kernel in
\Xi
is characteristic and finite index. It is therefore normal in
\Gamma
, and defines a finite quotient
q:\Gamma\twoheadrightarrow Q
with same kernel (hence in
\Xi
).

Assume that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
in
\Xi
are as in the statement. To prove that the
Q
-conjugacy classes of their images are distinct, we proceed by contradiction, assuming
[q(\xi_{1})]_{Q}=[q(\xi_{2})]_{Q}
.

Reformulating this equality: there is
\mu\in M
and
\zeta\in\Xi
such that
q(\xi_{1})=q(\xi_{2}^{\mu\zeta})
. Lifting this in
\Gamma
, we have that there exists

\zeta^{\prime}\in\ker q
such that
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}^{\mu\zeta}\zeta^{\prime}
. We rewrite this as
\xi_{1}\in(\xi_{2}^{\mu})^{\zeta}\cdot\ker q
.

Recall that
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\zeta
, and
\xi_{2}^{\mu}
are all in
\Xi
. Since
\ker q=\ker(\Xi\to Q_{0})<\Xi
, this relation descends in
Q_{0}
(through
V
) as

[q(\xi_{1})]_{Q_{0}}=[q(\xi_{2}^{\mu})]_{Q_{0}}
. However, this is contrary to our assumption on
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
and the fact that
\Xi\to Q_{0}
separates all conjugacy classes in
\mathcal{C}_{v}
.

∎

6.2.5
Minkowskian congruence

Proposition 6.24
.

If
\Gamma
is the fundamental group of a trivially partially suspended graph of group
\mathbb{X}
, then, for a characteristic clean covering
\mathbb{Y}
of
\mathbb{X}
, with fundamental group
\Xi\triangleleft\Gamma
, a virtually free vertex filling
\Xi\to V
of
\Xi
satisfying Corollary
6.22
, and a characteristic finite quotient
Q
of
\Gamma
satisfying Proposition
6.23
, then for each element
\phi
of
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)}
that is non-trivial, and of finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Gamma\right)}
, the induced automorphism of
Q
by
\phi
is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(Q\right)}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{X},\Gamma,\Xi
and
\phi
be in the statement of the proposition.
\phi
induces an automorphism of
\mathbb{Y}
as well, since it is characteristic.

First, we check that
\phi|_{\Xi}
has finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Xi\right)}
. A power
\phi^{\prime}
of
\phi
is inner in
\Gamma
, hence is
{\mathrm{ad}_{g}}
for some
g\in\Gamma
. Therefore,
(\phi^{\prime})^{m}={\mathrm{ad}_{g^{m}}}
, and for
m=[\Gamma;\Xi]
,
g^{m}\in\Xi
. In particular a power of
\phi|_{\Xi}
is inner in
\Xi
.

We next check that
\phi|_{\Xi}
is non-trivial in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\Xi\right)}
:
if
\phi
itself is inner in
\Xi
, then using uniqueness of roots, one can easily check that the same conjugator makes it inner in
\Gamma
.

Denote by
\phi=(\phi_{Y},(\phi_{v}),(\phi_{e}),\gamma_{e})
the obtained automorphism of
\mathbb{Y}
.
By Proposition
6.13
, there are two cases for
\phi
in
\delta{\mathrm{Aut}\left(\mathbb{Y}\right)}
.

The first case is when
\phi_{Y}
is non-trivial. Then let
v
be a white vertex of
Y
such that
\phi_{Y}(v)=w\neq v
. Then
\mathbb{Y}_{v},\mathbb{Y}_{w}
with their adjacent edges peripheral structures are isomorphic.

If
g_{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{v}^{0}
(from Corollary
6.22
) then it is not conjugate to
\phi_{v}(g_{v})
, which by construction is also in
\mathcal{S}
. By that corollary, both are then sent on finite order non-conjugate elements of the quotient
V
of the corollary. The Proposition
6.23
then ensures that they are sent to non-conjugate elements of the finite quotient
Q
of
\Gamma
, therefore
\phi
induces on
Q
an automorphism that does not preserve a certain conjugacy class. It is therefore non-trivial.

If
\phi_{Y}
is trivial, there must be some
v
, such that
\phi_{v}
is non-trivial and of finite order in
{\mathrm{Out}\left(\mathbb{Y}_{v}\right)}
. By the construction in Corollary
6.22
, there exists an element in
\mathcal{S}_{v}
that is sent on a non-conjugate element. Again, by that Corollary, it is sent on finite order non-conjugate elements of the quotient
V
of the corollary. The Proposition
6.23
again allows us to conclude in the same way.
∎

We just proved Theorem
6.5
by the proposition, and therefore we proved Theorem
6.3
as well.
